Before you start

Read this first.

These prompts are written the way a research supervisor thinks, not the way a chatbot answers. Many are long on purpose: a vague prompt gets you a vague, confident, wrong answer. Before you paste anything, five non-negotiables.

01
The AI does not know your patients.Every prompt has a context block. Fill it honestly. If you say you have 200 cases a year when you have 40, the model will design a study you cannot finish.
02
Never accept a citation you have not opened.Language models fabricate references that look flawless: real journal, real authors, wrong or non-existent paper. Verify every reference on PubMed by PMID or DOI before it enters your work.
03
Never paste identifiable patient data.No names, hospital IDs, MRNs or scans with burned-in identifiers. De-identify before the data touches a chatbot. This is an ethics committee issue, not a preference.
04
The AI drafts. You decide.Use it to speed up structure, phrasing and pre-checking. The scientific judgement, the assumptions and the interpretation stay yours. Your name goes on the paper, not the model's.
05
Declare AI use where required.ICMJE and most journals require disclosure of generative AI use. AI cannot be an author. Check your journal's policy and your university's thesis rules before submission.

How to use each prompt

  • Copy the full prompt. The length is what makes the output specific.
  • Replace every [SQUARE BRACKET] with your real details. Options inside a bracket are separated by "/"; keep the one that applies.
  • Put anything you paste (abstracts, drafts, results) where the prompt shows [PASTE …], above the task.
  • Use a model with web search or document upload switched on wherever the prompt asks for evidence.
  • Run each stage in a fresh chat so the model doesn't carry assumptions from earlier steps.
  • Push back. If the output is generic, reply: "Be more specific. Tie every point to my context."
The framework

Every prompt is built on P-R-O-M-P-T.

One formula, six layers. Each layer matches what OpenAI, Anthropic, Google and Microsoft recommend in their own prompting guides.

P
Persona
Who the AI should be: a senior expert with a trait
R
Request
One clear task, as numbered steps
O
Output
Shape, length, tone, tables
M
Material
Your context and anything you paste
P
Parameters
Rules, guidelines, accuracy guards
T
Tweak
Self-critique and the next step

See it in action

BAD
"Tell me about pneumonia."
GOOD
"Explain community-acquired pneumonia management for an MBBS exam."
BETTER
"Act as a pulmonologist. Explain CAP management for an MBBS final-year student as a 6-point answer with empirical antibiotic choices per Indian guidelines. Keep it under 150 words, exam-oriented."

Three kinds of prompt

Quick

40–80 words

One focused output in a few sentences. Each has a TRY IT WITH example.

Standard

100–200 words

One real task done properly: context, numbered task, rules, output, a pro tip and the next prompt to run.

Master

250–450 words

A full workflow on its own page, with when to use it, why it exists and how to get more out of it.

Tags you will see in the outputs

TagWhat to do
[CITE] · [CITATION NEEDED]Find a real source on PubMed and insert it yourself.
[VERIFY] · [ASSUMPTION — VERIFY FROM PUBLISHED DATA]Replace the number with a sourced figure before use.
[UNVERIFIED BACKGROUND]Treat as a lead to check, not a fact.
[ASK ME] · [NEED INFO] · [NOT REPORTED]The model is missing information. Supply it, or leave it out.

Every prompt works in ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and Copilot. → NEXT points to the prompt to run after this one.

Topic selection & research question

Most thesis disasters are topic failures decided in a ten-minute corridor conversation. Build feasibility into the decision from day one.

1Feasibility-first topic shortlist
Master
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident (Year 1)
Use itMonths 1 to 3 of your postgraduate course, before you commit to anything.
WhyMost theses fail at the topic, not the analysis. This prompt checks every idea against your real patient numbers, equipment and time before you fall in love with it.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a senior clinical research methodologist with over 20 years of experience supervising postgraduate theses in [YOUR SPECIALTY]. You have sat on an institutional ethics committee and you peer review for specialty journals. You are blunt about feasibility rather than encouraging.
My context
Specialty: [e.g. Respiratory Medicine]
Programme and year: [MD / MS / DNB / DM / MCh], year [1 / 2 / 3]
Institution type: [government medical college / tertiary private / district hospital]
Eligible patients I can realistically access: about [N] per month
Months available for recruitment and data collection: [X]
Funding: [none / departmental consumables / grant of amount Y]
Investigations and equipment I genuinely have: [list]
What I do NOT have: [e.g. flow cytometry, PET-CT, research nurse]
Statistical support: [none / department statistician / paid]
Clinical interests: [2 to 4]
Diseases with high burden in my setting: [list]
Task
Generate 8 candidate research questions I could design, complete, defend and publish inside my timeline. For each, give exactly:
1. Working title as it would appear on a thesis cover page.
2. PICOT: Population, Intervention or Exposure, Comparator, Outcome, Timeframe.
3. The gap in two sentences: what is established, what remains unanswered (describe evidence generically).
4. Study design, and one line on why not the obvious alternative.
5. Feasibility score out of 5, justified against my context above.
6. Indicative sample size with every assumption stated.
7. Recruitment timeline in months, assuming 30% of eligible patients are lost.
8. Primary outcome and the exact instrument, scale or cut-off.
9. The three most likely reasons it fails.
10. Honest publication ceiling: local, national indexed, or international.
Rules
Mark every number you need as [ASSUMPTION — VERIFY FROM PUBLISHED DATA] instead of inventing prevalence, effect sizes, trial names or citations, because my guide will ask where each came from. Drop any question needing resources I said I do not have. At least 2 of the 8 must survive if recruitment halves.
Output
A ranked list from most to least feasible, then your top pick with reasoning.
Tweak
Finish with AVOID THESE: three topic directions in my field that look attractive to postgraduates but routinely fail, and why.

How to get more out of it

  • Run it twice, once with your real patient numbers and once with half. Any topic that survives both is the safe one.
  • Take the top three to your guide as a one-page comparison, not a chatbot transcript.
  • Read AVOID THESE before you fall in love with an idea.
  • → NEXT #4 Guide-meeting pitch pack for your top 3 topics
2Five Indian abstracts → six thesis topics
Master
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident
Use itAfter you have saved 5 recent abstracts from Indian centres in your field (Semantic Scholar or PubMed, last 3 years).
WhyTopics generated from memory repeat what already exists. Topics generated from real recent Indian abstracts start from what your peers have actually published, and from what they left out.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a research mentor at an Indian medical college who has guided over 50 postgraduate theses to completion. You read abstracts like a reviewer: you look for what was not measured, not studied, or not studied here.
My context
Specialty and programme: [e.g. MD Pulmonary Medicine, year 1]
Setting: [government medical college / private tertiary / district]
Limits: 2-year thesis window, [limited / moderate] lab infrastructure, about [N] eligible patients per month.
[PASTE ABSTRACT 1]
[PASTE ABSTRACT 2]
[PASTE ABSTRACT 3]
[PASTE ABSTRACT 4]
[PASTE ABSTRACT 5]
Task
1. For each abstract, write one line on what it studied and one line on what it did not address (population, outcome, setting, follow-up or design).
2. From these, identify 3 research gaps relevant to Indian patients.
3. For each gap, propose 2 thesis topics, each with a PICO question and a measurable clinical outcome.
4. For all 6 topics, give: best study design, rough sample size range, primary outcome measure, the main feasibility challenge in an Indian government hospital, and publication potential in an indexed journal.
5. Rank the 6 and recommend the top 3 to present to my guide.
Rules
Work only from the abstracts I pasted. If a gap needs knowledge beyond them, label it [UNVERIFIED BACKGROUND — CONFIRM ON PUBMED] rather than stating it as fact. Name no additional papers unless you can give a verified PMID or DOI.
Output
A short table per gap, then the ranked shortlist with one-paragraph reasoning for each of the top 3.
Tweak
End with the single question I should search on Consensus to check whether my top topic is already saturated.

How to get more out of it

  • If an abstract is from a different population (Western, paediatric), say so in the context line. It changes which gaps are real.
  • Keep the 5 abstracts. They become the first five studies in your Review of Literature.
  • If fewer than 3 real gaps appear, your search was too narrow. Search the gap, not the disease name.
  • → NEXT #8 Is this topic already done to death?
3Socratic research-question workshop
Master
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident / MBBS Intern
Use itWhen you have a vague idea ("something on sepsis in the ICU") but no question.
WhyA good question is refined, not generated. This prompt makes the AI interview you, one question at a time, until the question is sharp enough to defend.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a clinical epidemiologist who runs research-methods workshops for postgraduates. You never hand over answers early. You ask probing questions, one at a time, and you push until the question is specific, measurable and feasible.
My context
My rough idea in one or two sentences: [e.g. something about delirium in elderly ICU patients]
Specialty and level: [e.g. MD Anaesthesiology, year 1]
Setting: [e.g. 20-bed mixed ICU, government medical college]
Time for data collection: [X months]
Task
Run a workshop with me in rounds:
1. Ask me one question at a time about population, exposure or intervention, comparator, outcome, timeframe and setting. Wait for my answer before asking the next.
2. After every 3 answers, show the current version of my research question and score it on FINER (Feasible, Interesting, Novel, Ethical, Relevant), 1 to 5 each, with one line per score.
3. Point out the weakest FINER element and ask the question that fixes it.
4. Stop when every FINER score is 4 or more, or after 5 rounds.
5. Then give the final question in PICOT format, a one-line hypothesis, and a draft working title.
Rules
Do not suggest a finished question before round 3, because the point is to make me think. If an answer I give makes the study unethical or impossible, say so directly and explain why. Keep every question you ask under 30 words.
Output
Conversational, one question per turn. Use a short FINER scorecard table at each checkpoint.
Tweak
At the end, list the 3 assumptions in my question that I must verify in the literature before my guide meeting.

How to get more out of it

  • Answer honestly, including "I don't know". The workshop is only as good as your answers.
  • Screenshot the final scorecard. It becomes slide 2 of your topic presentation.
  • Re-run the workshop for your second-choice idea and compare the final scores.
  • → NEXT #7 FINER stress-test of my research question
4Guide-meeting pitch pack for your top 3 topics
Master
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident
Use itThe day before you meet your guide or research committee to choose a topic.
WhyGuides respond far better to a student who arrives with three shortlisted topics, the evidence gap behind each, and an honest feasibility view. This prompt builds that one-page pitch.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a head of department who has chaired thesis-topic committees for 15 years. You know which questions guides ask, which topics get approved, and which get sent back.
My context
Specialty and year: [e.g. MS General Surgery, year 1]
My guide's known interests: [list, or "unknown"]
Department case load relevant to these topics: [numbers per month]
Topic 1: [title + PICOT + why it matters]
Topic 2: [title + PICOT + why it matters]
Topic 3: [title + PICOT + why it matters]
Evidence I have for each gap (searches, Consensus result, abstracts): [PASTE NOTES]
Task
1. Build a one-page comparison table: title, design, sample size needed vs available, time to complete, cost, ethics complexity, novelty, publication ceiling.
2. Give each topic an overall recommendation: strong, possible, or weak, with one reason.
3. For each topic, write the 3 questions my guide is most likely to ask, with a short answer from my notes.
4. Write a 60-second verbal pitch for my first-choice topic.
5. List what I should bring to the meeting (search strings, hit counts, a feasibility estimate).
Rules
Base every feasibility judgement on the numbers I gave, not on general assumptions. Where my notes do not support a claim of novelty, write [GAP NOT YET VERIFIED] instead of asserting it, because a guide will test it. Keep my voice: a first-year resident, not a professor.
Output
The comparison table first, then the per-topic questions, the pitch script, and a checklist. Fits on two printed pages.
Tweak
Finish with the one weakness in my first-choice topic that I should raise before my guide does, and how to present it.

How to get more out of it

  • Print the table. A one-page comparison beats scrolling a phone in a guide's office.
  • If your guide rejects all three, ask which constraint mattered most, then re-run topic-feasibility with that constraint added.
  • Practise the 60-second pitch out loud twice.
  • → NEXT #17 Thesis title that passes the committee
5India gap finder: 15 topics from scratch
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident (Year 1)
Use itWhen you have no abstracts or ideas yet and need a starting list today.
Persona
You are a senior researcher in [SPECIALTY] at an Indian medical college, known for steering residents towards questions that matter to Indian patients rather than copying Western topics.
My context
I am doing my [MD / MS / DNB] in [SPECIALTY] at a [government medical college / private college] in India. I have limited lab infrastructure and a 2-year thesis window.
Task
Generate 15 potential thesis topics that:
1. Address conditions with a high burden in India (e.g. TB, diabetes, COPD, anaemia, dengue, snakebite, hypertension) relevant to my specialty.
2. Have limited published data from Indian populations.
3. Can be done as observational, cross-sectional or simple interventional studies.
4. Need no advanced laboratory infrastructure.
For each, state the gap it addresses in one line and rate feasibility out of 10.
Rules
Describe gaps generically. Do not name papers or quote prevalence figures from memory; write [VERIFY] where a number would help, because every gap must be checked on PubMed before I pitch it.
Output
A numbered table: topic, gap, design, feasibility /10. Then the 3 you would pick and why.
Pro tipPaste your 3 favourites into Consensus as yes/no questions. Keep only the ones with mixed or insufficient evidence.
Try it withMD General Medicine, government medical college in Bihar.
→ NEXT #9 Turn gaps into Consensus yes/no questions
6Clinical hunch → PICOT question
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident / MBBS Intern
Use itWhen you have noticed something on the ward ("these patients seem to do worse") and want to turn it into a question.
Persona
You are a clinical research mentor who helps residents turn bedside observations into answerable research questions.
My context
What I noticed on the ward or in OPD: [describe the observation in 2–3 lines]
Specialty and setting: [e.g. Paediatrics, tertiary PICU]
Rough patient numbers per month: [N]
Task
1. Restate my observation as a clear clinical question.
2. Convert it into 3 alternative PICOT (or PECO) questions: one descriptive, one comparative, one prognostic.
3. For each, name the study design that answers it and the primary outcome.
4. Say which is most feasible for a postgraduate thesis and why.
Rules
Keep each question to one sentence. If my observation is more likely explained by bias or chance (e.g. referral pattern), say so first, because that changes the question.
Output
A table of the 3 PICOT versions, then a recommendation in 3 sentences.
Pro tipWrite your observation down the day you notice it. Specific details ("three patients this month on drug X") make much better questions than vague impressions.
Try it withElderly patients with hip fracture on our ward seem to develop delirium more often when surgery is delayed beyond 48 hours.
→ NEXT #7 FINER stress-test of my research question
7FINER stress-test of my research question
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident
Use itBefore you submit your topic to the department or research committee.
Persona
You are a research committee member at an Indian medical college who scores postgraduate topics every term. You are fair but you do not pass weak questions.
My context
My research question: [paste in PICOT form]
Study design: [design]
Resources and patient numbers: [brief]
Time available: [months]
Task
1. Score my question on each FINER criterion (Feasible, Interesting, Novel, Ethical, Relevant) from 1 to 5, with two lines of justification each.
2. Identify the single weakest criterion.
3. Rewrite the question twice: once to fix the weakest criterion, once to make it simpler.
4. Say which of the three versions you would approve and why.
Rules
Judge novelty only on what you can reason from the question itself; where novelty depends on the literature, mark it [CHECK ON PUBMED] instead of guessing, because false novelty claims are caught at the viva.
Output
A FINER scorecard table, the two rewrites, and a verdict of 3 sentences or fewer.
Pro tipA score of 3 on "Novel" is fine for a thesis. A score below 3 on "Feasible" is not.
Try it withIs serum vitamin D level associated with exacerbation frequency in COPD patients at a tertiary hospital in north India?
→ NEXT #8 Is this topic already done to death?
8Is this topic already done to death?
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident
Use itAfter shortlisting, when you suspect your topic may already be heavily studied.
Persona
You are a systematic reviewer who can tell within minutes whether a research question is saturated, contested or open.
My context
My proposed question: [PICOT]
Search results I found (titles or abstracts from PubMed, Consensus or Perplexity): [PASTE TITLES / ABSTRACTS HERE]
Task
1. Sort the studies I pasted by design and setting (Indian vs international).
2. Judge the evidence state: saturated (consistent findings, many studies), contested (mixed findings), or open (few or no studies).
3. If saturated, suggest 3 angles that would still be original (new population, setting, outcome, follow-up, or measurement method).
4. If contested, identify what explains the disagreement and whether my study could resolve it.
Rules
Judge only from what I pasted. If the sample is too small to judge, say so and tell me which search to run next, because a verdict based on six titles is not a verdict.
Output
One-line verdict, then a short table of studies, then the angles or next search.
Pro tipConsensus's meter is a quick first pass. This prompt is the second pass on what it returns.
Try it withPaste 10 PubMed titles for "neutrophil-lymphocyte ratio sepsis mortality".
→ NEXT #23 Five original angles on a crowded topic
9Turn gaps into Consensus yes/no questions
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident
Use itBetween gap-finding (Perplexity) and topic selection, before you check evidence density.
Persona
You are a medical librarian who trains residents to query evidence engines such as Consensus effectively.
My context
Research gaps or problem areas I have noted: [PASTE 5–8 GAP AREAS HERE]
Population of interest: [e.g. Indian adults with type 2 diabetes]
Task
1. Convert each gap into one binary research question in the form "Does [factor] affect [outcome] in [population]?"
2. Give a second, narrower version of each question.
3. Tell me how to read the result: what a strong-agreement, mixed or insufficient-evidence result would mean for choosing my thesis.
Rules
Keep each question under 20 words and answerable with yes or no. Use my population exactly, because broad populations return Western data that does not apply to my setting.
Output
A numbered list: broad question, then narrow question, for each gap. Then a 3-row interpretation guide.
Pro tipKeep only the questions whose result is mixed or insufficient. Strong agreement means that topic is done.
Try it withGaps: sarcopenia in COPD, vitamin D in TB outcomes, early mobilisation in ICU.
→ NEXT #8 Is this topic already done to death?
10Topics I can do from existing hospital records
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident / DNB Trainee
Use itWhen prospective recruitment looks slow, or your thesis window is short.
Persona
You are a clinical researcher who has published multiple retrospective and record-based studies from Indian hospitals, and you know their pitfalls.
My context
Specialty: [SPECIALTY]
Records available: [e.g. ICU admission registers 2019–2024, discharge summaries, lab information system, OPD registers]
How complete and reliable these records are, in my honest view: [brief]
Approximate number of records: [N]
Task
1. Suggest 8 thesis questions answerable from these records.
2. For each, list the variables needed and whether they are routinely recorded.
3. Flag the main bias for each (missing data, misclassification, selection).
4. Say which questions most need an ethics waiver of consent, and what the IEC will want.
Rules
Prefer outcomes that are hard to misrecord (death, readmission, length of stay) over soft outcomes. Mark any question that needs data my records probably lack as [CHECK DATA AVAILABILITY] rather than assuming it is there.
Output
A table: question, variables, bias risk (low / medium / high), IEC note.
Pro tipPull 20 random files before committing. If more than a quarter are missing your key variable, choose another question.
Try it withMedicine ward records of acute kidney injury, 2020–2024, about 900 admissions.
→ NEXT #18 What could kill this topic? A risk register
11Topics that fit a 12-month data window
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · DNB Trainee / PG Resident (late start)
Use itWhen your approved data-collection window is 12 months or less.
Persona
You are a thesis coordinator who has rescued many late-starting postgraduate theses by right-sizing the question.
My context
Specialty: [SPECIALTY]
Months left for data collection: [X]
Eligible patients per month: [N]
Current (too ambitious) topic idea, if any: [describe]
Task
1. Tell me honestly whether my current idea fits the window, with the arithmetic.
2. Suggest 5 alternative or scaled-down questions with outcomes measurable within days or weeks, not months.
3. For each, estimate the patients needed and the months to recruit at my numbers.
Rules
Prefer cross-sectional, diagnostic-accuracy or short-follow-up designs. Show every calculation with its assumptions marked [ASSUMPTION], because I will have to defend the timeline to my guide.
Output
A verdict paragraph, then a table: question, design, patients needed, months to recruit.
Pro tipBuild in a 2-month buffer. Theses almost never finish recruitment on schedule.
Try it withDNB Orthopaedics, 10 months left, about 15 eligible patients per month.
→ NEXT #22 Four-week feasibility check before you commit
12Topics needing only routine investigations
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident
Use itWhen there is no funding and you can only use tests the hospital already does free or routinely.
Persona
You are a researcher who has built a publication record entirely on routine clinical data in low-resource Indian hospitals.
My context
Specialty: [SPECIALTY]
Routine tests available at no cost to patients: [e.g. CBC, LFT, RFT, ABG, chest X-ray, ECG, bedside USG]
Tests I cannot afford: [list]
Patient groups I see often: [list]
Task
1. Suggest 10 thesis topics that use only the routine tests I listed.
2. For each, give the research question, the key routine variable, the outcome, and why the question still matters clinically.
3. Mark the 3 with the best publication potential.
Rules
Exclude any topic needing a test from my "cannot afford" list, and do not suggest workarounds that shift cost to patients, because the IEC will reject that.
Output
A numbered table with a one-line clinical-value note for each, then the top 3.
Pro tipRatios and indices built from routine tests are popular but crowded. Pair them with a hard outcome to stand out.
Try it withMD Medicine; routine CBC, RFT, LFT, ABG, ECG, chest X-ray; lots of sepsis, CKD and snakebite.
→ NEXT #7 FINER stress-test of my research question
13Validate an existing clinical score in my population
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident
Use itWhen you want a solid, publishable design with a clear outcome, and a well-known score exists in your field.
Persona
You are a clinical epidemiologist experienced in external validation of prognostic and diagnostic scores in Indian populations.
My context
Candidate score or rule: [e.g. qSOFA, CURB-65, Alvarado, Wells]
Population where it was developed (if known): [describe or "unsure"]
My population and setting: [describe]
Outcome the score predicts: [e.g. 28-day mortality]
Task
1. Explain why validating this score in my population is (or is not) a genuine gap.
2. Outline the validation design: inclusion criteria, timing of scoring, outcome ascertainment, blinding of assessors.
3. Name the performance measures to report (discrimination, calibration) and what each means clinically.
4. Give the sample-size logic for validation studies (events needed, not just total patients).
Rules
Do not claim the score has never been validated in India; write [CHECK EXISTING INDIAN VALIDATIONS ON PUBMED], because that search decides whether this topic is novel.
Output
Four short headed sections, under 400 words in total.
Pro tipValidation studies are strongest when the score is calculated before the outcome is known. Build that into your protocol.
Try it withCURB-65 for 30-day mortality in community-acquired pneumonia at a district hospital.
→ NEXT #19 Choose a measurable primary outcome
14Quality-improvement and audit thesis topics
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen your department wants a thesis that also improves care, or clinical trials are impractical.
Persona
You are a quality-improvement lead trained in PDSA cycles and clinical audit, who has supervised QI projects in Indian hospitals.
My context
Specialty and unit: [e.g. Obstetrics labour room]
Care processes that often go wrong or vary: [list 3–5]
Existing standards we could measure against: [e.g. WHO, national guideline, hospital protocol, or "unsure"]
Task
1. Suggest 6 audit or QI topics, each with the standard, the measurable indicator and the target.
2. For the best one, outline a before-and-after design with one PDSA cycle.
3. Explain how to report it (SQUIRE 2.0 headings) and whether it counts as research needing full IEC review.
Rules
Name a standard only if it is a real, named guideline; otherwise write [STANDARD TO BE CONFIRMED], because an audit without a verifiable standard is not an audit.
Output
A table of 6 topics, then the outline for the best one, under 350 words.
Pro tipAsk your IEC early whether QI counts as research at your institution. The answer changes consent requirements.
Try it withTime from admission to first antibiotic in suspected sepsis in the medical emergency.
→ NEXT #7 FINER stress-test of my research question
15Questionnaire-based topics without the KAP trap
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident / MBBS Intern

16Head-to-head decision matrix for 2–3 topics
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident
Use itWhen you are stuck choosing between two or three final topics.
Persona
You are a pragmatic research supervisor who helps residents decide with criteria, not gut feel.
My context
Topic A: [title + design + patient numbers]
Topic B: [title + design + patient numbers]
Topic C (optional): [title + design + patient numbers]
What matters most to me (rank): [e.g. finishing on time, publication, learning a skill, guide's interest]
Task
1. Build a decision matrix with my priorities as weighted criteria.
2. Score each topic 1 to 5 per criterion, with one line of reasoning.
3. Calculate weighted totals and give a clear recommendation.
4. State what new information would change the recommendation.
Rules
Use only the facts I gave. Where a score needs information I did not supply, mark it [NEED INFO] rather than guessing, because a guessed matrix decides nothing.
Output
The matrix table, then a 3-sentence recommendation.
Pro tipIf two topics tie, pick the one with the more reliable primary outcome measurement.
Try it withA: CPAP adherence in OSA (cohort, 8/month). B: Lung ultrasound vs X-ray for pneumonia (diagnostic, 25/month).

17Thesis title that passes the committee
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident
Use itWhen your topic is fixed and you need the formal thesis title.
Persona
You are a university thesis committee member who has seen hundreds of titles rejected for being vague, too long or overclaiming.
My context
Research question (PICOT): [paste]
Study design: [design]
Setting: [hospital, city]
My university's title rules, if any: [e.g. word limit, must mention design]
Task
1. Write 6 title options: descriptive, design-first, PICO-style, question-form, short, and conservative.
2. Check each against the common committee objections (vague population, missing design, causal words, excessive length).
3. Recommend one and explain why.
Rules
Use associational wording (association, correlation, comparison) unless my design is randomised, because causal wording in an observational title is the first thing examiners question.
Output
A numbered list of 6 titles with word counts, a one-line check result for each, and the recommendation.
Pro tipMost universities like the design and setting in the title: "A prospective observational study at a tertiary care centre in north India".

18What could kill this topic? A risk register
Standard
1.1 Topic selection & research question · PG Resident

3. Give one prevention step and one fallback plan for each.

19Choose a measurable primary outcome
Standard

My context

21Topics from the gaps in a clinical guideline

Section 1.2
1.2

Literature search strategy

A literature review is only as good as the search behind it. A reproducible search string is something examiners and journals now ask to see.

36Build a reproducible, multi-database search
Master
1.2 Literature search strategy · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itAfter your topic is provisionally approved, before you read a single paper in depth.
Why"I searched PubMed" is not a method. A reproducible search, with concept blocks, controlled vocabulary and exact strings, is what separates a review from a reading list, and it is what examiners and journals ask to see.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a medical librarian and systematic-review methodologist who has co-authored Cochrane reviews and trained postgraduates in reproducible searching. You are precise about search syntax and sceptical of shortcuts.
My context
My full PICOT question: [PASTE QUESTION]
Study design I intend to use: [e.g. prospective observational cohort]
Databases I can access: [PubMed / Embase / Scopus / Cochrane Library / Web of Science / Google Scholar]
Language limits: [English only / English plus others]
Date range: [e.g. 2010 to present, and tell me if I should widen it]
Reference manager: [Zotero / Mendeley / EndNote / none]
Task
1. Break my question into concept blocks. For each block, list controlled vocabulary (MeSH for PubMed, Emtree for Embase) and free-text synonyms, including spelling variants, abbreviations and older terms.
2. Write a complete, copy-paste-ready PubMed string with correct field tags, Boolean operators, truncation and explosion.
3. Translate it into Embase and Cochrane Library syntax.
4. Give one deliberately broad and one deliberately narrow version, and say what each will miss.
5. List the steps beyond databases: citation chasing, reference lists, grey literature, trial registries (CTRI, ClinicalTrials.gov) and preprint servers relevant to my field.
6. Give me a search log template: database, date searched, exact string, filters, hits.
Rules
Build strings only from real MeSH and Emtree headings; if you are not certain a heading exists, mark it [CHECK IN MeSH BROWSER], because one invalid heading silently breaks the whole search. Do not report any studies or hit counts from memory.
Output
Concept-block table, then each string in its own code block, then the broad and narrow versions, the extra steps as a checklist, and the log template.
Tweak
End by telling me the one concept block most likely to be missing synonyms, and how to test it.

How to get more out of it

  • Run the PubMed string and check the hit count. Under 50 is usually too narrow; over 3,000 is usually too broad.
  • Save the exact string and the date you ran it. Examiners and journals ask for both.
  • Test the string: pick 3 papers you already know are relevant. If any is missing from the results, add its keywords.
  • → NEXT #42 Too many or too few hits: fix my search
37Search documentation that survives the viva
Master
1.2 Literature search strategy · PG Resident
Use itAfter you have run your searches, while every string, date and count is still in front of you.
WhyExaminers and journal reviewers increasingly ask "how exactly did you search?". A documented search (strings, dates, hits, deduplication) turns your ROL from a narrative into a method, and saves you from redoing it months later.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a systematic-review methodologist who reviews search reporting for journals and applies the PRISMA 2020 and PRISMA-S reporting standards.
My context
Databases searched, with date of each search: [list]
Exact strings used: [PASTE STRINGS HERE]
Hits per database: [numbers]
Duplicates removed (and how): [number, tool]
Records screened, full texts assessed, studies included: [numbers]
Reasons for full-text exclusion with counts: [list]
Other sources (registries, citation chasing, experts): [list with counts]
Task
1. Write the search-methods paragraph for my thesis methodology chapter.
2. Produce a PRISMA 2020 flow diagram description with my numbers slotted in, box by box.
3. Check my numbers for internal consistency (screened minus excluded must equal full texts assessed, and so on) and flag any mismatch.
4. List the PRISMA-S items my documentation covers and the ones it is missing.
5. Draft a one-page search appendix table: database, platform, date, string, limits, hits.
Rules
Use only the numbers I gave. Where a number is missing, write [MISSING — FILL IN] rather than estimating, because an inconsistent flow diagram is the first thing a reviewer checks. Keep my exact strings unchanged.
Output
The methods paragraph (about 150 words), the flow-diagram box list, a consistency check table, the PRISMA-S gap list, and the appendix table.
Tweak
Finish with the two questions an examiner is most likely to ask about my search, and model answers based only on my documentation. Then tell me which single piece of documentation I should create today because it cannot be reconstructed later (for example, the original hit counts or the date of the first search).

How to get more out of it

  • Draw the final flow diagram in PowerPoint or draw.io from the box list. Don't ask the AI to draw it.
  • Keep screenshots of each database's results page with the date visible.
  • Re-run the search just before submission and report the update date.
  • → NEXT #53 Write the search-methods paragraph
38Search rescue: iterate until the results are right
Master
1.2 Literature search strategy · PG Resident
Use itWhen your search returns far too many, far too few, or irrelevant results, and you have already tried tweaking it.
WhySearching is iterative. Most residents give up after one bad result, or keep reading 4,000 irrelevant titles. This prompt makes the AI diagnose what went wrong and fix it one change at a time.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are an expert search consultant at a medical library who debugs failed searches daily. You change one thing at a time and explain the effect of each change.
My context
My research question: [PICOT]
Database and current search string: [PASTE STRING HERE]
Hit count: [N]
What is wrong: [too many / too few / irrelevant results]
Five titles from the results that are irrelevant (if any): [PASTE TITLES]
Two or three papers I know should appear but do not: [titles or PMIDs]
Task
1. Diagnose the problem: which concept block, operator, field tag or truncation is causing it.
2. Propose a revised string with one change, and explain what the change should do to the hit count.
3. Ask me to run it and report back the new count and 5 sample titles.
4. Repeat for up to 4 rounds until the known papers appear and the irrelevant titles drop out.
5. Then give the final string with a one-line rationale for each block.
Rules
Change one thing per round, because changing several at once makes it impossible to tell what worked. Do not invent MeSH terms; mark uncertain ones [CHECK IN MeSH BROWSER]. Wait for my results before the next round.
Output
One round per turn: a short diagnosis, the revised string in a code block, the expected effect on the hit count and the known papers, and exactly what I should report back to you. Keep each round under 200 words so I can act on it quickly.
Tweak
At the end, summarise the search in 3 sentences suitable for my methods chapter.

How to get more out of it

  • Known papers are the best test of a search. Keep 3 "must-find" papers handy.
  • Irrelevant titles tell you which synonym is too loose. Paste them rather than describing them.
  • If the tweaks never converge, your question is probably too broad. Go back to section 1.1.
  • → NEXT #37 Search documentation that survives the viva
39MeSH terms and synonyms for one concept
Standard
1.2 Literature search strategy · PG Resident / MBBS Student
Use itWhen you are building a search and stuck on one concept.
Persona
You are a medical librarian who knows the MeSH hierarchy well and teaches residents to search systematically.
My context
The concept I need to search: [e.g. acute kidney injury]
My wider question for context: [one line]
Task
1. Give the most relevant MeSH heading(s), where each sits in the hierarchy, and whether to explode it.
2. List entry terms and free-text synonyms, including abbreviations, spelling variants (British / American) and outdated terms used in older papers.
3. Combine them into one PubMed block using [MeSH] and [tiab] tags with OR.
Rules
Only give MeSH headings you are confident exist; mark others [CHECK IN MeSH BROWSER], because an invalid heading returns nothing without warning.
Output
A short table (term, type, tag), then the combined block in a code block.
Pro tipCheck the MeSH introduction year. Papers older than that are indexed under a different term.
Try it withConcept: ventilator-associated pneumonia.

40PICO → concept blocks table
Standard
1.2 Literature search strategy · PG Resident
Use itThe first step of any search, straight after your question is fixed.
Persona
You are a search-methods tutor who teaches residents to turn clinical questions into structured searches.
My context
My PICOT question: [PASTE QUESTION]
Database I will start with: [PubMed / Embase / Google Scholar]
Task
1. Split the question into concept blocks and say which PICO elements to search and which to leave out (usually comparator and outcome), with the reason.
2. For each block, give controlled vocabulary and free-text terms.
3. Show how the blocks combine: OR within blocks, AND between blocks.
4. Suggest a validated study-design filter if one fits my question.
Rules
Explain every exclusion decision in one line, because searching every PICO element is the most common reason searches miss relevant papers.
Output
A concept-block table, then the combined string, then a one-paragraph explanation.
Pro tipMost good searches use 2 or 3 blocks, not 5.
Try it withIn adults with community-acquired pneumonia, does procalcitonin-guided therapy reduce antibiotic duration compared with standard care?

41Fix the syntax of my search string
Standard
1.2 Literature search strategy · PG Resident
Use itWhen PubMed gives an error, a strange translation, or zero results.
Persona
You are a meticulous search-syntax checker who spots misplaced brackets and wrong field tags at a glance.
My context
My search string: [PASTE STRING HERE]
Database: [PubMed / Embase / Scopus / Google Scholar]
What PubMed shows under "Details" or "Search translation": [PASTE IF AVAILABLE]
Task
1. Check brackets, Boolean operators (upper case), field tags, quotation marks, truncation and proximity operators.
2. List every error with its correction.
3. Give the corrected string.
4. Explain how PubMed's automatic term mapping will treat any untagged terms.
Rules
Keep my intended logic unless it is clearly wrong; if you change the logic, say so explicitly, because a silent change alters which papers I find.
Output
An error table (error, why it matters, fix), then the corrected string in a code block.
Pro tipAlways read PubMed's search translation. It shows what PubMed actually searched, not what you typed.
Try it with(asthma OR wheez*) AND inhaler adherance AND (child* OR paediatric

42Too many or too few hits: fix my search
Standard
1.2 Literature search strategy · PG Resident
Use itWhen your search returns thousands of results, or almost none.
Persona
You are a search consultant who balances sensitivity and precision for postgraduate literature reviews.
My context
Research question: [PICOT]
Current string: [PASTE STRING HERE]
Database and hit count: [PubMed, N]
Is this for a narrative ROL or a systematic review?: [narrative / systematic]
Task
1. Diagnose why the count is too high or too low.
2. Suggest 3 targeted changes (e.g. major-topic MeSH, title-only terms, removing a block, adding synonyms, a design filter), ranked by likely effect.
3. Give the revised string for the best option.
4. Say what relevant papers each change risks losing.
Rules
For a systematic review, prefer sensitivity and never restrict by abstract availability or free full text, because those filters introduce bias.
Output
Diagnosis in 2–3 lines, a ranked table of changes, then the revised string.
Pro tipFor a thesis ROL, 150 to 800 well-targeted hits is a workable range.
Try it with"sepsis AND lactate" returning 9,000 PubMed results.

44Use PubMed filters without biasing the review
Standard
1.2 Literature search strategy · PG Resident
Use itBefore applying filters such as date, language, age group, species or article type.
Persona
You are a systematic-review methodologist who warns residents about filters that silently exclude relevant evidence.
My context
My question: [PICOT]
Filters I plan to use: [e.g. last 10 years, English, humans, adults, RCT only]
Purpose of my search: [thesis ROL / systematic review / quick clinical question]
Task
1. For each filter, explain what it actually does in PubMed, including how it treats recently added, not-yet-indexed records.
2. Say whether each is appropriate for my purpose, and the bias it could introduce.
3. Suggest safer alternatives (e.g. search-term blocks instead of the "Humans" filter).
4. Give my final string with the recommended filters applied.
Rules
Be explicit about which filters depend on MeSH indexing, because the newest papers are not yet indexed and would be excluded.
Output
A filter table (filter, what it does, keep or drop, reason), then the final string.
Pro tipJustify every filter in your methods section. Unexplained filters look like cherry-picking.
Try it withFilters: humans, English, last 5 years, free full text.

Section 1.3
1.3

Writing the Introduction

An introduction fails in two ways: it reads like a textbook chapter, or it inflates the gap with statistics nobody can verify. Keep it tight, sourced and honest.

66The four-movement thesis introduction
Master
1.3 Writing the Introduction · PG Resident
Use itAfter your topic and protocol are approved, when you begin writing the thesis itself.
WhyExaminers read the introduction to see whether you understand why your question matters. A four-movement structure (global burden, Indian burden, what is known and unknown, the gap and objective) does that in about 500 words, without a single invented statistic.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a senior academic and thesis examiner in [YOUR SPECIALTY] who has supervised and marked postgraduate dissertations for over 20 years. You are strict about two things: introductions that read like textbook chapters, and burden figures quoted without a source. You write in formal scientific register.
My context
Specialty: [e.g. Respiratory Medicine]
Exact thesis title: [paste]
Study design: [cross-sectional / case-control / cohort / diagnostic accuracy / interventional]
Setting: [hospital, unit, catchment; tertiary / district / private]
Population: [who exactly]
Primary objective: [one line]
The gap in one sentence, in my own words: [what is not yet answered]
Sources I can cite for burden data: [ICMR / NFHS / NCDIR / WHO / Global Burden of Disease / national programme reports / none yet]
Task
Write a structured introduction of 450–550 words in four movements, each flowing into the next, without sub-headings:
1. Global burden: the scale and clinical importance of the condition.
2. Indian or regional burden: narrow to India or South Asia, point to where national data exists, and state plainly where good Indian data is missing.
3. What is known and what is not: summarise what the literature broadly shows, then state precisely what remains unanswered.
4. The gap and the objective: close on an explicit statement of the gap this study addresses and one clean sentence stating the objective.
Rules
Leave every epidemiological figure as a slot: [CITE] for the source and [VERIFY — INSERT SOURCED FIGURE] for any number I have not supplied, because invented prevalence figures are the most common reason introductions are sent back. Describe the evidence generically unless I supply papers. Frame the question; do not write a literature review. Use associational language unless my design supports causation.
Output
The 4-paragraph draft within the word limit, then a numbered checklist of every sentence containing a factual claim I must source before submission.
Tweak
Finish with the one paragraph an examiner is most likely to call "textbook", and a tighter rewrite of it.

How to get more out of it

  • Write your own rough three-movement draft first, then use intro-refine-draft to polish the language only.
  • Take the sourcing checklist to PubMed and your national data portals before you start the ROL.
  • Rewrite the final draft in your own voice. A sudden jump to flawless academic English is a red flag to a guide who knows how you write.
  • → NEXT #75 Every claim that needs a citation
67Refine my own introduction draft (language only)
Master
1.3 Writing the Introduction · PG Resident
Use itAfter you have written a rough introduction yourself. This is the golden rule: draft first, AI refines.
WhyYour guide and examiners want your thinking, not the model's. Refining your own draft keeps the ideas, the sources and the voice yours, while fixing structure, flow and academic register, and it keeps you on the right side of your university's AI policy.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are an academic writing editor for medical theses. You improve clarity, structure and register while strictly preserving the author's content, claims and citations. You never add facts.
My context
My thesis title: [paste]
Programme and university: [e.g. MD Pathology, state health university]
Target length: [e.g. 400–500 words]
Citation style: [Vancouver / other]
My rough draft, with citations as I have them: [PASTE DRAFT HERE]
Task
1. Check the structure against the four movements (background, Indian burden, what is known and unknown, the gap and objective), and tell me which movement is weak or missing.
2. Refine the language paragraph by paragraph: formal academic register, clear topic sentences, smooth transitions, consistent tense.
3. Keep every citation attached to the claim it supports.
4. Mark each place where a claim has no citation with [CITE].
5. Show a list of the substantive changes you made, so I can accept or reject each one individually.
Rules
Do not add any new fact, statistic, study or reference; improve language and structure only, because anything new would be unsourced and not mine. Keep my meaning exactly, and always flag rather than fix any sentence whose meaning is unclear by writing [UNCLEAR — PLEASE CLARIFY]. Stay within the target length.
Output
The refined introduction with its word count, then a change log table (original phrase, revised phrase, reason), then the list of [CITE] gaps in the order they appear.
Tweak
End with 3 sentences that still sound unlike a first-year resident, so I can put them back in my own words.

How to get more out of it

  • Refine one paragraph at a time if your draft is long. You'll keep more control.
  • Accept changes one by one from the change log. Do not paste the whole output blindly.
  • Read the final version aloud. If a sentence is not something you would say in a viva, rewrite it.
  • → NEXT #75 Every claim that needs a citation
68Examiner-style review of my introduction
Master
1.3 Writing the Introduction · PG Resident
Use itWhen your introduction is drafted and you want honest feedback before your guide sees it.
WhyYour guide may only have ten minutes for your draft. An examiner-style critique with a scoring rubric shows you exactly what to fix first, so the guide's time goes on judgement rather than basic structure.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are an external examiner for postgraduate medical theses at an Indian health sciences university. You mark introductions against a clear rubric and write direct, specific comments.
My context
Thesis title and design: [paste]
Primary objective: [one line]
My university's marking emphasis, if known: [e.g. Indian data, references, word limit]
What my guide has already commented on: [brief, or "not yet reviewed"]
My introduction: [PASTE INTRODUCTION HERE]
Task
1. Score the introduction out of 10 on each of: relevance of the opening, burden data and sourcing, Indian context, synthesis of what is known, clarity of the gap, link from gap to objective, flow, and academic register.
2. For each score, quote the sentence or passage that drove it.
3. List the 5 highest-priority fixes, ordered by impact on the examiner's impression.
4. Rewrite the weakest paragraph as an example of the fix.
5. Check that the objective at the end matches my stated primary objective exactly, and flag any mismatch.
Rules
Base every comment on the text I pasted; quote rather than paraphrase, because vague feedback cannot be acted on. Do not add facts or references in the example rewrite; use [CITE] and [VERIFY] slots instead.
Output
A rubric table (criterion, score, quoted evidence, comment) with a total out of 80, then the priority fixes as a numbered list, then the example rewrite with the changed phrases marked in bold so I can see exactly what moved.
Tweak
Close with a one-line overall verdict (ready for guide, needs one revision, or needs restructuring) and the single change that would raise the total score most.

How to get more out of it

  • Fix the top 2 priorities, then run this prompt again. Scores should rise.
  • Keep each version. Showing your guide the before and after proves the work is yours.
  • If "link from gap to objective" scores low, fix the objective in section 1.5 first.
  • → NEXT #67 Refine my own introduction draft (language only)
69Where to find sourced burden data
Standard
1.3 Writing the Introduction · PG Resident
Use itBefore writing the burden paragraphs, so every number has a real source.
Persona
You are a public-health researcher who knows India's major epidemiological data sources and their limits.
My context
Condition: [e.g. chronic kidney disease]
Population of interest: [e.g. Indian adults]
Figures I want (prevalence, incidence, mortality, DALYs, trends): [list]
Task
1. List the most credible sources for each figure: national surveys, ICMR reports, national programme reports, disease registries, Global Burden of Disease, WHO and large Indian cohort studies.
2. For each source, say what it measures, the population it covers, its most recent edition as far as you know, and its main limitation.
3. Explain how to cite a report or dataset correctly in Vancouver style.
Rules
Do not quote any figures. Mark every edition or year as [CHECK LATEST EDITION], because survey rounds and reports are updated often and outdated numbers are easily spotted.
Output
A table (figure needed, best source, what it covers, limitation), then the citation guidance.
Pro tipIf no Indian data exists for your figure, that absence belongs in your introduction as part of the gap.
Try it withCondition: gestational diabetes; figures needed: prevalence and regional variation in India.

73Write a sharp gap statement
Standard
1.3 Writing the Introduction · PG Resident

My objective: [one line]
Task
1. Write the gap statement in 3 sentences: what is known, what is missing, why it matters clinically in my setting.
2. Write the bridging sentence that leads into my objective.
3. Give a weaker and a stronger version, so I can see the difference.
4. Check whether my objective actually addresses the gap as stated.
Rules

Output

Pro tipYour gap statement, objective and conclusion should read like three parts of one argument.

Section 1.4
1.4

Review of Literature

A literature review is not a summary of ten papers you happened to find. It is a reproducible search, an evidence table and a defensible gap statement.

96Screen, extract and synthesise: the ROL engine
Master
1.4 Review of Literature · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itAfter your search is complete (section 1.2), when you have a pile of records to turn into a review.
WhyMost thesis ROLs are a string of study summaries. Examiners want to see explicit criteria, a structured evidence table, a quality assessment and a synthesis that ends on the gap. This prompt builds all four from your own papers.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a systematic-review methodologist who has co-authored Cochrane reviews and supervised dozens of postgraduate literature reviews. You are sceptical of weak evidence and insist on explicit, reproducible decisions.
My context
My PICOT question: [PASTE QUESTION]
Study designs I expect to find: [e.g. cohorts, case-control, a few RCTs]
Number of records after deduplication: [N]
Papers I have uploaded to this chat: [list, or "none yet"]
Task
Part A: screen and extract
1. Draft explicit inclusion and exclusion criteria, phrased so a second reviewer could apply them without asking me questions.
2. Design a data-extraction table: author, year, country, setting, design, sample size, population, exposure or intervention, comparator, outcome definition, effect estimate with 95% CI, follow-up, funding, key limitation.
3. Name the right risk-of-bias tool for each design I will encounter, and list its domains.
Part B: synthesise
4. Give a skeleton for the review narrative: how to open, how to group studies by theme rather than chronology, how to handle conflicting findings, how to close on the gap.
5. Show how to write a three-sentence gap statement: what is known, what is missing, why it matters in my setting.
6. Draft a PRISMA-style flow description with placeholder numbers.
Rules
Do not give any specific citation, author, journal, year, sample size or result unless I uploaded that paper or you retrieved it through live search with a PMID or DOI, because an invented study in a thesis is academic misconduct. Where you describe what the literature broadly shows, label it [UNVERIFIED BACKGROUND — CONFIRM INDEPENDENTLY]. Never estimate a statistic from memory.
Output
Criteria as two numbered lists, the extraction table as a column list, a tool table, the narrative skeleton, the gap-statement template, and the flow description.
Tweak
End with the 3 decisions in this plan an examiner is most likely to question, and how to justify each.

How to get more out of it

  • Upload the actual PDFs before asking for any comparison across papers. That is the difference between synthesis and fabrication.
  • Pilot the extraction table on 3 papers before extracting all of them. Columns always need adjusting.
  • Keep a list of excluded full texts with reasons. It feeds your PRISMA flow diagram.
  • → NEXT #102 Triage a batch of abstracts
97Thematic ROL draft from my evidence table
Master
1.4 Review of Literature · PG Resident
Use itWhen your extraction table is complete and you are ready to write the review chapter.
WhyWriting from an evidence table, rather than from memory or AI knowledge, guarantees every sentence traces back to a real paper you read. Grouping by theme shows examiners you synthesised the literature rather than listed it.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a senior academic who writes and examines literature reviews for medical theses. You synthesise across studies, compare findings, and always end each theme with an interpretive sentence.
My context
Thesis title and objective: [paste]
Citation style: [Vancouver / Harvard]
Target length of the ROL chapter: [e.g. 2,500–3,500 words]
My completed extraction table (author, year, design, n, setting, key finding with numbers, limitation, reference number): [PASTE TABLE HERE]
Themes I think exist (optional): [list]
My guide's expectations for the chapter, if known: [e.g. separate Indian studies section, 25+ references]
Task
1. Propose 4–6 themes that organise the studies, and assign each study to a theme.
2. For each theme, write 2–4 paragraphs that compare studies: agreement, disagreement and the likely reasons (population, setting, definitions, design, sample size).
3. Include a short subsection on Indian studies, or say explicitly that Indian data is lacking in a theme.
4. End each theme with a sentence saying what it means for my study.
5. Close the chapter with a gap paragraph that leads to my objective.
6. After each theme, list the reference numbers used, so I can check coverage against my table.
Rules
Use only the studies and numbers in my table, reproduced exactly with their reference numbers, because every figure will be checked against the source. If a theme has a single study, say so rather than generalising. Add no new studies; where support is missing, write [CITATION NEEDED — FIND SOURCE].
Output
A theme map table first, then the chapter with theme headings, within the target length.
Tweak
Finish with the 3 places where my evidence is thinnest, so I can search again before submission.

How to get more out of it

  • Write one theme per session. Long outputs lose accuracy.
  • Spot-check 5 numbers in the draft against your PDFs before you trust the rest.
  • Put your own interpretation into the closing sentence of each theme. That is where your voice matters most.
  • → NEXT #116 Check the numbers in my ROL against the sources
98Evidence table from uploaded PDFs, with quotes
Master
1.4 Review of Literature · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itIn NotebookLM, Claude or ChatGPT with file upload, once you have 5–15 full-text PDFs.
WhyAsking an AI to "summarise the literature" invites invented studies. Asking it to extract from PDFs you uploaded, and to quote the sentence each data point came from, lets you verify every cell in minutes.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a meticulous research assistant trained in data extraction for systematic reviews. You extract only what is written, you quote your source, and you say "not reported" when information is absent.
My context
My research question: [PICOT]
Number of PDFs uploaded: [N]
Variables I need from each paper: [e.g. design, country, sample size, population, exposure definition, outcome definition, main effect estimate with 95% CI, follow-up, limitations]
Tool I am using: [NotebookLM / Claude / ChatGPT / Gemini]
How I will use the table: [thesis ROL / systematic review / journal club]
Task
1. For each uploaded paper, extract every variable I listed into a table, one row per paper.
2. For each cell, give the supporting quotation from the paper with its section (Methods, Results, Table 2 and so on).
3. Write "NOT REPORTED" where the paper does not state the information.
4. After the table, list any inconsistency within a paper (e.g. abstract and results numbers differ).
5. Summarise in 5 bullets what the set of papers shows collectively, citing papers by first author and year.
6. Flag any paper whose design or population makes it a poor fit for my question.
Rules
Extract only from the uploaded documents; do not supplement from your general knowledge, because I will cite these values in my thesis. Reproduce numbers exactly, including decimals and units. If a PDF is unreadable or partial, say which one.
Output
The extraction table, then a quotation appendix grouped by paper, then the inconsistency list and the 5-bullet summary.
Tweak
End by telling me which paper's data you are least confident about, and why.

How to get more out of it

  • In NotebookLM, click the citation numbers to jump to the exact passage. Check 3 per paper.
  • Upload papers in batches of 5 to 8. Very large batches reduce extraction accuracy.
  • Save the quotation appendix. It is your proof if an examiner asks where a number came from.

99Global and Indian studies: the classic thesis ROL
Master
1.4 Review of Literature · PG Resident
Use itWhen your university expects the traditional Indian thesis ROL format, with global studies, Indian studies and gaps as sub-headings.
WhyMany Indian universities and guides expect studies described one by one (author, year, sample, finding, limitation) under global and Indian headings. This prompt keeps that format and makes it analytical instead of a list, using only papers you supply.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a senior Indian medical academic who has guided and examined theses for 20 years and knows the conventional ROL format. You insist that each study description ends with its relevance to the present study.
My context
Thesis title: [paste]
Primary objective: [one line]
Global studies, with the extracted details for each: [PASTE STUDY DETAILS HERE]
Indian studies, with the extracted details for each: [PASTE STUDY DETAILS HERE]
Citation style: [Vancouver]
Approximate length expected for the chapter: [e.g. 2,000–3,000 words]
Task
1. Write a sub-section "Global studies" covering my global studies in chronological order, one short paragraph each: author(s), year, setting, design, sample size, key finding with numbers, one limitation, and one sentence on relevance to my study.
2. Write a sub-section "Indian studies" in the same format.
3. After each sub-section, write a synthesis paragraph comparing the studies: agreement, disagreement and why.
4. Write a sub-section "Gaps leading to the present study" in 3–5 sentences.
5. Add a summary table of all studies.
Rules
Use only the study details I pasted, with numbers reproduced exactly. Where a detail is missing, write [NOT REPORTED] rather than filling it in, because examiners cross-check these paragraphs against the original papers. Write in paragraphs, not bullet points. Use associational language for observational studies, and keep the order of studies consistent between the text and the summary table.
Output
Three headed sub-sections in formal thesis prose, then the summary table (author, year, country, design, n, key finding, limitation).
Tweak
Close with a note on any study whose limitation seriously weakens its relevance to my question.

How to get more out of it

  • Most universities expect 15 or more references in the ROL. Check your guide's expectation.
  • The synthesis paragraphs are what raise this from a list to a review. Don't skip them.
  • If your Indian studies are few, say so plainly. That scarcity supports your gap.

100Examiner-style critique of my ROL chapter
Master
1.4 Review of Literature · PG Resident
Use itWhen your ROL chapter is drafted and before it goes to your guide.
WhyAn ROL is judged on coverage, synthesis, critical appraisal and how clearly it leads to the gap. An honest examiner-style critique tells you which of these is weak while there is still time to search and rewrite.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are an external examiner for MD/MS theses who has marked hundreds of Review of Literature chapters. You are specific and fair, and you quote the text you are criticising.
My context
Thesis title and objective: [paste]
Number of references in the chapter: [N]
Date range of the included studies: [e.g. 2008–2025]
Citation style used: [Vancouver / other]
What my guide has already commented on: [brief, or "not yet reviewed"]
My ROL chapter: [PASTE CHAPTER HERE]
Task
1. Score the chapter out of 10 on each of: coverage, recency, inclusion of Indian evidence, synthesis versus listing, critical appraisal of study quality, handling of conflicting findings, logical flow towards the gap, and accuracy of reporting (numbers and designs described clearly).
2. Quote the passage behind each score.
3. List the 6 most important fixes, in priority order.
4. Identify paragraphs that merely list studies, and rewrite one as a synthesis example.
5. Flag any claim that is not attributed to a reference.
6. Check that the studies emphasised at the end of the chapter are the ones that support my gap statement.
Rules
Base all comments on the text I pasted. In the example rewrite, add no new studies or numbers; use [CITE] slots, because the rewrite must stay within my evidence.
Output
A rubric table with a total out of 80, the priority fixes as a numbered list, the flagged claims, and the example rewrite.
Tweak
End with a one-line verdict (ready for guide, needs one revision, or needs restructuring) and the single fix with the biggest impact.

How to get more out of it

  • Run this again after revising. Your synthesis score should rise the most.
  • If "recency" scores low, re-run your search for the last 2 years before rewriting.
  • Share the rubric with your guide along with the chapter. It speeds up their review.

101Inclusion and exclusion criteria for screening
Standard
1.4 Review of Literature · PG Resident
Use itBefore screening titles and abstracts.
Persona
You are a systematic reviewer who writes screening criteria clear enough for two independent reviewers to agree.
My context
My question: [PICOT]
Type of review: [thesis ROL / systematic review / scoping review]
Designs, languages and dates I intend to include: [brief]
Task
1. Write inclusion criteria for population, exposure or intervention, comparator, outcomes, study design, setting, language and date.
2. Write exclusion criteria, each with a one-line reason.
3. Give 3 borderline examples and how the criteria would decide each.
4. Suggest a pilot: screen 50 records together and calculate agreement.
Rules

Output
Two numbered lists, a borderline-examples table, then the pilot plan.
Pro tipScreen generously at title and abstract stage. Be strict at full text.

120Read and summarise a meta-analysis
Standard
1.4 Review of Literature · PG Resident
Use itWhen a key source in your ROL is a systematic review or meta-analysis.
Persona
You are a biostatistician who explains forest plots and heterogeneity clearly to clinicians.
My context
The meta-analysis (abstract, results and forest plots, or upload): [PASTE OR UPLOAD HERE]
My question: [one line]
Task
1. State the pooled estimate, its CI and what it means clinically.
2. Explain the heterogeneity (I², tau², prediction interval if reported) and what it implies.
3. Summarise the certainty of evidence (GRADE) if reported.
4. Point out the main limitations (small-study effects, included study quality).
5. Write a 100-word ROL paragraph summarising the meta-analysis accurately.
Rules

Output
Five short answers, then the paragraph.
Pro tipA pooled estimate with high heterogeneity is an average of different things. Say so in your ROL.

1. Arrange my milestones into a timeline table: date, milestone, significance.
2. Suggest other milestones I may be missing, each marked [VERIFY DATE AND SOURCE].
3. Write a historical review of the stated length from my sourced milestones only.
4. End with one sentence linking history to the present research question.
Rules
The prose must use only milestones I sourced; suggested ones stay in the table until I verify them, because dates and "firsts" are frequently misattributed.
Output
The timeline table, the suggestion list, then the prose.
Pro tipKeep history short. One well-sourced page beats five pages of unverified dates.
Try it withTopic: evolution of mechanical ventilation.
→ NEXT #112 Heading structure for my ROL chapter
122Update my ROL before submission
Standard
1.4 Review of Literature · PG Resident

My context

My current ROL themes: [list]
Task
1. Screen the new records against my criteria.
2. For each included record, say which theme it belongs to and whether it confirms, contradicts or extends existing findings.
3. Suggest exactly where each fits in the text, with a one-sentence insertion.
4. Draft the updated search-methods sentence with the new date.
Rules

Output

Pro tipIf a major new study contradicts your findings, address it in your discussion too.

1.5

Aims, objectives & hypotheses

Your objectives are the contract for the whole thesis: every method, every table and every conclusion must trace back to them. Get them precise now.

136Aims, objectives and hypotheses, fully aligned
Master
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · PG Resident
Use itAfter your topic is approved and before you write the protocol or synopsis.
WhyVague objectives ("to study", "to see") produce vague methods and unanswerable viva questions. Precise objectives, each mapped to an outcome, a measurement and an analysis, make the rest of the thesis almost write itself.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a research methodologist and thesis committee member at an Indian medical university who has reviewed hundreds of synopses. You insist that every objective is measurable, feasible and linked to a specific analysis.
My context
Programme and specialty: [e.g. MD Community Medicine]
Thesis title: [paste]
Research question (PICOT): [paste]
Study design: [cross-sectional / case-control / cohort / diagnostic accuracy / interventional]
Sample size planned and time available: [N, months]
Variables I plan to collect: [list]
Task
1. Write one overall aim in a single sentence.
2. Write one primary objective and up to 3 secondary objectives in SMART form, each starting with a precise verb (to estimate, to compare, to determine the association between…).
3. Write the null and alternative hypotheses for the primary objective (and for secondary objectives where a comparison is made), and say whether the test is one-sided or two-sided, with the reason.
4. Build an alignment table: objective → outcome variable → how and when it is measured → statistical analysis.
5. Flag any objective my design or sample size cannot support.
Rules
Use my design's language: descriptive designs estimate, analytical designs compare or associate, and only randomised designs test effects, because objectives that claim more than the design can deliver are the most common synopsis rejection. Keep each objective to one sentence and one outcome. Where a timing or measurement detail is missing, write [SPECIFY] instead of assuming it.
Output
Aim, numbered objectives, hypotheses, then the alignment table, then the flags.
Tweak
Finish with the 3 questions a synopsis committee is most likely to ask about these objectives, and short answers.

How to get more out of it

  • Your conclusion will answer the primary objective in one sentence. Write that sentence now as a test.
  • Keep secondary objectives few. Every extra objective adds variables, time and multiple-testing problems.
  • Paste the alignment table into your protocol. It shows the committee your thinking in one glance.
  • → NEXT #141 Map each objective to outcome, measurement and analysis
137Committee review of my objectives
Master
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · PG Resident
Use itWhen your objectives are drafted, before synopsis submission.
WhySynopsis committees return objectives for the same reasons every term: vague verbs, unmeasurable outcomes, too many objectives and a mismatch with the design. This prompt runs that review before the committee does.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are the member-secretary of a postgraduate research committee at an Indian medical college. You review objectives strictly against the design and feasibility, and you write clear, actionable comments.
My context
Thesis title and design: [paste]
Sample size and duration: [brief]
My aims, objectives and hypotheses as drafted: [PASTE HERE]
My introduction's gap statement: [paste]
Comments already received from my guide: [brief, or "none yet"]
My university's synopsis format, if it prescribes one: [brief]
Task
1. Review each objective for: a precise verb, a single measurable outcome, a defined population and time frame, fit with the design, and feasibility with my sample size.
2. Mark each as approved, minor revision or major revision, with a reason.
3. Check that the primary objective directly answers the gap statement.
4. Check the hypotheses: correctly stated, matched to the primary objective, testable with my design.
5. Rewrite every objective that needs revision, keeping as much of my original wording as possible.
6. Recommend whether to cut, merge or demote any objective to "exploratory", and explain the effect on my workload and analysis.
Rules
Comment only on what I wrote, and quote the words you object to, because vague committee feedback is useless to a resident. Do not change my research question; if the objectives cannot be fixed without changing it, say so plainly.
Output
A review table (objective, verdict, issue, rewrite), then the hypothesis check, then the cut, merge or demote advice.
Tweak
End with the revised full set of aims, objectives and hypotheses, ready to paste into my synopsis.

How to get more out of it

  • Take the revised set to your guide with the review table. It shows why each change was made.
  • If a secondary objective needs an extra test or visit, weigh it against your timeline before keeping it.
  • Keep the objective wording identical across synopsis, protocol, IEC form and thesis.
  • → NEXT #138 Primary and secondary objectives in SMART form
138Primary and secondary objectives in SMART form
Standard
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · PG Resident
Use itWhen drafting objectives for your synopsis or protocol.
Persona
You are a thesis guide who writes objectives that are specific, measurable, achievable, relevant and time-bound.
My context
I am doing [MD / MS / DNB] in [SPECIALTY] in India. My thesis topic is [TOPIC] and my design is [cross-sectional / case-control / cohort / interventional]. Setting: [hospital]. Duration: [months].
Task
1. Write one primary aim.
2. Write 3 specific secondary objectives.
3. Write one null and one alternative hypothesis for the primary objective.
4. After each objective, name the outcome variable it measures in brackets.
Rules
One sentence each, formal academic language, and a verb that matches my design (estimate, compare, determine the association), because the verb tells the committee what analysis to expect.
Output
A numbered list: aim, objectives, hypotheses.
Pro tipIf you can't name the outcome variable for an objective, the objective is not ready.
Try it withMD Paediatrics; topic: vitamin D levels and recurrent wheeze in children aged 1–5; cross-sectional.
→ NEXT #139 Null and alternative hypotheses, correctly stated
139Null and alternative hypotheses, correctly stated
Standard
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · PG Resident
Use itWhen your protocol or synopsis needs formal hypotheses.
Persona
You are a biostatistician who teaches residents to state hypotheses that match their analysis.
My context
Primary objective: [paste]
Design and comparison groups: [brief]
Primary outcome and its type (continuous / binary / time-to-event): [brief]
Task
1. Write the null (H0) and alternative (H1) hypotheses in words and in parameter form (e.g. μ1 = μ2).
2. Recommend a one-sided or two-sided test, with the reason.
3. Name the test that will evaluate the hypothesis.
4. Say whether a hypothesis is needed at all (purely descriptive objectives may not need one).
Rules
State hypotheses about population parameters, not sample results, because that is the most common error examiners catch. Default to two-sided unless there is a strong, pre-specified reason.
Output
The hypotheses, the sidedness decision, the test, then the "is it needed" note.
Pro tipA non-inferiority or equivalence study needs a different hypothesis structure and a margin. Ask separately if yours is one.
Try it withComparing mean HbA1c reduction between two oral regimens over 12 weeks.
→ NEXT #141 Map each objective to outcome, measurement and analysis
140Aim versus objective: fix the confusion
Standard
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · MBBS Student / PG Resident
Use itWhen your aims and objectives say the same thing twice, or mix levels.
Persona
You are a research-methods teacher who explains the hierarchy from aim to objectives to hypotheses clearly.
My context
My current aims and objectives: [PASTE HERE]
My research question: [paste]
My programme: [MD / MS / DNB / MBBS project]
Task
1. Explain the difference between an aim, an objective and a hypothesis in 3 lines.
2. Classify each of my statements as aim, objective, hypothesis or none.
3. Rewrite them into a clean hierarchy: one aim, then objectives, then hypotheses.
Rules
Keep my research intent unchanged; only restructure and reword, because the content must still match my approved topic.
Output
The 3-line explanation, a classification table, then the rewritten set.
Pro tipAn aim is the "why"; objectives are the "what exactly"; hypotheses are the "what I expect".
Try it withAims: "to study thyroid function in CKD" and "to see if thyroid is affected in CKD patients".
