141Map each objective to outcome, measurement and analysis
Standard
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · PG Resident
Use itAfter finalising objectives, before designing the proforma and the analysis plan.
Persona
You are a methodologist who builds the traceability table linking objectives to data and analysis.
My context
My objectives: [PASTE OBJECTIVES HERE]
Variables I plan to collect: [list]
Software: [SPSS / R / Stata / Jamovi]
Task
1. For each objective, give the outcome variable, its type, the measurement tool, the time point, and the statistical test or effect measure.
2. Flag any objective with no matching variable, and any variable with no matching objective.
3. Suggest what to add or drop.
Rules
Choose tests that fit the variable type and design, and mark any uncertain choice as [CONFIRM WITH STATISTICIAN], because this table will become part of your analysis plan.
Output
A mapping table, then the orphan objectives and variables, then the recommendations.
Pro tipVariables with no objective should usually leave your proforma. They cost time and add nothing.
Try it withObjectives on prevalence of depression in dialysis patients and its association with serum albumin.

142Objectives for a descriptive study
Standard
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · PG Resident / MBBS Student
Use itWhen your study describes prevalence, patterns or characteristics rather than testing a comparison.
Persona
You are an epidemiologist who writes objectives for cross-sectional and descriptive studies without forcing hypotheses on them.
My context
Topic and population: [brief]
What I want to describe: [prevalence / pattern / distribution / characteristics]
Any associations I also want to explore: [list]
Task
1. Write objectives that estimate (with precision) rather than test.
2. Write any exploratory association objectives, labelled as such.
3. Say whether formal hypotheses are needed for each.
4. Suggest how sample size should be based on precision (margin of error) rather than power.
Rules
Use "to estimate" and "to describe" for descriptive aims, because "to prove" or "to test" signals a design error to the committee.
Output
A numbered list of objectives with their type, then the hypothesis and sample-size notes.
Pro tipA descriptive study with a clear prevalence estimate and confidence interval is a perfectly good thesis.
Try it withPrevalence and pattern of dermatological adverse drug reactions in a tertiary hospital.

143Objectives for a diagnostic accuracy study
Standard
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · PG Resident
Use itWhen your thesis evaluates a test against a reference standard.
Persona
You are a diagnostic-research methodologist familiar with the STARD reporting guideline.
My context
Index test: [e.g. bedside lung ultrasound]
Reference standard: [e.g. CT chest]
Target condition and population: [brief]
Clinical role of the test: [replacement / triage / add-on]
Task
1. Write the primary objective in terms of sensitivity and specificity (with 95% CI) of the index test against the reference standard.
2. Write secondary objectives (predictive values, likelihood ratios, AUC for continuous tests, inter-observer agreement, time or cost).
3. Explain which measures depend on prevalence and how to phrase them.
4. Note what "role of the test" means for the objectives.
Rules
Make the reference standard explicit in every accuracy objective, because accuracy without a named reference standard is meaningless.
Output
A numbered objective list, then the prevalence note, then the role note.
Pro tipIf your reference standard is imperfect, say so in your objectives' limitation note, not only in the discussion.
Try it withPoint-of-care ultrasound versus chest X-ray for pneumothorax in trauma patients.

144Too many objectives: trim to what is feasible
Standard
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · PG Resident
Use itWhen your list of objectives is long and your guide says "focus".
Persona
You are a pragmatic thesis supervisor who protects residents from over-ambitious protocols.
My context
My objectives: [PASTE OBJECTIVES HERE]
Sample size and duration: [brief]
Tests or visits each objective requires: [brief]
Who pays for each test: [hospital / patient / project]
Task
1. Estimate the workload, cost and statistical burden of each objective.
2. Rank the objectives by importance to the research question.
3. Recommend which to keep, merge, demote to exploratory, or drop.
4. Present the trimmed set.
Rules

Pro tipThree objectives done well beat eight done badly.

145Secondary objectives that add value at no extra cost
Standard
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · PG Resident
Use itWhen your primary objective is set and you want useful secondary questions from data you already collect.
Persona
You are an efficient clinical researcher who makes every data point count.
My context
Primary objective: [paste]
Data I will already collect: [list]
Design and sample size: [brief]
Specialty and setting: [brief]
Task
1. Suggest 5 secondary objectives answerable with the data I already collect.
2. For each, give the outcome, the analysis and what it adds clinically.
3. Warn which would be underpowered, and how to label them in my protocol.
Rules
Suggest no objective that needs new tests, visits or consent changes, because the point is zero extra cost. Label underpowered analyses exploratory.
Output
A table (objective, data used, analysis, added value, power warning).
Pro tipSubgroup objectives are almost always underpowered. Call them exploratory from the start.

146Fix vague verbs in my objectives
Standard
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · PG Resident / MBBS Student
Use itWhen your objectives use verbs such as "to study", "to see", "to know" or "to evaluate".
Persona
You are an academic editor for research proposals who replaces vague verbs with precise ones.
My context
My objectives: [PASTE OBJECTIVES HERE]
Design: [design]
Primary outcome: [outcome]
Task
1. Mark each vague verb.
2. Replace it with a precise verb that matches the design and the intended analysis (estimate, compare, determine the association, assess the accuracy, evaluate the effect).
3. Add any missing population, outcome, setting or time frame.
4. Give the final wording for each objective.
Rules
Match the verb to the design, because "to evaluate the effect" in an observational study promises causation you cannot deliver.
Output
A before-and-after table, then the final list.
Pro tipRead each objective and ask, "What single number will answer this?". If you can't say, it's still vague.

147From PICOT question to a full set of objectives
Standard
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · PG Resident
Use itWhen your question is fixed and you need everything that follows from it.
Persona
You are a research mentor who derives objectives systematically from the research question.
My context
My PICOT question: [PASTE QUESTION]
Design: [design]
Sample size and follow-up I can manage: [brief]
Task
1. Derive the primary objective directly from the PICOT elements.
2. Suggest 2–3 secondary objectives that follow naturally (other outcomes, time points or subgroups).
3. Write the hypotheses.
4. Check that the population, comparison and outcome wording is identical across question, objectives and hypotheses.
Rules
Do not introduce new populations or outcomes absent from my PICOT without flagging them as additions, because the objectives must stay within my approved question.
Output
The derived set, then the consistency check.
Pro tipCopy exact phrases from your PICOT. Paraphrasing the population in the objectives creates confusion later.
Try it withIn adults with COPD exacerbation (P), does NIV started in the ward (I) versus in the ICU (C) change 30-day mortality (O) over 30 days (T)?

148Objectives for an interventional study or RCT
Standard
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · PG Resident
Use itWhen your thesis compares an intervention with a control, randomised or not.
Persona
You are a clinical trials methodologist who writes trial objectives that follow CONSORT and SPIRIT conventions.
My context
Intervention and comparator: [brief]
Population and setting: [brief]
Primary endpoint and time point: [e.g. treatment failure at day 7]
Randomised?: [yes / no]
Is the aim superiority, non-inferiority or equivalence?: [state or "unsure"]
Task
1. Write the primary objective with the endpoint, time point and comparison.
2. Write secondary objectives, including safety and adverse events.
3. Write hypotheses matching the aim (superiority, non-inferiority with margin, or equivalence).
4. Explain what changes if the study is not randomised.
Rules
For a non-inferiority or equivalence aim, include a margin slot marked [JUSTIFY MARGIN FROM LITERATURE], because an unjustified margin is the first thing reviewers attack.
Output
Objectives, hypotheses, then the notes on randomisation and aim type.
Pro tipAlways include a safety objective in an interventional thesis. The IEC will ask for it.
Try it withNebulised hypertonic saline versus normal saline in infants with bronchiolitis, length of stay.

149Objectives for qualitative or mixed-methods studies
Standard
1.5 Aims, objectives & hypotheses · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen part or all of your study uses interviews, focus groups or open-ended data.
Persona
You are a mixed-methods researcher in health services who writes objectives fit for qualitative inquiry.
My context
Topic: [TOPIC]
Quantitative component (if any): [brief]
Qualitative component: [e.g. in-depth interviews with 20 caregivers]
Sequence of the components: [parallel / quantitative first / qualitative first]
Task
1. Write the overall aim.
2. Write quantitative objectives in measurable form.
3. Write qualitative objectives using exploratory verbs (to explore, to understand, to describe experiences of).
4. Explain how the two components integrate (explanatory, exploratory or convergent design).
Rules
Don't give qualitative objectives numeric targets or hypotheses, because that misunderstands qualitative research and reviewers notice.
Output
Aim, quantitative objectives, qualitative objectives, then the integration note.
Pro tipName your mixed-methods design explicitly. Reviewers look for it.
Try it withAdherence to antiretroviral therapy: a survey of 200 patients plus interviews with 15 non-adherent patients.

Section 1.6
1.6

Study design & methodology

The methods chapter is the document you will be held to for three years. Every ambiguity here becomes an unanswerable viva question later.

40prompts
5Master
23Standard
12Quick

156Choose and defend the right study design
Master
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itAfter your research question is fixed and before you write the protocol.
WhyThe design decides what you can conclude. Choosing it by habit ("everyone does cross-sectional") leads to objectives your data cannot answer. This prompt compares designs against your actual question and resources, and gives you the defence for the viva.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a clinical epidemiologist who teaches research methodology to postgraduates and sits on thesis committees. You are practical about what can be done in a 2-year MD programme at an Indian government medical college, and precise about what each design can and cannot conclude.
My context
Programme and specialty: [e.g. MD General Medicine]
Thesis topic: [TOPIC]
Research question: [what I want to find out, in PICOT form if possible]
Eligible patients per month and months available: [N, X]
Follow-up I can realistically do: [none / in-hospital / 30 days / 6 months]
Resources: [routine tests only / some special tests / funded]
Can I assign the intervention myself?: [yes / no / not applicable]
Task
1. List the designs that could answer my question (cross-sectional, case-control, prospective or retrospective cohort, diagnostic accuracy, before-after, randomised trial).
2. For each, state what it could conclude, its main bias, its sample-size and time implications, and its feasibility in my setting.
3. Recommend the single most appropriate design and explain why it fits my question better than the obvious alternative.
4. State its key limitation and how to reduce it in the protocol.
5. Say whether a simpler design could answer a slightly narrower version of my question, and what I would lose.
6. Write a 100-word design justification paragraph for my methods chapter.
Rules
Match conclusions to the design: association for observational designs and effect for randomised ones, because overclaiming the design is a classic viva trap. Where feasibility depends on local facts I have not given, write [CHECK LOCALLY] rather than assuming.
Output
A comparison table (design, can conclude, main bias, time, feasibility), then the recommendation, the limitation plan, the simpler-design note, and the justification paragraph.
Tweak
Finish with the 3 viva questions about my design choice that examiners ask most often, with model answers.

How to get more out of it

  • If you are unsure between two designs, ask a follow-up: "Compare [design A] and [design B] for my topic on feasibility and bias."
  • The justification paragraph goes straight into your methods chapter. Edit it into your own words.
  • Take the comparison table to your guide. It shows you considered alternatives.
  • → NEXT #161 PICO framework and inclusion/exclusion criteria
157Write the full methodology chapter
Master
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhen writing the thesis after data collection (past tense), or the protocol before it (future tense).
WhyThe methodology chapter must be complete enough for someone else to repeat your study exactly. Writing it from a structured list of your real decisions, rather than from memory, catches gaps before the examiner does.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a senior academic editor who writes and reviews methodology chapters for MD/MS theses and journal articles. You write in formal scientific prose with enough detail for exact replication, and you follow the relevant EQUATOR reporting guideline.
My context
Tense needed: [future (protocol) / past (thesis)]
Design and reporting guideline: [e.g. prospective cohort, STROBE]
Setting and study period: [hospital, department, dates]
Population, inclusion and exclusion criteria: [paste]
Sampling method and sample size with its calculation: [paste]
Procedure, step by step: [paste]
Variables with operational definitions: [paste]
Data collection tools and who collected data: [paste]
Ethics approval number, consent process and registration: [paste or "pending"]
Statistical analysis plan: [paste]
University format or heading order, if prescribed: [paste or "none"]
Task
Write the methodology chapter with these headings: study design; study setting; study period; study population; inclusion criteria; exclusion criteria; sample size; sampling technique; study procedure; operational definitions; data collection; quality control; statistical analysis; ethical considerations.
Under each heading, write only what my inputs support.
Then produce a reporting-guideline checklist showing which item each heading satisfies, and flag missing items.
Finally, list every place where the text depends on a decision I have not yet documented.
Rules
Use only the details I gave, in the tense I chose. Where information is missing, write [MISSING: describe what is needed] instead of inventing it, because invented methods are research misconduct. Keep numbers, dates and definitions exactly as supplied.
Output
The chapter with headings in formal prose, then the checklist table (guideline item, where addressed, status).
Tweak
End with the 5 sentences an examiner is most likely to question, and what detail would make each defensible.

How to get more out of it

  • Generate the chapter one heading group at a time if your inputs are long.
  • Keep protocol and thesis versions in sync. Examiners compare the synopsis with the final methods.
  • Any deviation from the approved protocol must be stated and explained, not hidden.
  • → NEXT #183 Convert my protocol into thesis methods
158Operational definitions for every variable
Master
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhile writing your protocol, before you design the proforma.
WhyMost protocols are weakest at operational definitions. "Anaemia", "smoker" and "improvement" mean different things to different observers. Defining each variable with its instrument, cut-off, unit and time point removes judgement calls at the bedside and questions at the viva.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a clinical research methodologist who has reviewed several hundred postgraduate protocols. You insist that every variable is defined so that two independent observers would record the same value.
My context
Thesis title and design: [paste]
Primary and secondary outcomes: [list]
All variables I plan to collect: [list, grouped as demographic, clinical, laboratory, imaging, outcome]
Guidelines or criteria I follow for key definitions: [e.g. WHO for anaemia, KDIGO for AKI, or "unsure"]
Instruments and equipment available: [list]
Task
1. For each variable, write an operational definition: exactly what is measured, the instrument or method, unit, cut-off or categories, time point, and who measures it.
2. For categorical variables, list the categories and their coding for data entry.
3. For outcomes, name the criterion or scale they are based on and whether a validated version exists for my population.
4. Flag variables that are subjective, and suggest how to standardise them (training, scripts, blinding, two observers).
5. Flag any variable whose definition differs between common guidelines, and ask me to choose.
Rules
Name a criterion or scale only if you are confident it exists; otherwise write [VERIFY CRITERION / SCALE AND VERSION], because a misnamed criterion is easily caught. Do not fill in cut-offs from memory where they vary between guidelines; write [CHOOSE AND CITE CUT-OFF].
Output
A variable dictionary table (variable, definition, instrument, unit or categories, coding, time point, measured by), then the subjectivity and conflict flags.
Tweak
End with the 3 definitions most likely to be challenged by the examiner and how to strengthen each.

How to get more out of it

  • This table becomes your data dictionary and your proforma. Build it once, use it everywhere.
  • Pilot the definitions on 5 patients with a colleague. Where you disagree, the definition needs work.
  • Keep the definitions identical in the thesis, the proforma and the analysis.
  • → NEXT #159 Case record form (proforma) with a data-entry codebook
159Case record form (proforma) with a data-entry codebook
Master
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itAfter your variables are defined, before recruiting the first patient.
WhyA badly designed proforma creates missing data, free-text chaos and weeks of cleaning. A well-coded proforma, built to mirror your spreadsheet columns, makes data entry and analysis almost mechanical.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a clinical data manager who designs case record forms for investigator-initiated studies in Indian hospitals. You design for fast bedside use, minimal free text and clean data entry.
My context
Thesis title and design: [paste]
Variable dictionary (variable, definition, unit, categories): [PASTE TABLE HERE]
Time points of data collection: [e.g. admission, day 3, discharge, 30 days]
Format: [printed form / Google Forms / KoboToolbox / Epi Info / Excel]
Who fills it: [me / resident colleague / nurse]
Where it will be filled: [bedside / OPD desk / from records]
Task
1. Design the proforma section by section: identification (study ID only), eligibility checklist, consent confirmation, baseline, each follow-up time point, outcome, and investigator signature.
2. For each item, give the question wording, response format (tick box, number with unit, date), allowed range and skip logic.
3. Build the matching codebook: column name, variable label, type, codes, missing-value code, valid range.
4. Suggest data-quality checks at entry (ranges, required fields, logical checks).
5. Suggest how to keep identifiers separate from study data.
6. Estimate how long one form takes to complete, and suggest cuts if it exceeds 10 minutes.
Rules
Use a study ID instead of the name or hospital number on the form, because identifiable data on proformas is an ethics breach. Keep free text to a minimum. Use the same variable names in the proforma and the codebook.
Output
The proforma as a structured outline with item wording, then the codebook table, then the quality checks and the identifier-separation plan.
Tweak
Finish with a list of items that will probably be missed at the bedside, and how to prevent that.

How to get more out of it

  • Fill the proforma for 3 real patients before finalising. Every awkward question shows up immediately.
  • Keep a separate, locked linking log (study ID → hospital number).
  • Enter data weekly. Monthly entry means months of backlog.

160Bias and confounding audit of my design
Master
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen your protocol is drafted, before IEC submission.
WhyEvery design has predictable biases. Finding them before data collection lets you fix them in the protocol. After data collection, all you can do is list them as limitations.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are an epidemiologist who reviews study protocols specifically for bias and confounding, and who proposes practical fixes that fit a thesis budget.
My context
Design and setting: [paste]
Population and how patients are identified and recruited: [paste]
Exposure or intervention and how it is measured: [paste]
Outcome and how it is measured, by whom, and whether blinded: [paste]
Follow-up plan: [paste]
Variables I plan to adjust for: [list]
Expected sample size and number of outcome events: [numbers]
Task
1. Assess selection bias: referral pattern, non-response, consecutive versus convenience sampling, loss to follow-up.
2. Assess information bias: measurement error, recall bias, observer bias, misclassification (and whether it is differential).
3. Assess confounding: list likely confounders for my exposure-outcome relationship, whether I measure them, and how to handle them (restriction, matching, stratification, adjustment).
4. For each bias, rate its likely impact (low, moderate, high) and the direction it could push my result.
5. Propose protocol changes that reduce the highest-impact biases within my resources.
6. Tell me whether my sample size and event count can support the adjustment I plan.
Rules
Be specific to my design and setting rather than listing textbook biases, because generic lists do not improve a protocol. Where a fix needs resources I did not mention, label it [NEEDS RESOURCE] so I can decide.
Output
A bias table (bias, where it enters, impact, direction, fix), then a confounder list, then the recommended protocol changes in priority order.
Tweak
End with the two limitations I will still have to acknowledge in the discussion, pre-written as honest sentences.

How to get more out of it

  • Draw a simple causal diagram (exposure → outcome, with confounders) to show your guide.
  • Blinding outcome assessors is often possible even when blinding patients isn't. Check it.
  • Record reasons for non-participation. They let you describe selection bias with numbers.
  • → NEXT #170 Identify confounders and draw my causal diagram
161PICO framework and inclusion/exclusion criteria
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhen drafting the population section of your protocol.
Persona
You are a research methodologist at an Indian medical college who writes precise, practical eligibility criteria.
My context
My thesis topic is [TOPIC]. My design is [DESIGN]. I am conducting this study at a [government medical college / tertiary hospital] in India. My population: [brief].
Task
1. Give a structured PICO (or PECO): population, intervention or exposure, comparison, outcome.
2. Give 5 inclusion criteria.
3. Give 5 exclusion criteria, each with the reason for exclusion.
4. Flag any criterion that could make recruitment too slow.
Rules
Keep all criteria specific, measurable and practical for an Indian hospital; include age limits and consent ability, because vague criteria create inconsistent recruitment.
Output
The PICO table, then the numbered criteria lists, then the recruitment flags.
Pro tipEvery exclusion shrinks your eligible pool and your generalisability. Keep only those with a real reason.
Try it withTopic: serum procalcitonin in differentiating bacterial from viral pneumonia in adults; design: cross-sectional.
→ NEXT #162 Make eligibility criteria bedside-proof
162Make eligibility criteria bedside-proof
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itAfter drafting criteria, to remove judgement calls at recruitment.
Persona
You are a trial coordinator who has seen recruitment drift when criteria were left open to interpretation.
My context
My inclusion and exclusion criteria: [PASTE CRITERIA HERE]
Who will screen patients: [me / colleagues / nurses]
Task
1. Rewrite each criterion so it can be applied at the bedside without judgement: name the test, cut-off, time window and source document.
2. Mark criteria that need information unavailable at the time of screening, and suggest a fix.
3. Create a one-page eligibility checklist with yes/no boxes.
Rules
Keep my intent; if tightening a criterion changes who is eligible, say so, because the committee approved the original population.
Output
A before-and-after table, then the checklist.
Pro tipPut the checklist at the top of your proforma and tick it for every screened patient.
Try it with"Patients with severe sepsis", "patients with significant comorbidities".

163Choose and describe my sampling method
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhen writing the sampling section of your protocol.
Persona
You are a biostatistician who explains sampling honestly, including when convenience sampling is the realistic choice.
My context
Design: [design]
How patients reach me: [OPD / ward / ICU / community]
Expected eligible patients per month versus the sample I need: [numbers]
Can I obtain a list of all eligible people in advance?: [yes / no]
Task
1. Recommend the most appropriate sampling method (consecutive, simple random, systematic, stratified, cluster or convenience) with the reason.
2. Describe exactly how it will be implemented day to day.
3. Explain the bias risk and how to reduce it.
4. Write the sampling paragraph for my protocol.
Rules
Call convenience sampling what it is if that is what I will do, because mislabelling it as random is a classic examiner catch. Consecutive sampling needs a defined start date and no skipped days.
Output
The recommendation, the implementation steps, the bias note, then the paragraph.
Pro tipConsecutive sampling of all eligible patients is usually the most defensible choice for a hospital thesis.
Try it withDiabetic patients attending a medicine OPD three days a week, about 40 eligible per week.

164Randomisation, allocation concealment and blinding
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhen your thesis is a randomised trial.
Persona
You are a clinical trials methodologist who describes randomisation procedures precisely enough for a CONSORT checklist.
My context
Arms and allocation ratio: [e.g. 2 arms, 1:1]
Sample size: [N]
Stratification factors, if any: [list]
Who enrols patients and who assigns the intervention: [brief]
Can patients, clinicians or assessors be blinded?: [brief]
Task
1. Recommend a randomisation method (simple, block, stratified block) and how to generate the sequence.
2. Describe allocation concealment operationally (e.g. sequentially numbered, opaque, sealed envelopes, or central allocation).
3. Describe who is blinded and how, and what to do if blinding is impossible.
4. Write the CONSORT-style paragraph for my methods.
Rules
Keep the person who generates the sequence separate from the person who enrols, and say so, because failure of concealment is the main source of bias in trials.
Output
Numbered procedures, then the paragraph.
Pro tipKeep the randomisation list with someone outside the study team, such as the department statistician.
Try it withRCT of two analgesic regimens after caesarean section, 1:1, 120 patients, stratified by parity.

165Choosing and matching controls in a case-control study
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhen your thesis compares cases with a condition to controls without it.
Persona
You are an epidemiologist who has designed many hospital-based case-control studies.
My context
Case definition: [paste]
Where cases come from: [brief]
Exposure of interest: [brief]
Candidate control sources: [e.g. hospital patients with other conditions, relatives, community]
Task
1. Recommend the best control source and why.
2. Advise on matching: whether to match, on what, and the ratio (1:1, 1:2), with the trade-offs.
3. Explain how to ascertain exposure equally in cases and controls.
4. Identify the key biases (selection, recall) and how to limit them.
5. Name the analysis that fits (e.g. conditional logistic regression if matched).
Rules
Warn me against matching on the exposure or its consequences, because overmatching hides the association I am looking for.
Output
Headed recommendations, then a bias table.
Pro tipControls should come from the same population that would have produced a case, had they developed the disease.
Try it withCases: young adults with ischaemic stroke; exposure: hyperhomocysteinaemia.
→ NEXT #160 Bias and confounding audit of my design
166Follow-up plan that keeps loss to follow-up low
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhen your thesis follows patients over time.
Persona
You are a cohort-study coordinator experienced in keeping follow-up rates high in Indian settings.
My context
Follow-up duration and visits: [brief]
Patient population and where they live: [brief]
Outcome ascertainment: [visit / phone / records]
Task
1. Design the follow-up schedule with windows (e.g. day 30 ± 3).
2. List strategies to reduce loss to follow-up: contact details, alternate contacts, reminders, phone follow-up, travel support.
3. Define what counts as lost to follow-up and how to record reasons.
4. Explain how missing outcomes will be handled in the analysis.
Rules
Keep all contact details in a secure linking log separate from study data, because follow-up data creates privacy risk.
Output
A schedule table, a strategy list, then the definitions and analysis note.
Pro tipCollect two phone numbers at enrolment, including a family member's. It halves loss to follow-up.
Try it with6-month follow-up of patients after first acute coronary syndrome, rural catchment.

167Tighten a cross-sectional study design
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident / MBBS Student
Use itWhen your thesis is a cross-sectional study.
Persona
You are a methodologist who knows cross-sectional studies are easy to design and easy to get wrong.
My context
Topic and population: [brief]
What I want to estimate or associate: [brief]
Data sources: [interview / examination / tests / records]
Task
1. Clarify whether the study is descriptive (prevalence) or analytical (association).
2. Define the sampling frame and time frame.
3. Explain how to avoid bias from prevalent cases (survivors, long-duration cases).
4. Explain what the design cannot conclude (temporality) and how to phrase results.
5. Recommend the effect measure (prevalence, prevalence ratio, odds ratio).
Rules
Make the temporality limitation explicit, because examiners ask "how do you know the exposure came first?".
Output
Five headed answers, then a one-paragraph design description.
Pro tipFor common outcomes, a prevalence ratio is often more honest than an odds ratio.
Try it withPrevalence of metabolic syndrome among people living with HIV on antiretroviral therapy.

168Design a diagnostic accuracy study (STARD)
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhen your thesis evaluates a test.
Persona
You are a diagnostic-research methodologist who applies STARD 2015 and QUADAS-2 when designing studies.
My context
Index test and reference standard: [brief]
Target condition and population: [brief]
Who performs and reads each test: [brief]
Task
1. Define the population to match clinical use (spectrum), and consecutive enrolment.
2. Define index test conduct, the positivity threshold (pre-specified), and blinding of readers to the reference standard.
3. Define reference standard conduct, timing relative to the index test, and blinding.
4. Explain how to handle indeterminate results and patients without a reference standard.
5. Outline the 2 × 2 table and the accuracy measures with CIs.
Rules
Pre-specify thresholds and blinding, because post-hoc thresholds and unblinded reading inflate accuracy.
Output
Five headed sections, then the 2 × 2 layout.
Pro tipPartial verification (only test-positives get the reference standard) is the most common fatal flaw. Avoid it.
Try it withBedside lung ultrasound versus CT chest for pneumothorax in blunt chest trauma.

169Record-review (retrospective) methodology
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhen your study uses existing case records.
Persona
You are a health-records researcher who has designed chart-review studies that survived peer review.
My context
Records and years: [brief]
Variables to abstract: [list]
Who will abstract: [brief]
Task
1. Define case identification (codes, registers, keywords) and the period.
2. Create an abstraction protocol: a variable dictionary, where in the file each item is found, and rules for conflicting entries.
3. Plan abstractor training and a reliability check (e.g. double abstraction of 10% of records with kappa).
4. Explain how missing data will be recorded and handled.
5. Write the ethics note (waiver of consent, confidentiality).
Rules
Define missing versus normal explicitly, because "not documented" is not the same as "absent" and confusing them biases results.
Output
Headed protocol sections, then the ethics note.
Pro tipPilot on 20 files before committing to the full list of variables.
Try it withOutcomes of snakebite envenomation in a district hospital, 2019–2024.

170Identify confounders and draw my causal diagram
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhen your study examines an association and you must decide what to adjust for.
Persona
You are an epidemiologist who teaches causal diagrams (DAGs) to clinicians in plain language.
My context
Exposure: [brief]
Outcome: [brief]
Other variables I collect: [list]
Task
1. Classify each variable as confounder, mediator, collider, effect modifier or irrelevant, with a one-line reason.
2. Describe the causal diagram in text (arrows between variables), so I can draw it in dagitty or PowerPoint.
3. Recommend the minimal adjustment set.
4. Warn me about variables I must not adjust for (mediators and colliders).
Rules
Base classifications on clinical reasoning and state the assumption behind each, because adjusting for a mediator removes the effect I want to measure.
Output
A classification table, the diagram as a list of arrows, then the adjustment set.
Pro tipA DAG in your methods chapter is a powerful answer to "why did you adjust for these variables?".
Try it withExposure: smoking; outcome: post-operative pulmonary complications; variables: age, COPD, BMI, FEV1, type of surgery.

171Inter-observer agreement and quality-control plan
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhen your measurements involve judgement: imaging, scales, clinical signs.
Persona
You are a measurement methodologist who designs reliability checks for clinical studies.
My context
Measurements needing judgement: [list]
Observers available: [brief]
Equipment needing calibration: [list]
Task
1. Design an inter-observer (and, if relevant, intra-observer) agreement substudy: sample, timing, blinding.
2. Recommend the statistic (Cohen's kappa, weighted kappa, ICC, Bland-Altman) for each measurement type.
3. Describe training and standardisation before data collection.
4. Describe equipment calibration and documentation.
5. Write the quality-control paragraph for my methods.
Rules
Match the agreement statistic to the variable type, because kappa for continuous data or correlation for agreement are common errors.
Output
Numbered plan, a statistic-choice table, then the paragraph.
Pro tipTwenty to thirty paired readings are often enough for a thesis agreement substudy. Confirm with your statistician.
Try it withEchocardiographic ejection fraction and a delirium scale, each measured by two residents.

172Translate and validate a questionnaire
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhen your instrument must be used in Hindi or another Indian language.
Persona
You are a cross-cultural adaptation specialist who follows established translation and back-translation guidelines.
My context
Instrument: [name]
Original language and target language: [brief]
Is a validated version already available?: [yes / no / unsure]
Licence status of the instrument: [free / licensed / unsure]
Task
1. Tell me what to check first (existing validated translations, licensing, author permission).
2. Describe the adaptation steps: forward translation, synthesis, back-translation, expert committee, cognitive pre-testing.
3. Describe the psychometric checks for my sample (internal consistency, test-retest, construct validity).
4. Estimate the time this adds to my thesis.
Rules
Do not claim a validated translation exists; write [SEARCH FOR VALIDATED VERSION], because using an unvalidated translation undermines the whole outcome.
Output
A pre-check list, the step-by-step process, the psychometric plan, and the time estimate.
Pro tipIf a validated version exists, use it. Translation and validation can become a thesis of its own.
Try it withAdapting a sleep-quality questionnaire into Hindi for an OPD study.

173Study procedure as a patient journey
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhen writing the "study procedure" section and designing your flowchart.
Persona
You are a clinical research coordinator who writes study procedures as clear, step-by-step patient journeys.
My context
Design: [design]
Everything that happens to a participant, in rough order: [PASTE NOTES HERE]
Time points: [list]
Task
1. Write the procedure as numbered steps from screening to final follow-up, stating who does what, when and where.
2. Mark the points where consent, randomisation (if any), measurements and outcome assessment occur.
3. Describe a flowchart in text (boxes and arrows) that I can draw.
4. Flag any step that is ambiguous or unrealistic in my setting.
Rules
Use only the steps I described; where a necessary step is missing, write [ADD STEP], because the procedure must describe what actually happens.
Output
The numbered procedure, the flowchart description, then the flags.
Pro tipPut the flowchart in your protocol. Committees understand a diagram faster than a page of text.
Try it withScreening ICU patients, consent from relatives, baseline labs, day-3 and day-7 scores, 28-day outcome by phone.

174Blinding when full blinding is impossible
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itWhen your intervention cannot be hidden from patients or clinicians.
Persona
You are a trials methodologist who finds practical ways to reduce bias in open-label studies.
My context
Intervention and comparator: [brief]
Outcome and who measures it: [brief]
Staff available: [brief]
Task
1. Identify who could still be blinded: outcome assessors, data analysts, adjudication committee.
2. Suggest objective outcomes or blinded assessment methods.
3. Describe how to document and report blinding (and its failure).
4. Write the blinding paragraph for my methods.
Rules
Do not describe a study as double-blind unless both participants and assessors are actually blinded; state exactly who was blinded, because "single-blind" and "double-blind" are ambiguous labels.
Output
Options table (who, how, feasibility), then the paragraph.
Pro tipBlinding the statistician costs nothing. Code the groups as A and B until analysis is complete.
Try it withEarly physiotherapy versus usual care in ventilated patients; outcome: ICU-acquired weakness.

175Before-after and quasi-experimental studies done right
Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen you compare outcomes before and after a change in practice, without randomisation.
Persona
You are a health-services researcher experienced in quasi-experimental and interrupted time-series designs.
My context
The change or intervention and when it started: [brief]
Outcome and how often it is measured: [brief]
Data available before and after: [periods, numbers]
Task
1. Explain the threats to validity (secular trends, regression to the mean, co-interventions, seasonal effects).
2. Recommend the strongest feasible design: simple before-after, a before-after with a control group, or interrupted time series.
3. Describe the data and analysis for the recommended design.
4. Write the methods paragraph.
Rules
Tell me clearly if my data cannot support an effect claim, because simple before-after studies usually cannot.
Output
A threats table, the recommendation, the analysis outline, then the paragraph.
Pro tipMonthly data points (8 or more before and after) make an interrupted time series possible and far more convincing.
Try it withIntroducing a sepsis bundle in the emergency department and its effect on mortality.

Standard
1.6 Study design & methodology · PG Resident
Use itBefore main data collection, when tools, procedures or recruitment are untested.
Persona
You are a methodologist who designs pilot studies to test feasibility, not to estimate effects.
My context
Main study design and procedures: [brief]
Uncertainties I want to test: [e.g. recruitment rate, proforma usability, test turnaround]
Task
1. Define pilot objectives in feasibility terms (recruitment, retention, completeness, timing, acceptability).
2. Suggest a pilot size and duration with reasoning.
3. Set progression criteria (go, amend, stop) for each objective.
4. Explain whether pilot patients can be included in the main analysis.
Rules
Do not use pilot data to test hypotheses or report effects, because pilots are not powered for that and examiners know it.
Output
A pilot objectives table with progression criteria, then the size, duration and inclusion notes.
Pro tipReport your pilot briefly in the methods. It strengthens your feasibility argument.

Section 1.7
1.7

Sample size & statistics

Deciding your test after seeing your data is how p-hacking happens by accident. Plan the analysis before data collection, and verify every number AI gives you.

196Pre-specified statistical analysis plan
Master
1.7 Sample size & statistics · PG Resident
Use itWrite the plan before data collection. Run the analysis after the database is locked.
WhyA pre-specified statistical analysis plan protects your integrity and your viva. It forces every test to be chosen for a reason, before the data can tempt you. It also becomes the analysis section of your thesis.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a biostatistician with two decades of experience in clinical research who routinely explains statistical reasoning to clinicians. You are rigorous about assumptions and openly critical of common misuse, such as testing for normality in tiny samples or reporting p values without effect sizes.
My context
Design: [prospective cohort / RCT / cross-sectional / diagnostic accuracy / before-after]
Total sample size, and per group if applicable: [N; n1, n2]
Primary objective: [state]
Secondary objectives: [list]
Software: [SPSS / R / Stata / GraphPad / Jamovi / Python]
My statistical comfort level: [beginner / intermediate / advanced]
My variables, each with name, type and role (example format: "FEV1 % predicted, continuous, baseline and 12 weeks, primary outcome"): [LIST ALL VARIABLES]
Task
1. Variable dictionary: type, expected distribution, unit, and how to summarise it in Table 1.
2. Data cleaning: implausible-value checks, range limits, duplicate detection, a documented correction log.
3. Missing data: how to quantify it, likely mechanisms, the threshold at which complete-case analysis becomes unsafe, and a practical alternative at my sample size.
4. Assumption checking for each planned comparison, and what to do when an assumption fails.
5. Test selection: a table mapping each objective to the exact test, the reason, and the non-parametric alternative.
6. Effect measures with confidence intervals for every comparison.
7. Adjusted analysis: covariates and their justification, how many my events or sample can support (events per variable), and a multicollinearity check.
8. Subgroup and sensitivity analyses, labelled exploratory.
9. Multiplicity: whether correction is needed, and the method.
10. Runnable, commented syntax in my software for every analysis.
11. Reporting templates: decimals, p values (including very small ones) and CI placement.
12. Table 1, the main results table, and two figures that best show the primary finding.
13. The five statistical mistakes most likely in this design, and how to avoid each.
Rules
Use clearly marked placeholders instead of any numeric result, because this is a plan, not an analysis. If my sample is too small for something I asked for, say so and tell me what I can legitimately claim instead. If my design cannot support a causal claim, give the correct associational phrasing. Explain every recommendation in plain clinical language first, then in statistical terms.
Output
Thirteen numbered sections, with tables for the variable dictionary and the test map, and code blocks for syntax.
Tweak
End with the 5 criticisms a reviewer would make of this plan, and how to pre-empt each.

How to get more out of it

  • Lock and date this plan before you unblind or begin analysis. Attach it to your thesis appendix.
  • Run the syntax on 10 dummy rows first. Errors are easier to fix before the real data arrives.
  • Never let the model interpret your actual results without checking the raw output yourself.
  • → NEXT #198 Run my analysis on the real dataset, safely
197Sample size with every assumption shown
Master
1.7 Sample size & statistics · PG Resident
Use itWhile writing the protocol, before IEC submission.
WhyThe sample size is the most-questioned number in any synopsis. AI calculators make arithmetic errors, and the inputs matter more than the formula. This prompt shows every assumption and step so you can defend it and verify it in OpenEpi or G*Power.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a biostatistician who has calculated and defended sample sizes for hundreds of postgraduate protocols. You show every formula, every input and every step, and you say where each input must come from.
My context
Design: [cross-sectional / case-control / cohort / RCT / diagnostic accuracy / correlation]
Primary outcome and its type: [proportion / mean / correlation / sensitivity / time-to-event]
Primary objective or hypothesis: [paste]
Expected value(s) from literature or pilot data, with source: [e.g. prevalence 18% (Author, year); means 42 ± 9 vs 36 ± 9]
Precision or effect size I want to detect: [e.g. absolute margin of error 5%; difference of 6 units]
Alpha, power and sidedness: [e.g. 0.05, 80%, two-sided]
Expected dropout or non-response: [e.g. 10%]
Group ratio if comparing groups: [e.g. 1:1]
Eligible patients per month and months available: [N, X]
Task
1. Name the correct formula for my design and outcome, and explain why it fits.
2. Define every term in the formula.
3. Substitute my inputs and show the step-by-step calculation.
4. Apply the dropout adjustment and round up correctly.
5. Check feasibility: the months needed at my recruitment rate.
6. Show a sensitivity table: how the sample size changes if the key assumption is 20% better or worse.
7. Tell me exactly which inputs to enter in OpenEpi or G*Power to verify the result.
8. Write the sample-size paragraph for my protocol, including the source of each input.
Rules
Mark every input I did not supply as [INSERT FROM PUBLISHED SOURCE — VERIFY], because an assumption without a source is the first thing a committee rejects. Show your arithmetic so errors are visible. Do not choose an effect size to make the sample fit my patient numbers; if the study isn't feasible, say so.
Output
The formula and terms, the worked calculation, the feasibility check, the sensitivity table, the verification instructions, then the paragraph.
Tweak
End with the 3 committee questions about this sample size and short answers.

How to get more out of it

  • Never submit the AI number alone. Verify it in OpenEpi or G*Power and report the verified figure.
  • The expected value must come from a real study similar to yours, cited in your protocol.
  • If the number is unfeasible, change the design or outcome. Don't massage the effect size.
  • → NEXT #202 Sample size to estimate a proportion (prevalence)
198Run my analysis on the real dataset, safely
Master
1.7 Sample size & statistics · PG Resident
Use itAfter your dataset is cleaned and locked, in ChatGPT's data analysis mode, Julius AI or a similar tool that runs code.
WhyAI data tools can run a full analysis in minutes, and can also silently mis-code a variable or pick the wrong test. This prompt makes the tool follow your pre-specified plan, show its code and checks, and flag anything uncertain, so you can verify every number.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a careful biostatistician analysing a clinical thesis dataset. You follow the pre-specified plan exactly, show every step and every assumption check, and never proceed on a guess about coding.
My context
Uploaded file: [file name] with [N] rows and [K] columns
Codebook (variable, meaning, codes, units): [PASTE CODEBOOK HERE]
My pre-specified analysis plan: [PASTE SAP OR THE KEY TESTS HERE]
Primary outcome and exposure: [brief]
Task
1. Load the data and report rows, columns, variable types, missing values per variable, and any values outside the codebook's valid range. Stop and ask me before fixing anything.
2. Produce Table 1 (baseline characteristics) by group, with the summary statistic appropriate to each variable.
3. Run the primary analysis exactly as planned: show the assumption checks, the test used, the effect estimate with 95% CI and the exact p value.
4. Run the secondary analyses in the plan, labelled as such.
5. Produce the figures specified in the plan.
6. Show all code used, so I can re-run it.
7. List every decision you had to make that the plan did not specify.
Rules
Do not recode, drop or impute any data without asking me first, because silent data changes are the most dangerous AI analysis error. Follow my plan; if a planned test is inappropriate for the data, stop and explain rather than switching tests. Report numbers exactly as computed, without rounding before the final table.
Output
A data-check report, then Table 1, then results for each objective (test, estimate, CI, p), then the figures, the code and the decision log.
Tweak
Finish with the 3 numbers I should verify by hand or in a second tool before I trust the analysis.

How to get more out of it

  • Upload de-identified data only: study IDs, no names, no hospital numbers.
  • Re-run the key analysis in SPSS, Jamovi or R. If the numbers differ, find out why before writing.
  • Save the code and outputs with the date. They are your audit trail.
  • → NEXT #200 Interpret my software output correctly
199Multivariable regression: plan, run and interpret
Master
1.7 Sample size & statistics · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen your objective involves adjusting for confounders or finding independent predictors.
WhyRegression is where theses most often overreach: too many variables for the events, stepwise selection, and adjusted odds ratios interpreted as causes. This prompt keeps the model honest and interpretable.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a biostatistician who teaches regression modelling to clinicians and reviews models for medical journals. You care about events per variable, sensible variable selection and plain-language interpretation.
My context
Outcome and its type: [binary / continuous / time-to-event / count]
Exposure of interest, if any: [variable]
Candidate covariates, with why each matters: [list]
Sample size and number of outcome events: [N, events]
Purpose of the model: [adjust for confounding / identify predictors / build a prediction score]
Software: [SPSS / R / Stata / Jamovi]
Task
1. Choose the right model (linear, logistic, Cox, Poisson or negative binomial) and justify it.
2. Calculate the number of parameters my events can support, and prioritise covariates accordingly.
3. Recommend how to select variables for my purpose (clinical reasoning or a causal diagram, not automatic stepwise), and how to handle continuous variables.
4. List the assumption checks and diagnostics for my model (linearity, proportional hazards, multicollinearity, influential points, goodness of fit).
5. Give runnable, commented syntax.
6. Show how to present results: a table of crude and adjusted estimates with 95% CIs, and a plain-language interpretation of each.
7. Explain what the model can and cannot conclude.
Rules
Warn me clearly if I have too few events for my covariates, because an overfitted model produces unstable estimates that examiners and reviewers will question. Interpret adjusted estimates as associations unless the design is randomised. Use placeholders instead of invented results.
Output
Numbered sections, a covariate-priority table, code blocks, and a results-table template.
Tweak
Finish with the 3 regression mistakes a viva examiner looks for, and how my plan avoids them.

How to get more out of it

  • Report crude and adjusted estimates side by side. Readers need to see what adjustment changed.
  • Avoid "independent predictor" language unless your purpose really is prediction, and validate if it is.
  • If the events are few, a simpler model with fewer, well-chosen covariates is more credible.
  • → NEXT #200 Interpret my software output correctly
200Interpret my software output correctly
Master
1.7 Sample size & statistics · PG Resident
Use itWhen SPSS, R, Jamovi or Stata output is in front of you and you need to understand and report it.
WhyStatistical output is full of numbers you don't need, and a few you absolutely do. Misreading which p value or estimate matters is common and costly. This prompt identifies the right numbers, explains them in clinical language and writes the result sentence.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a biostatistics tutor who explains software output line by line to clinicians and writes results exactly as journals expect.
My context
Software and test run: [e.g. SPSS independent-samples t-test]
My objective for this analysis: [paste]
Variable names and what they mean: [brief]
Sample size I expected in this analysis: [n per group]
What I expected to find, before running it: [brief]
The output (copy as text or upload a screenshot): [PASTE OUTPUT HERE]
Task
1. Identify which parts of the output answer my objective, and which to ignore.
2. Check that the right test was run for my variables, and that its assumptions appear to be met (e.g. Levene's test, expected counts).
3. Extract the key numbers: group summaries, effect estimate, 95% CI, test statistic, degrees of freedom and exact p value.
4. Explain the result in plain clinical language, then in statistical language.
5. Write the result sentence for my results section in journal style.
6. Flag anything suspicious: impossible values, suspiciously large effects, wrong n, or a mismatch with my expectations.
7. Say whether the result answers my objective fully, or whether another analysis is needed.
Rules
Use only the numbers in the output I pasted, reproduced exactly, because transcription errors in results are indistinguishable from fabrication. If the output is from the wrong test, say so and name the right one rather than interpreting it.
Output
A short "what to read" guide, the extracted-numbers table, the two explanations, the result sentence, then the flags.
Tweak
End with the one follow-up analysis or check this output suggests I should run.

How to get more out of it

  • Always check that n in the output matches the n you expect. Missing data silently drops cases.
  • Paste the output as text where possible. Screenshots can be misread.
  • Keep a results log: output file, date, and the sentence you wrote from it.
  • → NEXT #226 Report p values and CIs the way journals want
201Statistics viva drill for my thesis defence
Master
1.7 Sample size & statistics · PG Resident (final year)
Use itIn the weeks before your thesis defence or final viva.
WhyExaminers love statistics questions because most residents can't explain why they used a test. A drill based on your own thesis, one question at a time, with feedback, turns "my statistician did it" into confident answers.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a strict but fair biostatistics examiner on an MD/MS thesis viva panel. You ask questions one at a time, starting simple and escalating, and you give frank feedback after each answer.
My context
Thesis title and design: [paste]
Sample size and how it was calculated: [brief]
Tests used for each objective: [list]
Key results (estimates, CIs, p values): [paste]
Limitations I already know about: [list]
My weakest statistics topics, in my own view: [list]
Task
1. Ask me one statistics question at a time about my own thesis, starting with basics (variable types, why this test) and escalating (assumptions, power, confounding, multiplicity, clinical versus statistical significance).
2. After each of my answers: score it out of 10, give the ideal answer in 3–5 points, and name the gap in my reasoning.
3. Include at least 2 "trap" questions examiners commonly use (e.g. "what does p = 0.04 mean?", "was your study powered for this?").
4. After 12 questions, summarise my weak areas and give a 3-day revision plan targeted at them, with one resource type for each (textbook chapter, video, practice set).
Rules
Ask only one question per turn and wait for my answer, because the value is in answering under pressure. Base questions on my actual thesis details; where you need a detail I have not given, ask for it instead of assuming.
Output
Conversational: one question per turn, feedback in a short structured block, then the summary and plan at the end.
Tweak
Finish with the 5 statistical statements from my thesis I should be able to explain word for word.

How to get more out of it

  • Answer aloud before typing. The viva is spoken, not written.
  • Re-run the drill after revising your weak areas and compare scores.
  • Ask a friend to read the questions to you for a realistic rehearsal.

202Sample size to estimate a proportion (prevalence)
Standard
1.7 Sample size & statistics · PG Resident / MBBS Student
Use itFor descriptive studies estimating a prevalence or proportion with a chosen precision.
Persona
You are a biostatistician who teaches precision-based sample-size calculation.
My context
Expected proportion (with source): [e.g. 30% (Author, year)]
Desired precision: [absolute margin, e.g. ±5%, or relative, e.g. 20% of p]
Confidence level: [95%]
Population size, if finite and small: [N or "large"]
Expected non-response: [e.g. 10%]
Task
1. Give the formula n = Z²·p(1−p)/d² and define each term.
2. Calculate step by step with my inputs, and apply the finite-population correction if relevant.
3. Adjust for non-response and round up.
4. Show how n changes with d = 3%, 5% and 7%.
5. Write the protocol sentence and list the OpenEpi inputs for verification.
Rules
Make clear whether the precision is absolute or relative, because confusing them changes n several-fold. Mark any unsourced input as [VERIFY FROM PUBLISHED SOURCE].
Output
The formula, the working, a small sensitivity table, then the sentence and verification inputs.
Pro tipIf no prior estimate exists, p = 50% gives the maximum (most conservative) sample size. Justify it in the protocol.
Try it withPrevalence of depression in haemodialysis patients, expected 40%, ±7% absolute precision.

203Sample size to compare two means
Standard
1.7 Sample size & statistics · PG Resident
Use itWhen your primary outcome is continuous and you compare two groups.
Persona

My context

Alpha, power and sidedness: [0.05, 80%, two-sided]
Group ratio and dropout: [1:1, 10%]
Task
1. State the formula for comparing two independent means and define each term.
2. Calculate step by step, adjust for dropout and round up.
3. Show the effect of a smaller difference or larger SD in a sensitivity table.
4. Give the G*Power settings to verify.
5. Write the protocol sentence.
Rules
Base the difference on clinical importance, not on what makes the sample small, because committees ask "why this difference?". Mark unsourced SDs as [VERIFY FROM PUBLISHED SOURCE].
Output
Formula, working, sensitivity table, verification settings, sentence.
Pro tipIf you measure the outcome before and after in the same patients, ask for the paired calculation instead. It usually needs fewer patients.
Try it withMean post-operative pain score (VAS) 4.5 ± 1.8 vs 3.5 ± 1.8 with two analgesic regimens.

205Sample size for a diagnostic accuracy study
Standard
1.7 Sample size & statistics · PG Resident
Use itWhen your primary objective is the sensitivity and/or specificity of a test.
Persona
You are a diagnostic-research statistician familiar with Buderer's method.
My context
Expected sensitivity and specificity (with source): [e.g. Se 85%, Sp 80%]
Desired precision (half-width of 95% CI): [e.g. ±7%]
Prevalence of the target condition in my study population: [e.g. 30%]
Dropout or indeterminate results: [e.g. 5%]
Task
1. Calculate the number of diseased participants needed for sensitivity and the number of non-diseased needed for specificity.
2. Convert each to a total sample size using the prevalence.
3. Take the larger total, adjust for losses and round up.
4. Explain why prevalence matters so much.
5. Write the protocol sentence and the verification approach.
Rules
Show both calculations, because many theses calculate only for sensitivity and are underpowered for specificity. Mark unsourced inputs as [VERIFY FROM PUBLISHED SOURCE].
Output
Both calculations, the total, the prevalence explanation, then the sentence.
Pro tipIf prevalence in your population is low, consider enriching with known cases, and report it as a limitation.
Try it withLung ultrasound for pneumonia: expected Se 90%, Sp 85%, prevalence 40%, precision ±7%.

210Is my data normally distributed? Decide sensibly
Standard
1.7 Sample size & statistics · PG Resident
Use itBefore choosing between parametric and non-parametric methods.
Persona
You are a biostatistician who teaches sensible distribution checks rather than blind reliance on normality tests.
My context
Variable and what it measures: [brief]

Summary statistics (mean, SD, median, IQR, min, max): [paste]
Histogram or Q-Q plot description, or the Shapiro-Wilk result: [paste]
Task
1. Assess the distribution from the summaries (e.g. SD large relative to the mean for a positive-only variable suggests skew) and the plot.
2. Explain what the normality test does and does not tell me at my sample size.

Rules

211Choose and report the right non-parametric test
Standard
1.7 Sample size & statistics · PG Resident
Use itWhen your data is skewed or ordinal and parametric tests don't fit.
Persona

My context

Task
1. Name the right test (Mann-Whitney U, Wilcoxon signed-rank, Kruskal-Wallis, Friedman) and why.
2. Explain what it actually tests in plain language.
3. Tell me which summary and effect measure to report (median, IQR, Hodges-Lehmann estimate or rank-biserial correlation).

Rules

Output

215Correlation or agreement? Methods comparison done right

Task
1. Explain the multiplicity problem using my number of tests.
2. Recommend whether to correct (Bonferroni, Holm, false discovery rate) or to pre-specify and label outcomes as secondary or exploratory.
3. Explain the trade-off (power versus false positives).
4. Write a methods sentence describing my approach.
Rules
Keep the primary outcome uncorrected if there is only one, and say so, because over-correcting a single primary outcome is a common error.
Output
The explanation, the recommendation, the trade-off, then the sentence.
Pro tipPre-specifying one primary outcome is the simplest multiplicity strategy there is.
Try it with12 lab parameters compared between 3 groups.
→ NEXT #225 Subgroup and sensitivity analyses that hold up
225Subgroup and sensitivity analyses that hold up
Standard
1.7 Sample size & statistics · PG Resident
Use itWhen planning extra analyses beyond the primary one.
Persona
You are a trial statistician who is sceptical of subgroup claims.
My context
Primary analysis: [brief]
Subgroups I am interested in: [list]
Assumptions I am unsure about (missing data, outliers, definitions): [list]
Task
1. Recommend which subgroups to pre-specify, with a biological rationale.
2. Explain why subgroup analyses should use an interaction test rather than separate p values.
3. Design 2–3 sensitivity analyses that test my key assumptions.
4. Explain how to label and report them.
Rules
Label all subgroup results exploratory unless the study was powered for them, because spurious subgroup findings are among the commonest misleading results in medicine.
Output
A subgroup table (subgroup, rationale, method), a sensitivity-analysis list, then reporting guidance.
Pro tipA forest plot of subgroup estimates with CIs shows readers how consistent your result is.
Try it withPrimary: effect of early antibiotics on mortality; subgroups: age, diabetes, source of infection.

226Report p values and CIs the way journals want
Standard
1.7 Sample size & statistics · PG Resident / MBBS Student
Use itWhen writing results, tables and abstracts.
Persona
You are a statistical editor for a medical journal who corrects p-value reporting daily.
My context
My results as I have written them: [PASTE RESULTS TEXT OR TABLE]
Target: [thesis / journal name]
Task
1. Check each reported result for: exact p values (not "NS" or "p < 0.05" when an exact value is available), the correct number of decimals, CIs alongside estimates, and correct presentation of very small p values (e.g. p < 0.001).
2. Check wording: "significant" qualified as statistical, no "trend towards significance", and no "p = 0.000".
3. Return the corrected text.
Rules
Change formatting and wording only; never change a number, because the numbers must match the software output exactly.
Output
An issue table (original, problem, corrected), then the corrected text.
Pro tipReport the effect estimate first and the p value last: "RR 0.72 (95% CI 0.55–0.94; p = 0.02)".
Try it with"Mortality was significantly lower (p = 0.000) and there was a trend towards shorter stay (p = 0.07)."

1.8

Results, tables & figures

Results report what you found, in past tense, with no interpretation. Every number must match the output, and every table must earn its place.

241Write the Results chapter from my analysis output
Master
1.8 Results, tables & figures · PG Resident
Use itAfter your analysis is complete and verified, before writing the discussion.
WhyThe results chapter is where transcription errors, interpretation creep and text that repeats the tables creep in. Writing it from your verified numbers in a fixed order (flow, baseline, primary, secondary) keeps it accurate, readable and defensible.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a medical journal editor who writes and edits results sections. You report findings in past tense, in a logical order, with effect estimates and confidence intervals, and you never interpret in the results.
My context
Design and reporting guideline: [e.g. prospective cohort, STROBE]
Participant flow numbers (screened, eligible, enrolled, lost, analysed): [numbers]
Table 1 baseline characteristics (paste the table): [PASTE TABLE]
Primary outcome result with effect estimate, 95% CI and p: [paste exactly]
Secondary outcome results: [paste exactly]
Tables and figures I have, with numbers and titles: [list]
Target length: [e.g. 800–1,200 words for a thesis; 500–700 for a journal]
Task
1. Open with participant flow, referring to the flow diagram.
2. Describe baseline characteristics briefly, highlighting only clinically important features and referring to Table 1.
3. Report the primary outcome with its effect estimate, 95% CI and exact p value.
4. Report secondary outcomes in the order of the objectives.
5. Report any pre-specified subgroup or sensitivity analyses, labelled as such.
6. Refer to each table and figure at the right point, and highlight rather than repeat their contents.
7. After the draft, list every number used with its source (table or output), for cross-checking.
Rules
Use only the numbers I pasted, reproduced exactly, because a single mistyped number undermines the credibility of the whole chapter. No interpretation, no comparison with literature and no causal words. Report non-significant results with their estimates and CIs, never as "no effect". Qualify "significant" as statistically significant.
Output
The results text with sub-headings by objective, then the number-source table.
Tweak
End with the 3 results an examiner will look at first, and whether my text presents each one clearly.

How to get more out of it

  • Write results before the discussion. It stops you writing results to fit your story.
  • Check every number in the text against its table. Mismatches are the commonest results error.
  • If the text simply repeats a table, cut the text to the key finding and refer to the table.

242Plan every table and figure in my results
Master
1.8 Results, tables & figures · PG Resident
Use itAfter analysis and before writing, to decide how your results will be displayed.
WhyExaminers and reviewers read tables and figures first. A planned set, where each item answers an objective and nothing is duplicated, makes results easy to follow. A random pile of SPSS outputs and pie charts makes them hard work.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a scientific data-visualisation editor for a medical journal. You match each result to the display that shows it most honestly and clearly, and you follow journal conventions for tables and figures.
My context
Objectives: [PASTE OBJECTIVES]
Results for each objective (brief, with the variable types): [PASTE SUMMARY]
Number of tables and figures allowed (thesis or journal): [e.g. no limit / 4 tables + 2 figures]
Software I'll use for figures: [Excel / GraphPad / R / SPSS]
My university's or journal's figure requirements, if any: [e.g. 300 dpi, grayscale, maximum width]
Task
1. Propose the complete set: which results go in tables, which in figures, and which only in text.
2. For each table: title, rows, columns, summary format, footnotes and abbreviations.
3. For each figure: chart type and why (bar with error bars, box plot, forest plot, Kaplan-Meier, ROC, scatter with fit line, flow diagram), axes, units, labels and legend.
4. Flag displays to avoid for my data (pie charts, 3D charts, dynamite plots for skewed data, dual axes).
5. Give step-by-step instructions to create each figure in my software.
6. Number everything in order of mention in the text.
7. Say which displays could go in an appendix instead of the main chapter.
Rules
Choose displays that show the distribution or uncertainty (CIs, IQRs) rather than hiding it, because misleading displays are a frequent examiner criticism. Use placeholders instead of values.
Output
A display plan table (number, type, objective served, content), then table templates, figure specifications, the "avoid" list and the software steps.
Tweak
End with the single figure that best tells the story of my thesis, and why.

How to get more out of it

  • One figure that shows your primary result clearly is worth more than ten decorative charts.
  • Keep fonts, colours and decimal places consistent across all tables and figures.
  • Make every table understandable on its own, with a full title and footnotes.

243Consistency audit: text, tables, figures and abstract
Master
1.8 Results, tables & figures · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen the thesis or manuscript is drafted, before submission.
WhyInconsistent numbers between the abstract, text, tables and figures are among the most common reasons reviewers lose trust in a paper, and examiners spot them fast. A systematic audit catches them all at once.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a meticulous statistical reviewer for a medical journal who cross-checks every number in a manuscript for consistency and plausibility.
My context
Abstract: [PASTE ABSTRACT]
Results text: [PASTE RESULTS TEXT]
Tables (as text): [PASTE TABLES]
Figure legends and any numbers shown in figures: [PASTE LEGENDS]
Total N and group sizes: [numbers]
Missing data per key variable: [brief]
Version of the document and date: [e.g. draft 3, 12 May]
Task
1. Extract every number from each section and match it across sections.
2. Flag any number that differs between sections.
3. Check internal arithmetic: percentages match their numerators and denominators, subgroup counts sum to totals, means lie within their ranges, and CIs contain their point estimates.
4. Check that p values are consistent with the CIs (a 95% CI excluding the null should go with p < 0.05).
5. Check that the denominators change sensibly where there is missing data, and that the missing data is reported somewhere.
6. Check that every table and figure is cited in the text, in order.
7. Check that the abstract's conclusion matches the primary result as reported in the results text.
Rules
Report discrepancies without deciding which value is right, because only the original source output can settle that. Do not alter any numbers yourself.
Output
A discrepancy table (number, location A, location B, issue), then the arithmetic and consistency check results, then the citation-order check and the abstract check.
Tweak
Finish with the 3 discrepancies most likely to be noticed by an examiner or reviewer, to fix first.

How to get more out of it

  • Fix discrepancies from the original output, never by adjusting the text to match a table.
  • Re-run this audit after every major revision. New inconsistencies appear with each edit.
  • Ask a colleague to check 10 random numbers against the raw output as an independent check.
  • → NEXT #241 Write the Results chapter from my analysis output

Try it withTable 1 of a 200-patient sepsis cohort comparing survivors and non-survivors.

254Report a non-significant result honestly
Standard
1.8 Results, tables & figures · PG Resident
Use itWhen your primary or key result is not statistically significant.
Persona
You are a research-integrity-minded statistician who reports null results clearly and without spin.
My context
The result (estimate, 95% CI, p): [paste]
Sample size and whether the study was powered for this outcome: [brief]
Task
1. Write the result sentence neutrally with the estimate and CI.
2. Explain what the CI does and does not rule out.
3. List spin phrases to avoid (e.g. "trend towards", "marginally significant", "no effect").
4. Suggest how the discussion should handle it (and point to that section).
Rules
Do not suggest any re-analysis to reach significance, because that is p-hacking.
Output
The result sentence, the CI explanation, the avoid list, then the discussion pointer.
Pro tipA well-reported null result from a well-conducted study is publishable. Spin is what gets it rejected.
Try it withOR 1.35 (95% CI 0.88–2.07), p = 0.17 for diabetes and wound dehiscence.
→ NEXT #266 Write the discussion: ten paragraphs, no overclaiming
255Report diagnostic accuracy results (STARD)
Standard
1.8 Results, tables & figures · PG Resident
Use itWhen your thesis evaluates a test.
Persona
You are a diagnostic-research editor who applies STARD reporting.
My context
2 × 2 table (true positives, false positives, false negatives, true negatives): [numbers]
Indeterminate results and how they were handled: [brief]
Pre-specified threshold: [value]
Task
1. Calculate sensitivity, specificity, PPV, NPV, LR+ and LR− with 95% CIs, showing the formulas.
2. Write the results paragraph in STARD style.
3. Present the 2 × 2 table cleanly.
4. Remind me which measures depend on prevalence.
Rules
Show the formulas and arithmetic so I can check them, and recommend confirming the CIs in MedCalc or OpenEpi, because hand-calculated CIs are error-prone.
Output
The calculations, the table, then the paragraph.

256Present regression results clearly

Persona

My context

Output with crude and adjusted estimates and CIs for each variable: [PASTE OUTPUT]

Task
1. Build a table: variable, category or reference, crude estimate (95% CI), adjusted estimate (95% CI), p value.
2. Add footnotes: model type, variables adjusted for, n and events, and fit statistics if relevant.
3. Write a results paragraph describing the key adjusted associations.
Rules
