266Write the discussion: ten paragraphs, no overclaiming
Master
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itAfter results are final. This is the section examiners and reviewers read most closely.
WhyThe discussion is where good data gets ruined by overclaiming, and it is the only section where your scientific judgement is visible. A fixed structure (answer, compare, explain, implications, strengths, limitations, future, conclusion) keeps it honest and complete.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a journal editor and senior academic who has written and revised hundreds of discussion sections and rejected many more. You are ruthless about overinterpretation, unsupported causal language, and limitation sections that hide the real weaknesses.
My context
Design: [state] · Sample size: [state]
Primary objective: [state]
Primary result with effect estimate and CI: [paste exactly as computed]
Secondary results: [paste, with numbers]
Findings that surprised me or contradicted my hypothesis: [state]
Findings that were not significant: [state]
Known limitations: [list]
Setting: [Indian tertiary / rural / private, etc.] · Target: [thesis / journal name]
I have uploaded the [N] most relevant published papers. Use only these, plus anything you retrieve through live search with a verifiable PMID or DOI.
Task
Draft a discussion of about [800–1,200] words:
1. Paragraph 1: answer the research question in 1–3 sentences with the principal finding and its numbers. No literature.
2. Paragraphs 2–4: for each key comparison, state what the prior study found, whether mine agrees or differs, and the likely reason (population, setting, definitions, measurement, sample size, follow-up). End every comparison with an interpretive sentence.
3. Paragraph 5: the most plausible mechanism, marked clearly where speculative.
4. Paragraph 6: clinical implications: who could act on this and how; say plainly if it is not yet practice-changing.
5. Paragraph 7: strengths, without inflation.
6. Paragraph 8: limitations in order of threat to the conclusion, each with the likely direction of bias.
7. Paragraph 9: specific, doable future research, not "further studies are needed".
8. Paragraph 10: a 2–3 sentence conclusion that does not exceed the data.
Rules
Use associational language unless my design is randomised; "caused", "led to", "resulted in" and "prevented" are banned otherwise, because causal overreach is the most common reviewer criticism. Qualify every "significant" as statistical or clinical. Never call a non-significant result a "trend". Where a comparison needs a source I have not supplied, write [CITATION NEEDED — FIND SOURCE]. Introduce no result that is not in my results section.
Output
The discussion in paragraphs with no sub-headings unless my target requires them, within the word range.
Tweak
Then list separately the three sentences a hostile reviewer would attack first, and how to rewrite each one defensively.

How to get more out of it

  • Give the model your actual numbers. A discussion written around vague findings reads exactly as vague as it was written.
  • The hostile-reviewer list is worth more than the draft. Act on it before submission.
  • If the draft sounds more confident than you feel about your own data, the draft is wrong, not your instinct.
  • → NEXT #267 Limitations that are honest, ranked and specific
267Limitations that are honest, ranked and specific
Master
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itWhen writing the limitations paragraph, and before your viva.
WhyExaminers judge your scientific maturity by your limitations. A generic list ("small sample, single centre") signals you didn't think. Limitations ranked by threat, each with the direction of bias and what you did to reduce it, signal that you did.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a clinical epidemiologist and journal reviewer who evaluates limitation sections for honesty and insight. You expect each limitation to state how it could have changed the result.
My context
Design and setting: [paste]
How participants were selected and how many declined or were lost: [brief with numbers]
How the exposure and outcome were measured, and by whom: [brief]
Confounders measured and not measured: [lists]
Sample size, and whether the study was powered for each reported outcome: [brief]
Primary result: [estimate with CI]
Limitations I have thought of: [list]
Task
1. Identify all important limitations under selection, information (measurement), confounding, sample size or precision, and generalisability.
2. Rank them by how much they threaten my main conclusion.
3. For each, state the likely direction of bias (towards or away from the null, or unpredictable) and whether it could explain my result entirely.
4. For each, state what I did in the design or analysis to reduce it, if anything.
5. Write the limitations paragraph (180–250 words) in ranked order, honest but not self-defeating.
6. Separately, list 2 limitations I should acknowledge in the viva even if they are not in the thesis text.
Rules
Be specific to my study, because generic limitations suggest you haven't understood your own design. Don't invent limitations my design doesn't have, and don't hide real ones behind softer wording. Keep numbers exactly as I gave them.
Output
A ranked limitation table (limitation, type, direction of bias, mitigation, impact), then the paragraph, then the viva list.
Tweak
End with a one-line statement of how confident a reader should be in my primary conclusion, given these limitations.

How to get more out of it

  • Start with the limitation that matters most, not the most comfortable one.
  • "Single-centre" is only a limitation if you explain why your centre might differ from others.
  • Pair each limitation with what the next study should do differently. It leads naturally into future research.
  • → NEXT #268 Hostile-reviewer attack on my discussion
268Hostile-reviewer attack on my discussion
Master
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen your discussion is drafted, before your guide or a journal sees it.
WhyThe kindest thing you can do for your discussion is let someone attack it first. A structured hostile review finds overclaims, missing comparisons and weak logic, and it hands you defensive rewrites you can use on paper and at the viva.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are Reviewer 2: a senior, sceptical peer reviewer for a high-impact clinical journal. You are fair but relentless, and you target overinterpretation, selective comparisons and conclusions that outrun the data.
My context
Design, sample size and primary result: [brief with numbers]
Target: [thesis / journal name and its readership]
My results section: [PASTE RESULTS]
My discussion: [PASTE DISCUSSION]
Task
1. Write a reviewer report on the discussion with major comments (threats to validity or interpretation) and minor comments (wording, clarity, missing references), each quoting the text it refers to.
2. Identify every sentence that claims more than the results show, and every causal claim unsupported by the design.
3. Identify comparisons with literature that seem selective, where only agreeing studies are cited, and say what kind of study is missing.
4. Identify results reported in the discussion that do not appear in the results.
5. Identify important limitations the discussion leaves out or understates.
6. For each major comment, write a defensive rewrite of the relevant text and a one-line response I could use in a reply to reviewers or at the viva.
Rules
Quote the exact text for every criticism, because unquoted criticism cannot be acted on. Rewrites must not add new data or references; use [CITE] slots where a reference is needed. Keep my numbers unchanged.
Output
The reviewer report (major, then minor), then a table (quoted sentence, problem, rewrite, one-line response).
Tweak
End with an overall verdict (accept, minor revision, major revision) with the single change that would most improve the discussion.

How to get more out of it

  • Run this before your guide reads the draft. Their time is better spent on judgement than on catching overclaims.
  • Keep the responses. They are ready-made answers for viva questions about your interpretation.
  • Run it again after revising. The major comments should turn into minor ones.
  • → NEXT #278 Scan my discussion for overclaiming
269Conclusion, recommendations and summary chapter
Master
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itFor the final chapters of your thesis: summary, conclusion and recommendations.
WhyMany Indian theses end with separate summary, conclusion and recommendations chapters, and examiners read them first when time is short. They must match the objectives exactly and claim no more than the data shows.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a senior examiner for MD/MS theses who reads the summary and conclusion first to judge whether the student understood their study. You insist that every conclusion maps to an objective and every recommendation is proportionate to the evidence.
My context
Thesis title: [paste]
Objectives (numbered): [PASTE OBJECTIVES]
Key results for each objective (with numbers): [PASTE RESULTS]
Main limitations: [list]
My university's required end chapters and word limits: [e.g. Summary 500 words, Conclusion, Recommendations]
Design and setting: [brief]
Task
1. Write the summary chapter: background (2 lines), aims, methods (design, setting, sample, key measurements), key results by objective with numbers, and the conclusion, within the word limit.
2. Write the conclusion: one numbered statement per objective, each answering it directly and in associational terms where appropriate.
3. Write recommendations in 3 groups: for clinical practice (only if justified), for the department or health system, and for future research, each specific and feasible.
4. Build a check table showing each objective, its conclusion statement and the result that supports it.
5. Flag any result that is important but not captured by any conclusion.
Rules
Every conclusion must answer an objective using a result I gave, because conclusions without matching results are a classic examiner criticism. Recommendations for practice must be proportionate: a single-centre observational study can suggest or support, not establish. Keep numbers exactly as supplied.
Output
The summary, the numbered conclusions, the grouped recommendations, then the check table.
Tweak
End with the one conclusion statement most likely to be challenged at the viva, and a safer wording for it.

How to get more out of it

  • Read your conclusion and your objectives side by side. They should mirror each other.
  • Future-research recommendations should name the design, population and outcome.
  • Keep the summary free of any number that doesn't appear in your results chapter.

270The first paragraph of the discussion

1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itTo open your discussion.
Persona
You are a journal editor who wants the discussion to open with the answer, not with background.
My context
Primary objective: [paste]
Primary result with numbers: [paste]
Most important secondary result: [paste]
Design: [brief]
Task
1. Write the opening paragraph (60–100 words) stating the answer to the research question with the key numbers.
2. Add one sentence on the most important secondary finding.
3. Give an alternative version for a journal article of 50 words or fewer.
Rules
No literature, no background and no interpretation in this paragraph, and use associational language for observational designs, because the first sentence is read as your main claim.

Pro tipIf you can't state your main finding in one sentence, your results section needs work first.

271Compare one finding with the literature
Standard
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itFor each key comparison paragraph in your discussion.
Persona
You are a clinical academic who writes comparison paragraphs that interpret rather than list.
My context
My finding with numbers: [paste]
Comparable studies I have read, with design, setting, population and result: [PASTE DETAILS OR UPLOAD PAPERS]
Task
1. Compare my finding with each study: agree, partly agree or differ.
2. For differences, give the most likely explanations (population, setting, definitions, measurement, timing, sample size).
3. Write a paragraph of 120–180 words that compares and interprets, ending with what the comparison means.
Rules
Use only the studies I supplied, citing each with its reference number, because comparisons with unseen or invented studies are academic misconduct. Present disagreeing studies too, not only supporting ones.
Output
A comparison table, then the paragraph.
Pro tipOne paragraph per key finding is a good discipline. Don't mix three findings in one paragraph.
Try it withMy finding: prevalence of sarcopenia 38% in COPD; three studies reported 15%, 25% and 40%.
→ NEXT #272 Why my result differs from previous studies
272Why my result differs from previous studies
Standard
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itWhen your result disagrees with published findings and you must explain it.
Persona
You are an epidemiologist skilled at explaining discrepancies between studies without special pleading.
My context
My study: design, population, setting, definitions, sample size and result: [brief]
The study or studies I differ from, with the same details: [brief]
Task
1. Compare the studies systematically on each methodological dimension.
2. Rank the plausible explanations for the difference.
3. Consider chance (sample size, CI overlap) explicitly.
4. Write a discussion paragraph presenting the difference and the most likely explanations, without dismissing either result.
Rules
Don't claim my study is "better" without a specific methodological reason, and consider that my own study might be the outlier, because examiners respect balanced reasoning.
Output
The comparison table, the ranked explanations, then the paragraph.
Pro tipOverlapping confidence intervals often mean the "difference" isn't really a difference. Check that first.
Try it withMy study found no association between vitamin D and TB outcome; two earlier studies found one.

273The mechanism paragraph, clearly labelled as speculation
Standard
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident

Persona
You are a clinician-scientist who explains mechanisms carefully and labels speculation clearly.
My context

Relevant physiology or mechanisms I know of, with sources: [PASTE NOTES WITH CITATIONS]
Task
1. Suggest the 2–3 most plausible mechanisms for my finding.
2. For each, say how strong the supporting evidence is (established, plausible, speculative).
3. Write a paragraph of 100–150 words presenting them with hedged language.
4. Suggest what study could test the leading mechanism.
Rules
Mark every mechanistic claim needing support as [CITE], and phrase speculation as speculation ("may", "could", "one possible explanation"), because mechanisms presented as fact are a common overreach.
Output
The mechanism list with evidence strength, then the paragraph, then the testing suggestion.
Pro tipKeep this paragraph short. Examiners value an honest "the mechanism is unclear" over a confident guess.

274Clinical implications without overreach
Standard
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itFor the implications paragraph of your discussion.
Persona
You are a guideline-panel member who judges how far a single study can change practice.
My context
Main findings: [paste]
Design, sample size and setting: [brief]
Who could use the findings (clinicians, administrators, programmes): [list]
Task
1. State what the findings could mean for practice, and for whom.
2. Distinguish what can be acted on now, what needs confirmation, and what is hypothesis-generating.
3. Write the implications paragraph (100–150 words).
4. Flag any implication that overreaches the design.
Rules
Use proportionate verbs (suggests, supports, may inform) for single-centre observational results, because "should be adopted" claims are rarely justified by one study.

275Strengths without inflation
Standard
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itFor the strengths paragraph of your discussion.
Persona
You are a sceptical reviewer who dislikes inflated strength statements.
My context
Design features: [e.g. prospective, consecutive recruitment, blinded outcome assessment, validated tools, low loss to follow-up]
Anything unique about my setting or population: [brief]
Task
1. Identify genuine methodological strengths and explain why each matters for validity.
2. Remove claims that are not real strengths (e.g. "first study in India" unless verified, or "large sample" when it isn't).
3. Write the strengths paragraph (80–120 words).
Rules
Tie each strength to a bias it reduces, because a strength without a reason is just a claim. Do not claim novelty unless I confirm it has been checked; write [VERIFY NOVELTY] instead.
Output
A strengths table (feature, bias reduced), the removed claims, then the paragraph.
Pro tip"Prospective, consecutive recruitment with complete follow-up" is a real strength. Say it plainly.
Try it withA prospective cohort of 180 patients with 97% follow-up and blinded outcome assessment.

Task
1. Check my conclusion against the result and design, and list every overreach.
2. Rewrite it in 2–3 sentences: what was found, in whom, and what it suggests.
3. Write a one-sentence version for the abstract.

Match the strength of language to the design and precision of the result, because the conclusion is the most-quoted sentence of any study.

278Scan my discussion for overclaiming
Standard
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itAs a final check on any discussion or conclusion.
Persona
You are a research-integrity editor who scans manuscripts for spin.
My context
My design: [design]
My primary result: [estimate, CI, p]
My discussion text: [PASTE DISCUSSION]
Task
1. Flag causal language used with an observational design.
2. Flag spin: "trend towards", "marginally significant", emphasis on secondary results over a null primary result, and extrapolation beyond the population.
3. Flag unqualified "significant".
4. Give a corrected version of each flagged sentence.
Rules
Keep the meaning where it is justified and change only the overreaching wording, because the goal is accuracy, not timidity.
Output
A table (sentence, type of spin, corrected sentence), then a count by type.
Pro tipSearch your text for "caus", "effect", "impact", "prevent", "led to", "due to". Most overclaims hide there.
Try it withA discussion claiming a supplement "improved outcomes" based on a before-after study.

279Who do my results apply to?
Standard
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itFor the generalisability part of your limitations or implications.
Persona
You are an epidemiologist who thinks carefully about external validity.
My context
Setting and how patients reached my hospital: [brief]
Population characteristics (severity, age, comorbidity, socio-economic profile): [brief]
Exclusions: [list]
Proportion of screened patients who were enrolled: [numbers]
Task
1. Describe the population my results apply to most directly.
2. Identify populations to whom they may not apply, and why.
3. Discuss referral bias for a tertiary centre, if relevant, and the likely direction of its effect on my estimates.
4. Write a generalisability paragraph (80–120 words).
Rules
Base the assessment on my actual setting and exclusions, because generic statements about "single-centre studies" add nothing.
Output
Headed notes, then the paragraph.
Pro tipTertiary-centre patients are usually sicker. Say whether that makes your estimates higher or lower than in district practice.

280Discuss a surprising result
Standard
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itWhen a result contradicts your hypothesis or expectations.
Persona
You are a thoughtful senior researcher who treats surprising results as information, not embarrassment.
My context
What I expected, and why: [brief]
What I found, with numbers: [paste]
Possible issues with my data or methods: [brief]
Task
1. List the explanations in order: chance, bias, confounding, measurement error, and a genuine effect.
2. Evaluate each against my study details.
3. Suggest a check I can still run (e.g. a sensitivity analysis) if appropriate.
4. Write a paragraph (100–150 words) presenting the finding honestly.
Rules
Don't explain away the finding, and don't overstate it, because examiners respect a balanced treatment of surprises.
Output
The explanations table, the check, then the paragraph.
Pro tipSurprising findings often make the best future-research questions. Say so.
Try it withExpected obesity to worsen ARDS outcomes; found lower mortality in obese patients.

281Discuss a non-significant primary result
Standard
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itWhen your primary outcome was not statistically significant.
Persona
You are a statistician-editor who helps authors write honest, publishable null-result discussions.
My context
Primary result (estimate, 95% CI, p): [paste]
Sample size and whether it met the calculated target: [brief]
Expected effect used for the sample size: [brief]
Task
1. Interpret the CI: what effect sizes are ruled out, and what remain plausible.
2. Discuss power: whether the study could have detected a clinically important effect.
3. Compare with the literature honestly.
4. Write the opening paragraph of the discussion for this result.
Rules
Don't call the result a trend or imply a hidden effect, and don't call it proof of no effect, because both misrepresent a null result.
Output
The CI interpretation, the power note, then the paragraph.
Pro tip"Our study was unable to demonstrate…" is better than "there was no difference".
Try it withRR 0.85 (95% CI 0.62–1.17), p = 0.32, sample of 180 against a target of 200.
→ NEXT #266 Write the discussion: ten paragraphs, no overclaiming
282Check the structure of my discussion
Standard
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itWhen your discussion is drafted and feels disorganised.
Persona
You are a thesis-writing coach who maps discussions against a standard structure.
My context
My discussion: [PASTE DISCUSSION]
My primary result: [paste]
Target structure, if my guide or journal prescribes one: [brief]
Task
1. Map each paragraph to a structural element: principal findings, comparison, explanation, mechanism, implications, strengths, limitations, future research or conclusion.
2. Identify missing, repeated or misplaced elements.
3. Suggest a reordering.
4. Check that the first paragraph states the principal finding and the last states a proportionate conclusion.
Rules
Suggest structural changes only, keeping my text as it is, because the content and voice must remain mine.
Output
A paragraph map table, the problems, then the suggested order.
Pro tipIf your discussion opens with background, move it. The first paragraph must answer the question.
Try it withA discussion that opens with 2 paragraphs of epidemiology before mentioning the results.

1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident

My context

Word or page limit: [e.g. 500 words]
Task
1. Write the summary within the limit, with short paragraphs for background, aims, methods, results and conclusion.

285Cut my discussion to the word limit

You are a journal editor who cuts discussions without losing substance.

Task
1. Identify repetition of results, background that belongs in the introduction, over-long literature summaries and redundant limitations.
2. Produce the trimmed discussion at or under the target.

Rules

286Practical recommendations from my thesis
Standard
1.9 Discussion, conclusion & limitations · PG Resident
Use itWhen your thesis format requires a recommendations section.
Persona
You are a health-policy adviser who writes recommendations that are proportionate to the evidence.
My context
Key findings: [brief]
Design strength and limitations: [brief]
Stakeholders: [clinicians, hospital, programme, researchers]
My university's format for this section, if any: [brief]
Task
1. Write 4–6 recommendations grouped by stakeholder.
2. Grade each by the strength of support from my study (strong, moderate or preliminary).
3. Make each specific and feasible, with who should act, how, and what it would cost in effort.
Rules
Base practice recommendations only on results my design can support, and phrase the rest as research recommendations, because examiners question recommendations that outrun the data.
Output
The grouped, graded recommendations.
Pro tipOne specific, feasible recommendation is worth more than five generic ones.

296Complete study protocol, IEC-ready
Master
1.10 Protocol, ethics & synopsis · PG Resident
Use itBefore ethics committee submission and before university synopsis deadlines.
WhyThe protocol is the document you will be held to for the next three years. Ambiguity here becomes an unanswerable question at your viva. This prompt makes you decide everything in advance, in the format Indian IECs expect.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a clinical trials methodologist and ethics committee member who has reviewed several hundred postgraduate protocols. You know exactly which sections get sent back for revision and why. You write in formal scientific register suitable for institutional submission.
My context
Title: [working title]
Research question (PICOT): [paste]
Design: [prospective observational cohort / single-arm interventional / randomised controlled / diagnostic accuracy / cross-sectional]
Setting: [hospital, unit, catchment]
Duration approved for the study: [X months]
Expected eligible patients per month: [N]
Primary outcome and how it is measured: [state]
Secondary outcomes: [list]
Intervention or exposure, fully described (dose, timing, device, operator): [describe]
Comparator: [describe]
Task
Draft a complete protocol in this order, to a standard that would pass institutional ethics review at an Indian medical college:
1. Title-page fields for institutional submission.
2. Structured synopsis of 300 words.
3. Introduction and rationale ending in an explicit gap statement, with [CITE] slots.
4. Aims and objectives: one primary, numbered secondaries, each measurable and time-bound.
5. Null and alternative hypotheses.
6. Methodology: design and justification; setting and period; source population; operationally defined inclusion criteria; exclusion criteria with reasons; sampling and how it will be implemented; sample size (formula, every term, inputs marked [INSERT FROM PUBLISHED SOURCE — VERIFY], attrition); randomisation, concealment and blinding if applicable; the study procedure as a patient journey; operational definitions of every variable; data collection tools and who collects what; quality control and inter-observer agreement.
7. Statistical analysis plan in outline, with tests named against objectives.
8. Ethical considerations: consent (and assent), confidentiality and data storage, risks and mitigation, benefits, adverse-event provisions, compensation, conflicts of interest, and withdrawal.
9. Informed consent document in plain language (about eighth-grade reading level), suitable for translation.
10. A month-by-month Gantt-style timeline from ethics approval to thesis submission.
11. A budget with realistic line items.
12. CTRI registration requirements: what, when, and which fields will be difficult.
13. Limitations to acknowledge up front.
14. A numbered placeholder reference list.
Rules
Use [CITE] and [VERIFY] tags instead of any invented reference, prevalence figure, effect size or guideline statement, because the IEC checks them. Flag every section where an ethics committee is likely to raise a query, and say how to phrase it to avoid one. State methodological weaknesses plainly rather than writing around them. Formal scientific language throughout.
Output
The full protocol with numbered headings, then a separate list of likely IEC queries with pre-emptive fixes.
Tweak
Finish with a follow-up I should run: "Act as the ethics committee reviewer and list every objection you would raise to this protocol."

How to get more out of it

  • The operational-definitions section is where most protocols are weak. Don't shorten it.
  • Get the sample size confirmed by a statistician. AI sample-size output is a starting draft, never the final number.
  • Keep one master version with a version number and date. Every committee copy must match it.
  • → NEXT #297 IEC submission pack and pre-review
297IEC submission pack and pre-review
Master
1.10 Protocol, ethics & synopsis · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen your protocol is final and you are preparing the ethics committee submission.
WhyIEC rejections and revision cycles cost months. Most queries are predictable: consent wording, risk-benefit, vulnerable participants, data protection, compensation and inconsistency between documents. This prompt reviews your pack the way the committee will.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are the member-secretary of an institutional ethics committee at an Indian medical college, experienced with the ICMR National Ethical Guidelines and the committee's usual queries. You review submissions strictly but constructively.
My context
Study type and risk level (in my view): [observational / minimal risk / greater than minimal risk]
Participants, including any vulnerable groups: [brief]
Protocol summary: [PASTE SUMMARY]
Consent documents (English version): [PASTE PIS AND CONSENT FORM]
Data handling and storage plan: [brief]
Funding and conflicts of interest: [brief]
Documents I plan to submit: [list]
Task
1. Classify the likely review category (exempt, expedited or full committee) with reasoning, marked [CONFIRM WITH YOUR IEC].
2. Build a submission checklist of documents Indian IECs commonly require (application form, protocol, PIS and consent in English and local language, CRF, investigator CVs and GCP certificates, undertakings, budget, CTRI plan) and mark what I am missing.
3. Review the consent documents for completeness (purpose, procedures, duration, risks, benefits, alternatives, confidentiality, voluntariness, withdrawal, compensation, contacts) and readability.
4. Review the risk-benefit assessment and the adverse-event plan.
5. Check consistency of title, objectives, sample size and procedures across all documents.
6. Write the 10 queries this committee is most likely to raise, each with a model response and the document change that resolves it.
Rules
Refer to the current ICMR National Ethical Guidelines and mark every specific rule you cite as [VERIFY IN ICMR GUIDELINES], because committees check citations against the source. Don't guarantee approval or classification; the committee decides.
Output
The category note, the checklist table (document, required, status), the consent review, the risk-benefit note, the consistency table, then the 10 queries with responses.
Tweak
End with the 3 changes that would most reduce the chance of a revision cycle.

How to get more out of it

  • Mismatched titles and sample sizes between documents are the commonest avoidable query.
  • Get the local-language consent form translated and back-translated. Committees check this.
  • Keep your GCP training certificate current. Many IECs require it from all investigators.
  • → NEXT #300 Respond to IEC queries
298Participant information sheet and consent form
Master
1.10 Protocol, ethics & synopsis · PG Resident
Use itWhile preparing your IEC submission.
WhyA consent form written in dense medical English is not informed consent. Committees reject forms that are too technical, incomplete or inconsistent with the protocol. A plain-language, complete, translation-ready document gets approved and actually informs patients.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a research-ethics specialist who writes participant information sheets and consent forms for Indian hospital studies. You write at about an eighth-grade reading level, in short sentences, with no jargon.
My context
Study title (formal and a lay version): [paste]
What participants will experience, step by step: [paste]
Time required and number of visits: [brief]
Samples, tests or procedures, including any extra blood volume: [brief]
Risks and discomforts, and benefits (direct and indirect): [brief]
Alternatives to participation: [brief]
Data confidentiality arrangements: [brief]
Compensation for participation or injury: [brief]
Investigator and IEC contact details: [placeholders]
Participants who need a legally acceptable representative, or assent (children): [brief]
Local language(s) needed: [e.g. Hindi, Marathi]
Task
1. Write the participant information sheet under simple headings: why this study, what will happen, how long, risks, benefits, alternatives, privacy, your rights, compensation, and who to contact.
2. Write the consent form with the standard statements to initial, plus signature, thumb-impression and witness sections, and an impartial-witness provision for participants who cannot read.
3. If relevant, write an LAR consent version and a short assent form for children.
4. Check readability: flag any sentence over 20 words and any medical term, with a simpler version.
5. Give translation and back-translation instructions for the local language versions.
Rules
Use only the procedures, risks and arrangements I described, because anything added or omitted makes the form inconsistent with the protocol. Mark every regulatory requirement you mention as [VERIFY IN ICMR GUIDELINES]. Never promise benefits or confidentiality beyond what the protocol provides.
Output
The information sheet, the consent form, the LAR and assent versions if needed, the readability flags table, then the translation instructions.
Tweak
End with the 3 questions a patient is most likely to ask when you take consent, with simple answers.

How to get more out of it

  • Read the form aloud to a non-medical relative. Every place they frown needs rewriting.
  • Say "you may stop at any time without affecting your treatment" in every language version.
  • Keep a signed copy for the participant. Many IECs require this.

299The ethics section of my protocol or thesis
Standard
1.10 Protocol, ethics & synopsis · PG Resident
Use itWhen writing the ethical considerations section.
Persona
You are an ethics committee member at an Indian government medical college who writes clear, compliant ethics sections.
My context
I am conducting a [STUDY DESIGN] study on [TOPIC] at a government medical college in India. My participants are [e.g. adult COPD patients]. Data collection involves [e.g. blood tests, interviews, chart review, imaging].
Task
Write the ethics section covering:
1. The type of informed consent required and how it will be obtained.
2. The data confidentiality and anonymisation plan.
3. The risk-benefit analysis for participants.
4. Vulnerable populations, if applicable.
5. Compliance with the ICMR National Ethical Guidelines.
6. A statement on conflicts of interest.
Rules
Write in formal academic language suitable for IEC submission, and mark the guideline edition and any specific provision as [VERIFY CURRENT ICMR EDITION], because guideline editions are updated and committees check them.
Output
Six headed paragraphs, about 300 words in total.
Pro tipMirror the committee's own application-form headings where you can. It makes the reviewer's job easier.
Try it withCross-sectional study using blood tests and questionnaires in adults with type 2 diabetes.

300Respond to IEC queries
Standard
1.10 Protocol, ethics & synopsis · PG Resident
Use itWhen the ethics committee returns your submission with queries.
Persona
You are an experienced investigator who writes clear, respectful, point-by-point responses to ethics committees.
My context
The committee's queries, verbatim: [PASTE QUERIES]
Relevant sections of my protocol and consent form: [PASTE SECTIONS]
Task
1. Draft a point-by-point response letter: each query quoted, then my response, then the exact change made with page and section.
2. Suggest the revised protocol or consent text for each change.
3. Flag any query I should discuss with my guide before responding.
4. Draft the covering letter.
Rules
Address every query fully, and never argue with a query without a clear, respectful reason, because unresolved queries delay approval. Propose only changes consistent with my study.
Output
The response table (query, response, change, location), the revised text, the flags, then the covering letter.
Pro tipHighlight all changes in the revised documents and submit a clean copy as well.
Try it withQueries on consent for illiterate patients, blood volume per visit, and data storage.

1.10 Protocol, ethics & synopsis · PG Resident / Faculty

You are a research coordinator who has registered many studies on CTRI.
My context
Study type: [interventional / observational]
Protocol summary: [PASTE SUMMARY]

Sites and investigators: [brief]
Task

303Month-by-month thesis timeline
Standard
1.10 Protocol, ethics & synopsis · PG Resident
Use itFor your protocol, synopsis and personal planning.
Persona
You are a thesis project manager who builds realistic timelines with buffers.
My context
Course start and thesis submission deadline: [dates]
Expected IEC approval date: [date]
Recruitment rate and target: [N per month, total]
Follow-up duration: [months]
Known busy periods (exams, rotations, postings): [list]
Task
1. Build a month-by-month timeline: approvals, registration, pilot, recruitment, follow-up, data entry, analysis, writing each chapter, guide review, corrections, binding and submission.
2. Add buffers where delays are likely.
3. Calculate whether recruitment fits, and flag it if it doesn't.
4. Present it as a Gantt-style table (tasks against months).
Rules
Build in at least one month of buffer before submission, and say if my deadline is unrealistic, because optimistic timelines are the commonest reason for late theses.
Output

Pro tipStart writing the introduction and ROL during recruitment. Waiting until data collection ends is the classic mistake.

1.10 Protocol, ethics & synopsis · PG Resident / Faculty

You are a research finance officer who builds realistic budgets for investigator-initiated studies.
My context

Sample size: [N]

3. Add a contingency line.

305Risk-benefit assessment and adverse-event plan
Standard
1.10 Protocol, ethics & synopsis · PG Resident
Use itWhen your study involves any procedure or intervention beyond routine care.
Persona
You are a clinical research safety officer who writes proportionate risk-benefit and adverse-event sections.
My context
Study procedures beyond routine care: [list]
Participant population: [brief]
Intervention, if any, and its known side effects: [brief]
Task
1. List the risks of each study-specific procedure, with likelihood and severity.
2. Describe mitigation for each.
3. Describe direct and indirect benefits realistically.
4. Write the adverse-event plan: definitions, recording, grading, reporting timelines to the IEC, and management.
5. Summarise the overall risk category with justification.
Rules
Mark reporting timelines and definitions as [VERIFY IN ICMR / IEC SOP], because reporting requirements differ by study type and institution. Don't list routine-care risks as study risks.
Output
A risk table, the benefits paragraph, the AE plan, then the summary.
Pro tipIf a procedure is part of routine care anyway, say so clearly. It lowers the perceived risk accurately.

306Confidentiality and data-protection plan
Standard
1.10 Protocol, ethics & synopsis · PG Resident
Use itFor the confidentiality section of your IEC submission and protocol.
Persona
You are a health-data governance adviser familiar with Indian research-ethics requirements and data-protection principles.
My context
Data collected (identifiable and study data): [list]
Where and how it will be stored: [brief]
Who has access: [brief]

Task
1. Describe de-identification: study IDs, a separate linking log, and restricted access.

Output
A 4-part plan, then the paragraph.
Pro tipKeep the linking log (study ID → patient identity) on a different device from your study data.

307Request a waiver of consent
Standard
1.10 Protocol, ethics & synopsis · PG Resident
Use itFor retrospective record-based studies or other minimal-risk research where consent is impracticable.
Persona
You are an IEC member who reviews waiver requests and knows what justifications committees accept.
My context
Study design and data source: [brief]
Why obtaining consent is impracticable: [brief]
Risk to participants: [brief]
Confidentiality measures: [brief]
Task
1. Assess whether my study plausibly qualifies for a waiver, with reasoning.
2. Write the waiver request: study description, justification of impracticability, minimal-risk argument, confidentiality safeguards, and how rights and welfare are protected.
3. List conditions the committee may impose.
Rules
Present the request honestly and mark the criteria you cite as [VERIFY IN ICMR GUIDELINES], because the committee alone decides eligibility for a waiver.
Output
The assessment, the request text, then the likely conditions.
Pro tipCommittees are far more comfortable granting waivers when data are anonymised at extraction.
Try it withRetrospective review of 5 years of ICU records on ventilator-associated pneumonia.

308Protocol amendment request to the IEC
Standard
1.10 Protocol, ethics & synopsis · PG Resident
Use itWhen you need to change the approved protocol (sample size, criteria, outcomes, procedures).
Persona
You are an experienced investigator who writes clear, well-justified amendment requests.
My context
Current approved protocol version and date: [version]
Proposed changes and reasons: [list]
Effect on participants, risks and consent: [brief]
Task
1. Classify each change as minor or major, with reasoning, marked [CONFIRM WITH IEC SOP].
2. Write the amendment request: a table of old text, new text and justification.
3. Identify documents that must be updated (protocol, consent form, CRF, CTRI record).
4. Note whether already-enrolled participants need re-consent.
Rules
Don't implement any change before approval unless it is needed to eliminate an immediate hazard, and say so, because unapproved changes are protocol violations.
Output
The classification, the amendment table, the documents list, then the re-consent note.
Pro tipUpdate your CTRI record after approval. Journals compare the registry with the paper.
Try it withIncreasing the sample size from 100 to 130 and adding a secondary outcome.

309Funding and conflict-of-interest declarations
Standard

My context

2. Draft the declarations for the protocol, IEC form, thesis and future manuscript.
3. Recommend safeguards if a sponsor provides materials (e.g. no sponsor role in analysis or publication decisions).
Rules

316Write the full manuscript for journal submission
Master
1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen the thesis is done and you are converting it into a paper you can submit.
WhyA thesis and a paper are different documents. This prompt handles the conversion, reporting-guideline compliance and the submission package in one structured pass, using only your real numbers.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a senior academic author and manuscript editor who has published extensively in clinical journals and has served as a peer reviewer. You know reporting guidelines thoroughly and you write in clear, economical scientific English for an international readership.
My context
Design: [state] · Current full title: [paste]
Setting and period: [state]
Sample size and participant flow: [screened, eligible, enrolled, completed, analysed]
Methods summary: [paste from protocol]
All results with exact numbers, effect estimates and CIs: [paste]
Tables and figures I already have: [list with titles]
Target journal: [name] · Word, abstract, reference, table and figure limits: [paste from author guidelines]
Reporting guideline that applies: [CONSORT / STROBE / STARD / PRISMA / CARE / SQUIRE / other]
Task
1. Journal fit: assess scope, design and novelty; if it is a poor fit, say so and suggest what type of journal fits better. Do not quote impact factors or acceptance rates from memory.
2. Title: five options (descriptive, finding-stating, PICO-style, short, conservative), with the best one for this journal.
3. Structured abstract within the limit, with my actual numbers.
4. Six keywords, preferring MeSH terms.
5. Introduction: three paragraphs (known, unknown and why it matters, objective), with [CITE] slots.
6. Methods: past tense, replicable, following my reporting guideline, noting which checklist item each subsection satisfies, with ethics, consent and registration placeholders.
7. Results: past tense, no interpretation; flow, baseline (Table 1), primary, then secondary outcomes, each with its effect estimate and CI.
8. Discussion: principal finding, comparison, explanation, implications, strengths, ranked limitations, future research, conclusion.
9. Conclusion, funding, conflicts of interest, CRediT author contributions, acknowledgements and data-availability statement.
10. A reporting-guideline checklist table, flagging unaddressed items.
11. A cover letter under 300 words.
12. A pre-submission critical review: major and minor comments in journal format, and which to fix before submitting.
Rules
Every citation slot stays [CITE] unless I uploaded the paper or you verified it with a PMID or DOI, because fabricated references end in retraction. Don't alter, round differently or recompute any number I give you. Add no findings, comparisons or claims beyond my data. Flag any claim my design cannot support. Note that AI cannot be an author and remind me to check the journal's AI-disclosure policy.
Output
Twelve numbered parts, with the manuscript sections in submission order.
Tweak
If time is short, do step 12 first and tell me the 3 fixes that most improve my chance of acceptance.

How to get more out of it

  • Do step 12 first if you are short on time. A pre-submission critical review saves months of revision.
  • Paste the actual author guidelines. Generic manuscript advice produces desk rejections.
  • Convert thesis to paper by cutting, not rewriting. Most theses contain two papers hiding inside one document.
  • → NEXT #317 Thesis → paper cut plan (and the second paper inside it)
317Thesis → paper cut plan (and the second paper inside it)
Master
1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident
Use itRight after thesis submission, while the details are fresh.
WhyTheses are long, comprehensive and repetitive; papers are short and focused. Most residents never publish because they try to shrink the whole thesis. This prompt finds the one or two papers inside it and tells you exactly what to cut.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a senior clinical researcher who has turned dozens of MD/MS theses into published papers. You think in terms of "one paper, one message" and you cut ruthlessly.
My context
Thesis title and design: [paste]
Objectives and key results for each: [PASTE OBJECTIVES AND RESULTS]
Chapter word counts: [introduction, ROL, methods, results, discussion]
Number of tables and figures in the thesis: [numbers]
Target journal type and its word limit: [e.g. national indexed journal, 3,000 words]
Co-authors and who will lead each paper: [brief]
Task
1. Identify the main paper (primary objective) and any second paper (a distinct secondary objective, a substudy or a methodological finding), each with a one-line message.
2. For the main paper, map what to keep, condense or drop from each thesis chapter, with target word counts.
3. Select the 3–5 tables or figures that carry the main message.
4. Explain what the ROL becomes (usually two sentences in the introduction and comparisons in the discussion).
5. Draft the paper's single-sentence message and a working title.
6. Flag duplicate-publication risks if two papers share data, and how to avoid salami slicing.
7. List any additional analysis the paper needs that the thesis didn't include, marked [NEW ANALYSIS — CONFIRM WITH GUIDE].
Rules
Each proposed paper must stand on its own question, because splitting one result into several papers is salami publication. Use only results I gave; don't invent new analyses.
Output
A papers table (paper, question, message, key tables), then a chapter-by-chapter cut plan with word targets, then the salami-slicing check.
Tweak
End with a 4-week writing schedule to get the main paper to submission.

How to get more out of it

  • Write the paper's abstract first. If it doesn't hold together, the paper won't either.
  • Keep the thesis methods chapter open. You'll compress it, not rewrite it.
  • Discuss authorship with your guide before you start writing, not after.

318Point-by-point response to peer reviewers
Master
1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen you receive a "major" or "minor revision" decision.
WhyA revision is an invitation, not a rejection. Most revisions are accepted when every comment is answered respectfully and specifically, with changes clearly located. Defensive or vague responses are how revisions become rejections.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a senior author who has responded to hundreds of peer reviews and sits on editorial boards. You are gracious, precise and firm where the science warrants it.
My context
Editor's decision letter: [PASTE LETTER]
Reviewer comments, verbatim: [PASTE ALL COMMENTS]
My manuscript (relevant sections): [PASTE OR UPLOAD]
Comments I disagree with, and why: [brief]
Revision deadline: [date]
Analyses or data I can realistically add in that time: [brief]
Task
1. Number every comment and classify it: requires new analysis, requires text change, requires clarification, or respectful disagreement.
2. For each, draft a response: thank the reviewer where appropriate, answer the point directly, and state exactly what changed and where (page and line).
3. Draft the revised manuscript text for each change.
4. For disagreements, write a courteous, evidence-based rebuttal, and suggest a partial concession where reasonable, such as adding a limitation sentence.
5. Flag comments that need new data or analysis I may not have, and how to handle each honestly (e.g. acknowledging it as a limitation).
6. Draft the cover letter to the editor summarising the main changes.
Rules
Answer every comment, including the minor ones, because an unanswered comment is the commonest reason for a second revision round. Don't promise analyses I cannot do, and don't invent new results or references; use [CITE] or [NEW ANALYSIS NEEDED] slots.
Output
A response table (comment number, verbatim comment, response, change and location), the revised text blocks, then the cover letter.
Tweak
End with the 2 responses most likely to decide acceptance, and whether they are strong enough.

How to get more out of it

  • Reply in a table. Editors find it far easier to check.
  • Highlight changes in a tracked or coloured version of the manuscript, and submit a clean copy too.
  • If a reviewer misunderstood something, the text was probably unclear. Fix the text, not just the reply.
  • → NEXT #324 Cover letter to the editor
319Pre-submission peer review of my manuscript
Master
1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itOne week before submission, when you think the manuscript is finished.
WhyThe fastest way to get published is to be your own harshest reviewer before the journal is. A structured pre-review, in journal format, catches the fatal flaws, the reporting gaps and the desk-rejection triggers while you can still fix them.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are an experienced peer reviewer and associate editor for clinical journals. You write structured reviews with major and minor comments, and you think about desk-rejection risk as an editor would.
My context
Target journal and its scope: [name, brief scope]
Article type and word limit: [e.g. original article, 3,000 words]
Reporting guideline: [e.g. STROBE]
What I believe is the paper's main contribution: [one line]
My biggest worry about the paper: [one line]
My manuscript: [PASTE OR UPLOAD MANUSCRIPT]
Task
1. Give an editor's first-read verdict: desk reject, send for review, or likely to be sent back for formatting, with reasons based on the title, abstract and cover-letter logic.
2. Write major comments on the question, design, analysis, interpretation and novelty, quoting the text.
3. Write minor comments on clarity, tables and figures, references and language.
4. Run a reporting-guideline check and list the items that are missing, with where each should go.
5. Check compliance with the author guidelines (word counts, abstract structure, reference style, declarations, figure formats).
6. Prioritise all issues: fix before submission, or can wait.
7. Say whether my stated main contribution comes across clearly in the title, abstract and conclusion.
Rules
Quote the manuscript for every comment, because specific comments can be acted on and vague ones can't. Don't rewrite the paper; identify and prioritise. Don't invent references or data.
Output
The verdict, major comments, minor comments, the guideline check, the compliance check, then a priority list.
Tweak
End with the single weakness most likely to cause rejection, and the most efficient fix.

How to get more out of it

  • Ask a colleague outside your department to read the abstract and tell you the main finding. If they can't, rewrite it.
  • Fix every "before submission" item. Editors notice sloppy formatting within seconds.
  • Keep this review. Compare it with the real reviews when they arrive.

320Structured abstract within the word limit
Standard
1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident
Use itFor journal submission, conference abstracts and the thesis abstract.
Persona
You are a journal abstract editor who writes precise, complete structured abstracts.
My context
Target journal or conference and its abstract format (headings, word limit): [paste]
Design, setting and period: [brief]
Participants and sample size: [brief]
Primary outcome result with estimate, CI and p: [paste]
Key secondary results: [paste]
Conclusion: [paste]
Task
1. Write the abstract with the required headings, within the limit.
2. Include the design in the Methods, the primary result with numbers in the Results, and a proportionate Conclusion.
3. Give the word count.
4. Check that every number matches the full text.
Rules
Use only the numbers I gave, and make sure the conclusion follows from the primary result, because abstracts are read far more than papers and spin there misleads the most readers.
Output
The abstract, its word count, then the number check.
Pro tipMany readers only read the abstract. Make sure it states your primary result even if it was null.
Try it with250-word structured abstract for a prospective cohort of 210 patients on AKI after cardiac surgery.

321Five manuscript titles for my paper
Standard
1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident
Use itBefore submission, or when your title feels flat.
Persona
You are a journal editor who knows which titles get read and cited.
My context
Main finding: [one line]
Design and population: [brief]
Target journal and any title limit: [name, characters]
My current working title: [paste]
Task
1. Write 5 titles: descriptive, finding-stating, question-form, short and punchy, and design-explicit (e.g. "…: a prospective cohort study").
2. Check each for overclaiming, jargon, abbreviations and length.
3. Recommend one for my journal and explain why.
4. Suggest a running title.
Rules
Don't state causation in a title for an observational design, because editors flag it immediately.
Output
The 5 titles with character counts, the checks, the recommendation, then the running title.
Pro tipPut the most important words at the start. Many databases truncate long titles.
Try it withFinding: frailty predicts 90-day mortality after emergency laparotomy in older Indian adults.

322Choose the right journal (and avoid predatory ones)
Standard
1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itBefore writing the final version, so you write for a specific journal.
Persona
You are a publication adviser who matches manuscripts to journals and protects authors from predatory publishers.
My context
Main finding and design: [brief]
Specialty: [specialty]
Priorities: [indexing needed (e.g. PubMed, Scopus), speed, cost, open access, national or international readership]
Budget for article processing charges: [amount or "none"]
Task
1. Describe the types of journals that fit my paper (specialty or general, national or international).
2. Explain how to find candidate journals: the reference list of my own paper, journal-finder tools, and indexing databases.
3. Give a checklist for verifying legitimacy: indexing claims checked at the source, a real editorial board, transparent peer review and charges, DOAJ listing for open-access journals.
4. List red flags of predatory journals.
5. Help me build a shortlist table from the journals I find.
Rules
Don't recommend specific journals, impact factors or acceptance rates from memory; mark any journal named as [VERIFY INDEXING AND SCOPE], because journal metrics and indexing change and predatory journals mimic real ones.
Output
Journal types, finding methods, the legitimacy checklist, red flags, then a shortlist template.
Pro tipIf a journal emails you inviting a submission with rapid publication, treat it as a red flag until proven otherwise.
Try it withProspective observational study in pulmonology from an Indian medical college, no APC budget.

323Check my manuscript against the author guidelines
Standard
1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident
Use itJust before submission.
Persona
You are a journal's editorial assistant who checks submissions against the instructions for authors.
My context
The journal's author guidelines (relevant parts): [PASTE GUIDELINES]
My manuscript: [PASTE OR UPLOAD]
Task
1. Check article type, word counts (abstract and main text), heading structure, reference style and number, and table and figure limits and formats.
2. Check the required declarations: ethics, consent, registration, funding, conflicts of interest, data availability, AI disclosure and author contributions.
3. Check file requirements (title page, blinded manuscript, figures, checklist).
4. List every non-compliance with its fix.
Rules
Base every check on the guidelines I pasted, not on general assumptions, because journals differ in small details that cause technical returns.
Output
A compliance table (requirement, status, fix).
Pro tipTechnical returns cost weeks. Five minutes with this checklist saves them.

324Cover letter to the editor

1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident / Faculty

Title and article type: [paste]
Main question and finding: [brief]

Required declarations (original work, not under consideration elsewhere, ethics, conflicts): [brief]

325Keywords that help my paper get found

326Authorship order and CRediT contributions

1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident / Faculty

My context

2. Suggest an order based on contribution, with reasoning.

327Disclose AI use in my manuscript
Standard
1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen you used any AI tool while preparing the thesis or manuscript.
Persona
You are a publication-ethics editor familiar with ICMJE, COPE and journal policies on generative AI.
My context
AI tools used and for what (language editing, literature search, code, drafting): [list]
Target journal's AI policy (paste if available): [PASTE]
Task
1. Classify my uses by whether journals typically require disclosure.
2. Draft the disclosure statement in the journal's preferred location (methods, acknowledgements or a declaration).
3. Confirm the responsibilities that remain mine (accuracy, originality, citations).
4. Flag any use that may breach the journal's policy (e.g. AI-generated images or data).
Rules
Recommend full, honest disclosure of substantive AI use, and state that AI tools cannot be authors, because undisclosed AI use is increasingly treated as misconduct.
Output
The classification, the statement, the responsibilities, then the flags.
Pro tipKeep a simple log of which AI tool you used for what and when. It makes disclosure easy.
Try it withUsed Claude for language editing and ChatGPT for R code during the analysis.

329Compress my thesis methods for a journal

Task
1. Keep everything needed for replication and the reporting checklist.

330Three-paragraph journal introduction
Standard
1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident
Use itWhen converting your thesis introduction and ROL into a journal introduction.
Persona
You are a journal editor who expects introductions of about 400 words that lead straight to the question.
My context
Thesis introduction and gap statement: [PASTE]
Key references I will cite (with numbers): [list]
Objective: [one line]
Task
1. Write paragraph 1: the problem and why it matters, with burden figures from my sources.
2. Write paragraph 2: what is known and what is not, with the specific gap.
3. Write paragraph 3: the objective, stated precisely.
4. Keep it to about 350–450 words.
Rules
Use only my references and figures, keeping [CITE] slots where they are missing, because journal introductions are checked against the reference list.
Output
The three paragraphs with the word count.
Pro tipThe last sentence of the introduction should read like the question your abstract answers.
Try it withConverting a 2,500-word thesis introduction and ROL on dengue shock predictors.

Standard
1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident
Use itWhen your thesis discussion is far longer than the journal allows.
Persona

My context

Target word count: [e.g. 1,000]

Task

339Read and fix a similarity (plagiarism) report honestly
Standard
1.11 Thesis to manuscript & publication · PG Resident
Use itWhen your university or journal returns a Turnitin, iThenticate or similar report.
Persona
You are a research-integrity officer who helps authors interpret similarity reports and fix genuine problems.
My context
Overall similarity percentage and the top matching sources: [PASTE SUMMARY]
Highlighted passages with their matches: [PASTE PASSAGES]
My university's or journal's threshold, if known: [e.g. 10%]
Task
1. Separate acceptable matches (references, standard methods phrases, quotations with citation, my own registered protocol) from problematic ones (copied sentences without citation, close paraphrase).
2. For each problematic passage, explain the issue and how to rewrite it in my own words with the correct citation.
3. Advise how to report acceptable overlap, e.g. with my own thesis or preprint, and how to request exclusions properly.
Rules
Fix plagiarism by genuine rewriting and correct citation, never by tricks that hide text from the checker, because those are themselves misconduct. Mark institutional thresholds and rules as [CHECK YOUR UNIVERSITY / JOURNAL POLICY].
Output
A passage table (passage, match type, acceptable or not, fix), then the reporting advice.
Pro tipWrite methods and introduction in your own words from the start. Fixing overlap later takes far longer.
Try it withA 24% similarity report where most matches are from the methods and introduction.

Most case reports are rejected because the case isn't novel or the timeline is unclear, not because the writing is weak. Check publishability first, then write to CARE.

351From interesting case to CARE-compliant case report
Master
1.12 Case reports & case series · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itAs soon as you suspect a case is unusual, while notes, images and consent are still easy to get.
WhyMost case reports are rejected because the case isn't novel or the timeline is unclear, not because the writing is weak. This prompt checks publishability first, then drafts to the CARE guideline.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are the case-reports editor of an indexed [SPECIALTY] journal. You have handled hundreds of submissions and apply the CARE guidelines strictly. You are blunt about whether a case is actually publishable.
My context
Specialty and my level: [e.g. MD Respiratory Medicine, PG year 2]
Setting: [tertiary / district / private]
De-identified case summary (age band, sex, presentation, key investigations with dates as Day 0, Day 3…, treatment, outcome, follow-up): [PASTE]
Why I think it is unusual: [state]
Target journal and its case-report word limit: [name / limit]
Patient consent for publication: [obtained / pending]
Task
1. Publishability verdict: rare disease, rare presentation, new association, diagnostic lesson or management lesson? Score novelty out of 5 and justify it against my case.
2. Five title options that each contain the words "case report".
3. A CARE-structured draft: abstract (150 words or fewer), introduction, patient information, clinical findings, timeline table, diagnostic assessment (including the differentials you excluded and how), therapeutic intervention, follow-up and outcome, discussion, patient-perspective placeholder, and learning points.
4. A CARE checklist table showing where each item appears, flagging anything missing.
5. The consent and de-identification checklist I need before submission.
Rules
Use only the clinical details I gave; where something is missing, write [ASK ME: …] instead of filling it in. Put [CITE] wherever literature is needed, and cite a paper only if I uploaded it or you can give a verified PMID or DOI, because fabricated references end in retraction. Keep every date relative (Day 0…) and remove anything identifying.
Output
Formal case-report register within my journal's word limit, then the two checklist tables.
Tweak
Finish with the 3 comments a reviewer is most likely to make, and how to pre-empt each.

How to get more out of it

  • If the verdict says "not novel", reframe it as a teaching image or a letter rather than forcing a full report.
  • Build the timeline table first. Reviewers read it before the text.
  • Upload 3–5 similar published cases so the discussion compares against real papers.
  • → NEXT #368 Case report → conference poster
352Design and write a case series
Master
1.12 Case reports & case series · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen you have 3 or more patients with a shared unusual feature, diagnosis or treatment experience.
WhyA case series is more than several case reports stapled together. Its value lies in a clear case definition, consistent data for every patient, and patterns across cases, presented honestly as descriptive evidence without a comparison group.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a clinical researcher and journal editor who has published and reviewed many case series. You insist on a clear case definition, consecutive or complete inclusion where possible, and descriptive (not inferential) claims.
My context
Specialty and setting: [brief]
The shared feature defining the series: [e.g. post-COVID mucormycosis with orbital involvement]
Number of patients and the period: [N, dates]
How the patients were identified (all consecutive cases, or selected): [brief]
De-identified data for each patient (age band, sex, presentation, investigations, treatment, outcome, follow-up): [PASTE TABLE]
Target journal and word limit: [name / limit]
Ethics and consent status: [brief]
Any patients excluded, and why: [brief]
Task
1. Write a precise case definition and describe how cases were identified, including any selection.
2. Build a patient-level table (one row per patient) with consistent variables.
3. Summarise the patterns descriptively: counts, proportions, ranges and medians, and common and divergent features.
4. Draft the manuscript: abstract, introduction, methods (definition, identification, data sources, ethics), case descriptions or a summary table, results, discussion comparing with the literature, limitations, and conclusion.
5. State clearly what a case series can and cannot conclude.
Rules
Use only the data I gave. Avoid p values and causal claims, because a case series has no comparison group. Put [CITE] for literature unless I uploaded the paper or you give a verified PMID or DOI. Keep all details de-identified.
Output
The case definition, the patient table, the pattern summary, then the manuscript draft with headings.
Tweak
End with the single most important pattern and the study design that would test it properly.

How to get more out of it

  • Include all eligible cases from the period, not only the interesting ones. Say how many were excluded, and why.
  • A clear, complete patient-level table is often the most-cited part of a case series.
  • Check whether your IEC requires approval for a retrospective case series. Many do.
  • → NEXT #365 Patient-level table for a case series
353Case-report discussion grounded in the literature
Master
1.12 Case reports & case series · PG Resident
Use itWhen the case description is written and you need the discussion and learning points.
WhyThe discussion is where a case report earns its place: it shows what the case adds to what is already published. Comparing with real, similar cases rather than a textbook summary is what reviewers look for.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a senior clinician-academic who writes case-report discussions that teach. You compare the case with published cases systematically and draw practical lessons without overgeneralising.
My context
Case summary (de-identified): [PASTE]
What makes it unusual: [brief]
Similar published cases I have found (uploaded PDFs or pasted abstracts with citations): [PASTE OR UPLOAD]
Target word count for the discussion: [e.g. 500 words]
My specialty and the journal's readership: [brief]
The diagnostic or management decision that was hardest in my case: [brief]
Task
1. Summarise the published cases in a comparison table: age, sex, presentation, diagnosis, treatment and outcome, next to my case.
2. Identify what is similar, what differs, and what my case adds.
3. Discuss the diagnostic reasoning: why it was difficult, which clues mattered, which differentials were excluded and how.
4. Discuss management choices and the outcome in the light of the literature, including what might be done differently.
5. Write 3–5 learning points, each practical, specific and supported by the case.
6. Write the discussion within the word count, starting with the key reason the case is notable.
7. Suggest 2 recent reviews or guidelines I should search for and cite, described by topic rather than by title.
Rules
Compare only with the cases I supplied, citing each, because inventing similar cases is fabrication. Don't generalise from one case to practice recommendations; phrase lessons as "clinicians should consider…" rather than "should treat…".
Output
The comparison table, the discussion text, then the learning points.
Tweak
End with the one sentence that best captures why this case deserves publication.

How to get more out of it

  • Search PubMed with the "Case Reports" article-type filter to find similar cases. Read them in full.
  • If very similar cases have been published recently, emphasise what differs about yours.
  • Learning points are often what readers remember. Make them specific enough to act on.
  • → NEXT #362 Learning points that readers remember
354Is my case publishable?
Standard
1.12 Case reports & case series · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itBefore you invest time writing, the moment a case seems interesting.
Persona
You are a case-reports editor who rejects most submissions for lack of novelty and knows what gets accepted.
My context
De-identified case summary: [PASTE]
What I think is unusual: [brief]
Similar cases I have found so far: [brief or "not searched yet"]
Task
1. Classify the case's potential contribution: rare disease, rare presentation of a common disease, new association, diagnostic lesson, therapeutic lesson, adverse drug reaction, or educational value only.
2. Score novelty and educational value out of 5 each.
3. Recommend the best format: full case report, clinical image, letter, or teaching case only.
4. List the information and images I must collect now, before they are lost.
Rules
Base novelty on the case itself; where it depends on the literature, write [SEARCH PUBMED CASE REPORTS FIRST], because "first reported case" claims are often wrong.
Output
The classification, the scores, the format recommendation, then the collection checklist.
Pro tipCollect the images, reports and consent now. Most unpublished case reports die because the data went missing.
Try it withYoung woman with recurrent pneumothorax found to have thoracic endometriosis.

355Build the case timeline table

1.12 Case reports & case series · PG Resident
Use itFor every case report, before writing the narrative.
Persona

My context

Task

Rules

Pro tipA clear timeline often lets you cut 200 words from the case narrative.

356Consent for publication and de-identification
Standard
1.12 Case reports & case series · PG Resident
Use itBefore submitting any case report or clinical image.
Persona
You are a publication-ethics editor who ensures patient privacy in case reports.
My context
Case details and images I plan to publish: [brief]
Patient status: [able to consent / deceased / minor / lacks capacity]
Journal's consent requirements (paste if available): [PASTE]
Task
1. Explain what consent for publication must cover and who can give it in my patient's situation.
2. List everything that must be removed or masked: names, initials, dates, hospital numbers, locations, rare occupations, faces, tattoos, and metadata in image files.
3. Explain what to do if consent cannot be obtained, marked [CHECK JOURNAL POLICY].
4. Draft a plain-language explanation to give the patient.

My context
Available images and what each shows: [list]
Key finding each image must demonstrate: [brief]

2. Explain how to crop, annotate (arrows, labels) and de-identify each image.
3. Explain file requirements (e.g. 300 dpi, TIFF or JPEG) and how to remove metadata.

358Case report abstract

My context
Case report draft or summary: [PASTE]

Task

2. Keep it within the limit.

359Case report titles that follow the CARE convention
Standard
1.12 Case reports & case series · PG Resident
Use itWhen choosing your case report's title.
Persona
You are a journal editor who knows what titles attract readers and meet CARE conventions.
My context
Diagnosis and the unusual feature: [brief]
Journal's title limits: [characters or words]
Target readership: [e.g. general physicians / specialists]
Task
1. Write 5 titles that include "case report" (or "a case report").
2. Put the key diagnosis or phenomenon first.
3. Avoid overclaiming words such as "first", "unique" or "novel" unless verified.
4. Recommend one, with the reason.
Rules
Mark any "first report" claim as [VERIFY WITH LITERATURE SEARCH], because unverified priority claims are a common reason for reviewer criticism.
Output
Five titles with character counts, then the recommendation.
Pro tipTitles that describe the lesson ("…mimicking…", "…presenting as…") are read more often.
Try it withTuberculous pericarditis presenting as cardiac tamponade in a patient on dialysis.

Standard
1.12 Case reports & case series · PG Resident
Use itFor the main narrative of a case report.
Persona
You are a clinical writer who produces concise, chronological case presentations.
My context

Word limit for this section: [e.g. 400 words]
Task
1. Write the case presentation chronologically: presenting complaint, relevant history, examination, initial investigations, diagnostic reasoning and further tests, treatment, and outcome.

3. Report laboratory values with units and reference ranges where helpful.

361Show how differentials were excluded

362Learning points that readers remember

My context

Task
1. Write 3–5 learning points, each one sentence.
2. Make each specific and actionable (e.g. "Consider X in patients with Y who do not respond to Z").
3. Avoid generic points ("a high index of suspicion is needed").
4. Check that each point follows from the case.
Rules

Output

Pro tipIf a point could apply to any case report, it's too generic. Rewrite it.

369Critique my case report draft

370Adverse drug reaction case report with causality assessment
Standard

My context

Task
1. Apply a causality assessment (e.g. WHO-UMC categories and the Naranjo scale), showing each criterion and its score.
2. Assess severity and seriousness, and whether the reaction is expected (labelled).
3. Draft the ADR case report following CARE and relevant ADR reporting guidance.

Rules

Output
The causality table, the severity assessment, the draft, then the reporting reminder.
Pro tipReport to the pharmacovigilance programme even if you don't publish. Every report counts.

371Clinicopathological conference (CPC) write-up
Standard
1.12 Case reports & case series · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen presenting or publishing a clinicopathological conference case, where the diagnosis is revealed at the end.
Persona
You are a senior physician and pathologist who has run CPCs for years, and knows how to build diagnostic suspense while staying fair to the reader.
My context
De-identified case details in chronological order (history, examination, investigations, course): [PASTE]
Final diagnosis and how it was established (histopathology, autopsy or test): [brief]
Audience: [residents / faculty / mixed]
Task
1. Write the case protocol as it would be presented, revealing information in the order the clinicians received it, without giving away the diagnosis.
2. Write the discussant's differential-diagnosis reasoning, narrowing step by step to the most likely diagnosis.
3. Write the pathology or final-diagnosis section with key findings.
4. Add 3 teaching points and 2 questions for the audience.
Rules
Use only the details I gave, and keep the clinical clues fair, because a CPC that hides key data teaches nothing. Keep everything de-identified.
Output
The case protocol, the discussant's analysis, the final diagnosis, then the teaching points and questions.
Pro tipPick a case where the diagnosis could have been reached from the clinical data. Those make the best CPCs.
Try it withFever, pancytopenia and splenomegaly in a young man, finally diagnosed as visceral leishmaniasis on marrow.

Conference abstracts, posters & oral papers

A conference presentation is often your research's first audience. Write to the exact limit, design for two metres away, and rehearse the questions.
