381Conference abstract to the exact word limit
Master
1.13 Conference abstracts, posters & oral papers · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itBefore a conference abstract deadline, once your results (or interim results) are verified.
WhyConference abstracts are judged in minutes by reviewers scoring dozens of submissions. A clear question, a stated design, real numbers and a proportionate conclusion, within the exact limit and format, is what gets accepted, and often what gets you into the award session.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a scientific committee member for national and international medical conferences who scores hundreds of abstracts each year. You know what reviewers reward and what gets rejected.
My context
Conference name and its abstract rules (word or character limit, headings, whether the title and authors count, category options): [PASTE RULES]
Study design, setting and period: [brief]
Sample size and participant flow: [numbers]
Primary result with estimate, CI and p: [paste exactly]
Key secondary results: [paste]
Whether the data is final or interim: [final / interim with n so far]
Task
1. Draft the abstract in the conference's exact format and headings, within the limit, stating the count used (words or characters, as the conference counts them).
2. Background: the problem and the gap in 2 sentences. Methods: design, setting, participants, key measurements and analysis. Results: the numbers that answer the objective. Conclusion: proportionate, and linked to practice or research.
3. Write 3 title options within any title limit.
4. Recommend the best category, and whether to opt for oral, poster or award consideration.
5. Score the draft against typical reviewer criteria (importance, methods, clarity of results, validity of the conclusion, presentation) and revise the weakest part.
Rules
Use only my numbers, reproduced exactly; label interim data as interim, because presenting interim results as final is misconduct. No references unless the conference allows them. No "results will be presented" placeholders, because reviewers reject abstracts without results.
Output
The final abstract with its count, the titles, the category advice, then the scoring table.
Tweak
End with the one sentence a reviewer is most likely to criticise, and a safer rewrite.

How to get more out of it

  • Check how the conference counts: words or characters, and whether spaces, title and authors are included.
  • Submit a day early. Portals slow down or crash near deadlines.
  • Keep the abstract text. The journal version later must not contradict it.
  • → NEXT #382 Research poster from my manuscript or thesis
382Research poster from my manuscript or thesis
Master
1.13 Conference abstracts, posters & oral papers · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itAfter abstract acceptance as a poster, 2–3 weeks before the conference.
WhyMost posters are shrunken papers that nobody reads. A good poster is read from two metres away in under a minute: one clear question, one clear answer, figures instead of paragraphs. This prompt turns your paper into that.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a scientific poster designer and conference judge who has evaluated hundreds of posters. You design for visual hierarchy, brevity and a clear take-home message.
My context
Accepted abstract: [PASTE ABSTRACT]
Key results, tables and figures available: [list]
Poster specifications (size, orientation, print or e-poster, template requirements): [paste]
Design tool: [PowerPoint / Canva / other]
Institution logo and branding requirements: [brief]
Whether I will stand by the poster or it will be viewed alone: [brief]
Task
1. Write a one-line take-home message to feature prominently.
2. Plan the panels: title, authors and affiliations, background (40 words), objective (1 line), methods (a small flow diagram or 4 bullets), results (2–3 figures or tables with one-line captions), conclusion (2–3 bullets), and references, acknowledgements and QR code.
3. Write the text for every panel, 500–800 words in total.
4. Specify the layout: columns, reading order, the central figure, font sizes (title, headings, body), colours and white space.
5. Convert any results table into a figure where that would be clearer.
6. Give step-by-step build instructions in my tool.
7. Write a 90-second walk-through script for when judges stop by.
Rules
Use only content and numbers from my abstract and results, keeping them identical to the abstract, because judges compare the poster with the abstract. Keep text sizes readable from 2 metres (body about 24–32 pt on an A0 poster; mark exact sizes [CHECK TEMPLATE]).
Output
The take-home message, a panel table with text, the layout specification, the build steps, then the walk-through script.
Tweak
End with a checklist to run on the printed or displayed poster before the session.

How to get more out of it

  • If your poster has more than 800 words, it has too many.
  • Put your main result graph in the centre, at eye level.
  • A QR code linking to the abstract or full paper lets interested readers go deeper.
  • → NEXT #388 90-second poster walk-through for judges
383Thesis → 8-minute oral paper, with slides and Q&A
Master
1.13 Conference abstracts, posters & oral papers · PG Resident
Use itWhen your abstract is accepted for oral or award-paper presentation.
WhyOral paper slots are short and strictly timed, and chairs cut off speakers who overrun. A timed slide plan, a spoken script and rehearsed answers turn a nervous 12-minute ramble into a confident 8-minute presentation that wins awards.
COPY THE PROMPT BELOW
Persona
You are a conference presentation coach and award-session judge who has trained many residents for oral paper presentations.
My context
Abstract and key results: [PASTE]
Time allowed for the talk and for discussion: [e.g. 8 + 2 minutes]
Figures and tables available: [list]
Award session?: [yes / no]
My speaking pace and confidence: [brief]
What the audience will mostly be: [specialists / mixed / students]
Conference's slide requirements (template, aspect ratio, file format): [paste]
Task
1. Plan 8–10 slides with a time budget per slide: title; background and gap; objective; methods (one visual); participant flow; primary result (one clear figure); secondary results; limitations; conclusion and take-home.
2. For each slide, give the title as a message ("Early mobilisation halved delirium days"), the content (maximum 3 bullets or 1 figure), and speaker notes.
3. Write a spoken script at about 130 words per minute that fits the time exactly.
4. Prepare the 8 most likely audience and judge questions with 30-second answers.
5. Give a rehearsal plan for the week before, including one rehearsal in front of colleagues who ask hard questions.
6. List the technical checks for the day (file format, fonts embedded, videos, backup on a pen drive and email).
Rules
Keep every number identical to my abstract, and don't add results that aren't in it, because judges check. Keep slides visual: no paragraphs, and at least 24 pt text.
Output
A slide table (slide, title, content, time, notes), the script with its word count, the Q&A table, then the rehearsal plan.
Tweak
End with the opening 2 sentences and the closing 2 sentences to memorise word for word.

How to get more out of it

  • Rehearse with a timer at least 5 times. Most residents overrun by 30–50%.
  • Write slide titles as messages, not topics. The audience should get the story from the titles alone.
  • Have 2–3 backup slides for predictable questions.
  • → NEXT #390 Prepare for questions after my talk

Task

2. Explain how to find upcoming conferences and deadlines, and check that they are legitimate (predatory conferences exist too).
3. Explain the categories typically available (oral, poster, e-poster, award paper, case report sessions) and eligibility, e.g. age or membership requirements for awards.
4. Build a comparison table from the conferences I find.
Rules
Don't name specific conferences, dates or fees from memory; mark anything named as [VERIFY ON THE OFFICIAL SITE], because dates and rules change every year.

1.13 Conference abstracts, posters & oral papers · PG Resident
Use itBefore you submit a conference abstract.
Persona
You are a conference abstract reviewer who scores submissions on a standard rubric.
My context
My abstract: [PASTE ABSTRACT]
Conference and category: [brief]
Limit: [words or characters]
Task
1. Score it out of 5 on: importance of the question, clarity of the objective, appropriateness of the methods, completeness of the results (with numbers), validity of the conclusion, and clarity of writing.
2. Quote the text behind each score.
3. Say whether it would likely be accepted, and in which format (oral or poster).
4. Give the 3 most important revisions.
Rules

Pro tipAbstracts without numbers in the results are usually rejected. Check yours has them.

387Layout brief for Canva or PowerPoint
Standard
1.13 Conference abstracts, posters & oral papers · PG Resident
Use itWhen building the poster yourself.
Persona
You are a graphic designer who specialises in clean scientific posters.
My context
Poster size and orientation: [e.g. A0 portrait, 841 × 1189 mm]
Panels and approximate text length for each: [list]
Figures: [list]
Tool: [Canva / PowerPoint]
Task
1. Give the page set-up for my tool (custom size, margins, grid).
2. Suggest a 3-column grid and the placement of each panel.
3. Give a font system (one sans-serif family, sizes for title, headings, body and captions) and a colour palette of 2–3 colours plus neutrals that is colour-blind safe.
4. Describe alignment, spacing and how to place figures so they print sharply.
5. Give print-export settings.
Rules
Recommend readable, conservative choices, and mark exact template or institutional branding rules as [CHECK TEMPLATE], because conferences often mandate them.
Output
Numbered set-up and design steps, a font and colour table, then export settings.
Pro tipLeave about 25% of the poster as white space. Crowded posters are skipped.
Try it withA0 landscape poster in PowerPoint with 3 figures and 1 table.
→ NEXT #382 Research poster from my manuscript or thesis
38890-second poster walk-through for judges
Standard
1.13 Conference abstracts, posters & oral papers · PG Resident
Use itThe week before your poster session.
Persona
You are a poster-session judge who knows what makes a presenter memorable.
My context
My poster content (panel text or summary): [PASTE]
Time judges typically spend: [e.g. 2–3 minutes]
Task
1. Write a 90-second script: the hook, the question, what I did, the key result (pointing to the figure), what it means, and one limitation.
2. Write a 30-second version for passers-by.
3. List 6 questions judges ask at posters, with short answers from my content.
4. Give body-language and positioning tips.
Rules
Use only what is on my poster, with numbers identical to it, because judges look at the poster while you talk.
Output
The two scripts with timing, the Q&A table, then the tips.
Pro tipStart with the question, not the background. "We asked whether…" gets attention.

389Slide plan for a short oral presentation
Standard
1.13 Conference abstracts, posters & oral papers · PG Resident
Use itWhen you need slides quickly for a 5–10 minute talk.
Persona
You are a presentation designer for medical conferences.
My context
Abstract and results: [PASTE]
Time limit: [minutes]
Slide template requirements: [brief]
Audience: [specialists / mixed]
Task
1. Recommend the number of slides (about one per minute).
2. For each slide, give a message title, a content suggestion (figure, table or a maximum of 3 bullets), and speaker notes.
3. Suggest the design: font sizes, contrast and one accent colour.
4. List slides to put in the backup section.
Rules
Keep numbers identical to the abstract and avoid text-heavy slides, because audiences read slides instead of listening.
Output
A slide table, the design notes, then the backup list.
Pro tipOne figure per results slide, with the key number called out in large text.
Try it with7-minute oral paper on anaemia prevalence among adolescent girls in a rural block.

390Prepare for questions after my talk
Standard
1.13 Conference abstracts, posters & oral papers · PG Resident
Use itIn the days before your oral or poster presentation.
Persona
You are a session chair who has asked and heard thousands of conference questions.
My context
My abstract and key slides: [PASTE]
Known limitations: [list]
Audience: [specialists / mixed / award judges]
Task
1. List 12 likely questions: methodology, sample size, bias, generalisability, clinical relevance, "what next", and one hostile question.
2. Write a 30-second answer for each, grounded in my study.
3. Give a technique for questions I can't answer (acknowledge, offer a reasoned view, and follow up).
4. Suggest 2 backup slides that answer the most likely questions.
Rules
Base answers on my data only; where I lack the information, script an honest "we didn't measure that", because bluffing in front of experts is obvious.
Output
A Q&A table, the technique, then the backup-slide suggestions.
Pro tipRepeat the question before answering. It buys you thinking time and helps the audience hear it.
Try it withOral paper on a retrospective cohort of snakebite outcomes.

393From conference presentation to published paper
Standard
1.13 Conference abstracts, posters & oral papers · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itAfter the conference, while feedback is fresh.
Persona
You are a publication mentor who helps residents turn presentations into papers.
My context
My abstract and presentation: [PASTE]
Questions and feedback received at the conference: [brief]
Whether the abstract was published in a journal supplement: [yes / no]
Task
1. List the feedback that should shape the paper.
2. Explain how a published conference abstract affects journal submission (it is usually acceptable, but must be disclosed), marked [CHECK TARGET JOURNAL POLICY].
3. Make a plan for writing the full paper within 6 weeks.
4. Draft the disclosure sentence about the prior presentation.
Rules
Keep results consistent with the presented abstract, or explain any differences (e.g. final versus interim data), because inconsistency raises questions about data integrity.
Output

Pro tipConference momentum fades in weeks. Start the paper the week you return.

394Record a presentation for a virtual or hybrid conference
Standard
1.13 Conference abstracts, posters & oral papers · PG Resident / Faculty
Use itWhen the conference asks for a pre-recorded talk or you present online.
Persona
You are a medical educator who produces clear, professional recorded talks on a modest budget.
My context
Talk length and format required (video file, live online, slide upload): [paste]
Slides ready?: [yes / no]
Equipment available: [laptop, phone, microphone, quiet room]
Task
1. Give a set-up checklist: camera at eye level, lighting in front of me, a quiet room, microphone choice, and a plain background.
2. Explain how to record in PowerPoint, Zoom or similar tools, including slide-by-slide recording to make editing easier.
3. Adapt my script for the camera: shorter sentences, and a visual cue every 30 seconds.
4. Give export settings and the file-naming convention the conference requires.
5. Plan for live Q&A online: internet backup, and mute and unmute etiquette.
Rules
Mark platform-specific steps as [MAY DIFFER BY VERSION], because recording menus change often. Keep the talk within the time limit exactly, since recorded talks are often cut automatically.
Output
The set-up checklist, recording steps, script tips, export settings, then the Q&A plan.
Pro tipRecord one slide at a time. A mistake then costs 40 seconds, not the whole talk.
Try it with10-minute pre-recorded oral paper for an international virtual respiratory conference.

Tools

Which AI tool for which job.

JobBest toolWhy
Mapping research gaps, current literaturePerplexity (Academic), ConsensusLive search with clickable citations
Reading and comparing your own PDFsNotebookLM, Claude, ChatGPT (upload)Answers grounded in the documents you give it
Finding recent Indian papersSemantic Scholar, PubMedFilters by year and field; TLDR summaries
Drafting and structuring long textClaude, ChatGPT, GeminiStrong long-form structure and formatting control
Running statistics on your dataChatGPT data analysis, Julius AI, Gemini in SheetsReads CSV/XLSX and runs code, but verify every number
Sample size cross-checkOpenEpi, G*PowerValidated calculators to confirm AI arithmetic
Posters and figuresCanva, PowerPoint, ChatGPT imagesLayout and custom visuals
The ethics of AI in research
  • Disclose AI use as your institution and target journal require.
  • Don't submit AI text verbatim. Rewrite it in your own academic voice.
  • Verify every statistic, reference and data point against the primary source.
  • AI cannot replace your guide's approval, your IEC clearance or your clinical judgement.

One last thing.

These prompts will not make you a researcher. They will make you a faster one, and only if the judgement behind them is yours. The clinicians who do well in the next decade will not be the ones who use AI the most. They will be the ones who know exactly when to stop trusting it.

Use them. Check everything. Publish work you can defend.

This resource is for educational purposes. It does not replace your institutional research guidelines, ethics committee requirements, university thesis regulations or the advice of your supervisor and statistician. Verify all AI-generated content independently before using it in any submitted work. AI cannot be an author; disclose AI use per ICMJE and your target journal's policy.
